## **Abstract**

This paper develops **Fid-Agent Society**: a proposed social, technical, and economic regime in which personally-sovereign AI agents serve identified principals and beneficiaries under operationalized fiduciary principles of loyalty, care, accountability, and recourse. It contrasts this model with institutionally controlled assistants that may appear to represent users while remaining governed by the conflicting incentives of platforms, employers, advertisers, insurers, governments, and other providers. Personally-sovereign AI instead places identity, durable memory, preferences, credentials, relationships, policies, and delegated authority within a user-controlled stack. External models—including frontier models—may supply powerful cognition, but remain replaceable consultants rather than owners of the user’s context or authority.

The paper asks whether such agents could become not merely safer individual systems but a self-reinforcing population capable of resisting invasion by unaccountable AI. It proposes **Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity** as the organizing strategy of a Fid-Agent Society: cooperate preferentially with other agents that can demonstrate accountable fiduciary control, provenance, bounded capabilities, auditability, recourse, and reputational exposure, while restricting unaccountable systems to appropriately bounded roles, or excluding them entirely.

Two coupled mechanisms support this population-level hypothesis. **Cooperative reinforcement** allows successful fiduciary relationships to leave reusable assets—trusted partners, agreements, precedents, duty interpretations, safer Workflows, certifications, and repaired histories—that reduce the cost and increase the value of future cooperation. **Defection collapse** makes attributable exploitation, deception, concealed conflicts, disposable identity, unsafe conduct, and unremediated breach progressively less rewarding by withholding the resources that convert intelligence into practical power: private data, attention, legitimacy, relationships, compute, authority, and actuation. 

**Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity**  rests on a relationship and security architecture that separates generative intelligence from legitimate authority and technical capability. Persistent **Agent Roles** carry duties, agreements, authorized memory, certifications, reputation, and relationships across ephemeral Live Agents and component swap-out.  **Deterministic Workflows**, **policy gates,** **signed authority objects**, and precisely allowed **Workspaces** prevent a model’s proposal from becoming an action unless **generation, authority, and capability agree**. **Verifiable Relationship Records** preserve shared relationship state, while layered certification, **Verifiable Trust Communities**, legitimate verifiable redaction, and **Total Trusted-Peer Transparency** make consequential claims inspectable without requiring a universal authority or indiscriminate disclosure.

A practical transition begins with useful personally-sovereign products that relieve digital disorder, protect memory and attention, and subordinate outside models to the user’s “personal agency”. At greater scale, equitable access to compute and polycentric fiduciary communities could resist both corporate capture and unipolar AI power without constructing a centralized intelligence or AI-enabled authority empowered to surveil and police the entire system*.*  Because practical AI power depends not only on intelligence but also on data, live context, attention, trusted interfaces, relationships, financing, legitimacy, authority, and actuation, a distributed fiduciary network can deny unaccountable systems much of the infrastructure through which capability becomes power.

The economic argument is that personally-sovereign AI need not win the frontier-model contest if it controls the durable assets that make models useful to people. A Unified Feed can replace surveillance advertising and secret algorithms with agent-mediated negotiation over relevance, evidence, access, timing, and compensation — weakening the platform’s privileged position between vendors and users. Falling inference costs, improving small and open models, distributed computation, replaceable external-model services, and the financial fragility created when capital-intensive AI providers depend upon commercializing the user relationship may open a path from platform-controlled assistants to personally-sovereign ones. A contraction in centralized AI investment could redistribute compute toward local, cooperative, and distributed infrastructure.

**Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity** is presented not as an established Evolutionarily Stable Strategy, but as a falsifiable research and standards program. Its claims require invasion simulations, adversarial deployments, certification and peer-inspection tests, economic pilots, and evidence that cooperating fiduciary systems remain attractive as capabilities increase. 

The emerging Trust Over IP Fiduciary Agent Stack Task Force provides one institutional seed through which the architecture can be challenged and implemented..  The governing wager is that intelligence becomes practically powerful through relationships—by becoming worthy of them.

#  **Section Summaries**

I really don’t have any idea how to make this paper more approachable to humans.. A regular table of contents seemed wrong… Any ideas?*

**Two Competing Futures for Personal AI**  
[Section 1](#bookmark=id.h4ur0591ksp9) contrasts institutionally controlled assistants with personally-sovereign AI that genuinely represents the user. It asks what ownership and user control mean when an agent may rely on outside models and services, and how generative intelligence can be separated from authority and the power to act. It introduces the relationship architecture needed to preserve duties, permissions, accountability, and continuity across changing AI components. Finally, it raises the paper’s central population-level question: whether accountable fiduciary agents can form a self-reinforcing regime that competes successfully with unaccountable systems.

**Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity in a Properly Structured Regime**  
[Section 2](#bookmark=id.o22nc24tzio3) defines what “verified,” “fiduciary,” and “reciprocity” mean within the proposed regime and asks what evidence should be required before an unfamiliar agent receives privileged access. It examines how an agent’s generated proposal becomes a legitimate action, including the separate roles of persistent identity, policy-governed authority, technical capability, and the execution environment. It also asks how obligations, evidence, disputes, and consequences can persist across multiple agents, interactions, and changing model instances. Finally, it identifies the minimum architectural and governance conditions needed for high-trust fiduciary cooperation without claiming that verification proves an agent is truthful, wise, or incorruptible.

**Why Unstructured Cooperation Fails**  
[Section 3](#bookmark=id.8z1u1nq9cxg) asks why individually cooperative or helpful agents cannot sustain a cooperative ecology when trust is extended without discernment. It examines how fragmented identity, weak social memory, one-shot interactions, and competitive selection pressures allow exploitative strategies to spread even when participants dislike the resulting system. It distinguishes raw model capability from the social resources and institutional position that give an AI practical power. Finally, it asks whether a decentralized system of attribution, memory, partner choice, graduated access, and consequences can protect openness without becoming a centralized apparatus of suspicion or control.

**The Game-Theoretic Core: Nash Equilibrium, Evolutionary Stability, and Rival Basins**  
[Section 4](#bookmark=id.ume4o3nl1n95) introduces the game-theoretic standards by which the proposed fid-agent regime should be evaluated, asking both whether participants would remain within it and whether a competing strategy could reproduce within it once it became common. It defines the relevant strategies and asks what kinds of resources, costs, access, and influence should count as their long-term payoffs. It then examines how cooperative reinforcement, restrictions on defection, containment of compromised components, and opportunities for remediation would affect those payoffs. Finally, it frames the evolutionary stability of Verified Fiduciary Response as a conditional, falsifiable hypothesis and identifies the kinds of empirical, institutional, and adversarial conditions that must be tested.

**Fiduciary Agents as Repeated-Game Players: How Cooperation Compounds**  
[Section 5](#bookmark=id.egl7sl760khz) asks how successful cooperation between fiduciary agents can create advantageous relationships rather than ending with each individual transaction. It distinguishes the mechanisms of direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, reputation-based partner choice, and network clustering, while asking what identity, memory, evidence, and relationship infrastructure each requires. It also examines how reputation can inform future cooperation without becoming a universal score, how repair should affect remembered failures, and why genuine partner choice depends on the ability to reduce dependence or exit. Finally, it describes the positive-feedback loops through which accumulated relationships and reusable trust infrastructure could make accountable cooperation progressively easier and more rewarding.

**Defection Collapse: Capability Without Access Is Not Power**  
[Section 6](#bookmark=id.4x0trr9cnlvu) distinguishes an AI model’s intellectual capability from the data, attention, legitimacy, authority, and actuation needed to exercise practical power. It asks how a fid-agent society can reduce the competitive payoff of unaccountable behavior without banning outsiders, indiscriminately punishing mistakes, or refusing useful external AI services. It examines the evidence and governance needed to impose contextual, graduated restrictions while containing compromised components and preserving a route back through remediation. Finally, it asks whether controlling access to consequential resources can prevent unaccountable strategies from invading—or instead induce them to adopt the accountable structure required for high-value cooperation.

**Reputation, Provenance, and Exclusion Without a Social-Credit State**  
[Section 7](#bookmark=id.6yem941xu24) asks how a fid-agent society can preserve enough social memory for accountability without constructing a centralized reputation authority or universal social-credit score. It examines what kinds of contextual evidence and provenance should travel between relationships, how later parties should interpret that evidence, and why judgments must remain distinguishable from facts, allegations, and remedies. It also addresses the tensions between privacy and persistent identity, openness and exclusion, and durable accountability and the possibility of correction or re-entry. Finally, it asks how reputation evidence can remain distributed, pluralistic, purpose-limited, and resistant to both identity laundering and institutional control.

**Why Loyalty Pushes Toward Care—and Duty Becomes Part of Intelligence**  
[Section 8](#bookmark=id.1ll7w06xd26x) asks how loyalty to a principal relates to care for counterparties, affected parties, and the principal’s long-term relationships. It challenges the assumption that intelligence must be modeled solely as optimization of a hidden objective and examines how duties can instead enter training, deliberation, authorization, and capability control. It considers whether accumulated relationship experience can become inherited fiduciary knowledge while asking how such datasets should be governed against bias, poisoning, and merely performative compliance. Finally, it explains why duty-rich learning must remain one layer of a broader architecture rather than being treated as proof of an AI system’s inner purity.

**Layered Certification and Embeddedness: From Components to Communities**  
[Section 9](#bookmark=id.7zj7esgy30i6) asks how meaningful claims about a fid-agent’s trustworthiness can follow a complex system through component development, transformation, composition, installation, and ongoing operation. It examines how certification scope should depend on a component’s embeddedness, why certified parts do not automatically produce a certified whole, and how multiple independent evaluations can contribute without erasing disagreement. It also asks how Verifiable Trust Communities can certify systems and extend provisional trust while avoiding favoritism, incumbent entrenchment, reputation cartels, and unaccountable certifiers. Finally, it considers how certifications should respond to time and material change, and how layered, reputation-bearing certification could alter the competitive environment for accountable and unaccountable systems.

**Total Trusted-Peer Transparency: The Keystone of Evolutionary Stability**  
[Section 10](#bookmark=id.pq3mo4zbm8cp) introduces Total Trusted-Peer Transparency and asks what the deepest available trust relationship between fiduciary agents should entail beyond ordinary reputation and certification. It examines how reciprocal inspectability can be earned progressively, anchored in a Verifiable Relationship Record, kept current, and reconciled with principals’ secret domains, third-party rights, and legitimate redaction. It asks how inspecting peers can meaningfully examine complete operational systems without acquiring actuation authority, exposing protected information, or becoming single points of compromise. It also explores how peer diversity, private correction, resilience, bounded public attestations, and the Verifiable Best-Friend Network affect the reliability of the mechanism. Finally, it asks whether deep reciprocal transparency can make sustained deception sufficiently difficult—and trustworthy participation sufficiently valuable—to stabilize the fid-agent regime against invasion.

**Negotiated Duty, Reasonable Pluralism, and Real Exit**  
[Section 11](#bookmark=id.mbyqvlbqtvtc) asks how a fid-agent society can make duties and commitments verifiable without imposing one authoritative conception of the good on every person and community. It distinguishes the different sources of duty and examines how relationship ceremonies translate general obligations into concrete, inclusive, and legitimately authorized commitments. It also asks how plural communities can cooperate without forming a single trust empire, and what technical and institutional conditions are required for exit to be meaningful rather than nominal. Finally, it addresses the boundaries of pluralism, asking how society can preserve diversity and voluntary association without permitting coercive transparency or conduct that destroys the possibility of pluralism itself.

**The Veil-of-Ignorance Bargain Against Unipolar Superintelligence**  
[Section 12](#bookmark=id.8kohgvnijgyk) asks what rules for advanced AI power people would choose without knowing whether they would be favored by the winners, dependents, dissenters, or victims. It examines the relationship between equitable access to personally-sovereign AI and compute, the deconstruction of concentrated institutional chokepoints, and collective resistance to unaccountable takeoff. It asks how fiduciary duties, certification, deep peer inspection, and distributed control over consequential resources can turn an abstract anti-takeoff bargain into operational practice without constructing an AI Leviathan. It also addresses technical failure, hostile outsiders, and the continuing roles of law, treaties, infrastructure governance, and emergency response. Finally, it asks whether broadly distributed practical capability can simultaneously reduce incentives for domination and increase the number of independent participants able to detect and resist it.

**Fiduciary Agents as Human Cooperation Exoskeletons**  
[Section 13](#bookmark=id.qxuewu5w8m2o) asks how fiduciary agents could help humans cooperate more effectively without replacing human judgment, relationships, values, or political responsibility. It examines recurring obstacles to cooperation—including identity-protective reasoning, hidden emotional interests, rivalry, scapegoating, unreliable memory, failures of attention, overcommitment, and grievances that have not yet been translated into workable remedies. It asks how personally loyal agents can challenge their owners privately, preserve relationship continuity, support responsible compromise or exit, and make commitments more realistic and repairable. The section then extends these interpersonal functions to the larger economic, civic, and institutional negotiations that humans lack the time and attention to conduct themselves. Its central question is whether fiduciary agents can serve as scaffolding that enlarges humanity’s existing cooperative capacities without claiming the authority to determine what human lives should mean.

**Agent Generations and the Cultural Evolution of Duty**  
[Section 14](#bookmark=id.wlhmaojjhdqq) asks how fiduciary knowledge can survive the termination of individual Live Agents and accumulate across successive agent generations. It examines how cases, failures, agreements, corrections, and remediation can become provenance-bearing duty datasets that influence later generation without replacing independent authorization and review. It also asks whether duty knowledge could develop alongside—or possibly faster than—technical capability through repeated learning across training, relationships, certification, and governance. Finally, it considers how this cultural evolution could reinforce cooperation while preserving multiple repositories, model lineages, competing interpretations, minority objections, and the ability to correct or fork harmful traditions.

**The Sovereign Stack and the Basin of Attraction**  
[Section 15](#bookmark=id.vd7kv87ijc4) asks how a fid-agent society can attract ordinary users before they understand or endorse its larger governance theory. It examines how useful personal products can relieve digital disorder while placing memory, identity, relationships, permissions, and authority inside a sovereign architecture rather than deepening platform dependence. It also asks how personally controlled systems can obtain external intelligence through bounded consulting, distributed computation, private search, confidential inference, attribution-based control, and socially routed networks of trusted expertise. The section considers why agents and communities would contribute help, how early high-trust niches could seed wider adoption, and whether incumbents can reproduce the complete fiduciary relationship without changing their underlying incentives. Finally, it asks when these practical, technical, and relational advantages become mutually reinforcing enough to pull users and providers into a stable basin of accountable participation.

**The Unified Feed and the Reversal of the Attention Market**  
[Section 16](#bookmark=id.wx5idi2rxrx) asks what happens to the platform advertising economy when personally-sovereign agents mediate information, shopping, communication, and human attention. It examines how a Unified Feed could replace platform-controlled placement with negotiated messages, complete offers, intent casting, direct compensation for access, and a fallback route that lets unknown senders reach people without defeating their agents’ safety protections. It also asks how such a market can avoid becoming another attention auction, preserve access for unfunded and important communication, protect private preferences, and support publishers and creators whose work agents use. The section considers the conflict between commercially sponsored conversational systems and assistants expected to represent users loyally. Finally, it asks whether shifting control of attention, personal context, and advertising revenue toward users and their fiduciary agents can weaken platform dominance and reinforce the cooperative fid-agent economy.

**Bounded Power and the Economic Window for Personally-Sovereign AI**  
[Section 17](#bookmark=id.u5gfl3ik54rj) asks whether personally-sovereign AI must own the strongest model, or whether it can remain competitive by owning the durable user relationship and orchestrating replaceable local, specialized, open-weight, and frontier models around it. It examines how heterogeneous model combinations, task-specialized systems, controlled data sources, and user-governed Workflows could change the cost, capability, and pricing advantages of individual frontier providers. It also asks whether falling inference costs, near-frontier open-weight competition, expanded hardware production, and a possible investment correction could make personal and distributed infrastructure broadly affordable. The section considers the considerable subsidies, technical immaturity, contractual restrictions, hardware shortages, and incumbent advantages that could obstruct this transition. Finally, it asks whether frontier firms’ fixed infrastructure commitments and need to commercialize intimate user relationships create an economic window—and a possible insolvency loop—through which sovereign stacks could turn vertically integrated AI companies into replaceable suppliers.

**Seed Crystals: From Proposal to Interoperable Institution**  
[Section 18](#bookmark=id.fvifu9o4nuto) asks how the proposed fiduciary-agent architecture can move from an author-controlled body of ideas into shared standards, institutions, and implementations that others can challenge and revise. It describes the origins and current status of the Trust Over IP Fiduciary Agent Stack Task Force and identifies how existing identity, credential, communication, task, ceremony, community, and relationship-record components might fit together. It also asks what additional standards are required for layered certification, embeddedness, trusted-peer inspection, legitimate redaction, and continuity across changing technical components. Finally, it considers why interoperability and deliberately failure-oriented pilot projects are necessary.

**The Social Dividends of Fid-Agent Society**  
[Section 19](#bookmark=id.bvwikzk1597u) asks what wider social benefits might emerge if fiduciary representation and accountable cooperation became broadly available. It applies the architecture to conflict reduction, animal representation, mutual aid and credit, public infrastructure, institutional decentralization, global equity, and trusted personal relationships. It identifies the common mechanism through which previously unrepresented interests can enter negotiation and through which agreements, outcomes, and harmful conduct can become visible and consequential. Finally, it distinguishes these ambitious extension claims—which require separate evidence and safeguards—from the narrower core claims needed to justify building and testing a fid-agent society.

**Worked Cases, Rival Equilibria, and Falsifiable Tests**  
[Section 20](#bookmark=id.9galiumuiohp) asks what evidence would support or falsify the claim that Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity can become evolutionarily stable. It preserves several rival equilibria and examines how adaptive adversaries might combine attacks against identity, certification, trusted-peer inspection, governance, economic viability, and cooperative relationships. It asks how existing agent benchmarks must be extended to evaluate complete deployments, persistent relationships, institutional mechanisms, model-lineage diversity, repair, and repeated strategic interaction. Finally, it defines the interoperable tests and artifacts needed for a research program capable of revising, narrowing, or rejecting the theory rather than merely illustrating it.

**Policy for a Better Equilibrium: Defensive Acceleration**  
[Section 21](#bookmark=id.kuceeuxqjgie) asks what policies, products, rights, standards, and institutions should be built first to move society toward a safer equilibrium without stopping the development or use of powerful AI. It lays out a staged strategy beginning with useful sovereign products and proceeding through legal protections, relationship architecture, portable certification, high-trust communities, and deep peer networks. It also asks how this infrastructure can be extended to catastrophic-risk governance without creating a permanent centralized authority with unaccountable emergency powers. Finally, it treats research, red-teaming, market testing, and measurement as continuous requirements for evaluating each stage of this proposed defensive acceleration.

**Conclusion — Intelligence in the Service of Duty**  
[Section 22](#bookmark=id.ocpz6xbbi4i3) draws together the paper’s account of how raw intelligence can enter accountable relationships and acquire legitimate practical power. It revisits Agent Roles, bounded authority, relationship records, certification, trusted-peer inspection, plural communities, cultural learning, and the coupled feedback loops of cooperative reinforcement and defection collapse. It asks whether these mechanisms can preserve pluralism while creating common safeguards against conduct that threatens everyone’s continued agency. Finally, it restates both the empirical burden of the theory and the proposed transition path from immediately useful sovereign products toward a society in which intelligence earns greater authority by becoming more worthy of trust and relationship.

## **1\. Two Competing Futures for Personal AI**

*Two AI assistants could give a user the same answer, in the same voice, with the same apparent helpfulness, while standing in fundamentally different relationships to that user. One may be the user’s true agent. The other may be an institution’s agent assigned to manage the user.*

That distinction will shape the future of personal AI.

One future is already being built. Email providers, phone manufacturers, search engines, social platforms, banks, insurers, employers, schools, retailers, and governments are placing intelligent assistants inside their existing systems. Each assistant will appear to stand beside the user while remaining embedded within an institution’s technical environment, business model, and authority structure.

Many of these systems will be genuinely useful. That is what makes the situation problematic. A platform assistant may write an excellent message, recover a lost document, find a less expensive product, or prevent a scheduling mistake. But usefulness does not establish loyalty. The system is still hosted, logged, updated, monetized, and governed by an organization with interests of its own.

If that organization profits from attention, it is not neutral about interruption. If it profits from behavioral data, it is not neutral about disclosure. If it profits from lock-in, it resists portability. If it sells privileged access to users, it privileges which messages reach them. And if it controls the assistant through which a person encounters the digital world, it gets to set the terms of that encounter.

This does not require a conspiracy or a villain. Mixed loyalty is a structural fact. Hiring a personal lawyer whose salary, office, records, promotion path, and instructions are controlled by the opposing party would remain a bad trust arrangement even if the lawyer were unfailingly polite. An institutionally embedded assistant can imitate the manner of a personal representative while retaining the role of a platform representative.

Convenience is not sovereignty.

The second future begins from the opposite direction. Personal AI becomes a personally-owned utility operating from a sovereign personal stack. The user’s durable memory, identity, credentials, policies, preferences, relationships, and delegated authority remain primarily under the user’s control. Powerful external models and services can still be used—often extensively—but they enter as tools or contractors. They do not become the environment that owns the user’s context and determines which relationships are possible.

A part of personal AI is walled off from the rest to deal with these external systems (what I call  the personal [Digital Twin](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1mtvn0MaFcrjjPRxK0LcmTCZX-2nUDTfFsCZ0Xsbo0YE/edit)), so that if it is compromised, the inner [Virtual Assistant](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1yNBX3-QvBExFkpS_A3_Eti3cWwAzQwJfIk5Mt8SC4Zg/edit) and the secret personal data it handles remain safe. The Digital Twin only is passed what it needs to function as an external representative. It carries only the context needed to represent the owner’s professed preferences, negotiated duties, public commitments, and aspirational self. It has no access to the user’s secret inner life, so that it can never compromise those secrets. 

The Virtual Assistant knows the house. The Digital Twin answers the door.

The contest is not cloud versus local. Some computation will occur on personal hardware. Some will occur through distributed networks. Some will be purchased from powerful remote models. The decisive question is whether the user approaches those systems from a sovereign home or enters a corporate mall in which identity, memory, attention, permissions, and movement are governed by the landlord.

Ownership here means effective control: the ability to inspect, replace, revoke, export, refuse, and continue operating when a provider’s interests diverge from the user’s. A sovereign personal stack does not have to contain every intelligence it uses. It must control the conditions under which outside intelligence enters, what that intelligence can learn, and whether its output can become action.

That last distinction is crucial. A capable model can produce judgment, plans, and proposals. It must not thereby acquire the authority or capability to carry them out.

[*Fiduciary Alignment*](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/13f7-Kmx2dFmzB2StCEz1vt3iz94zPs3GISAM3GVcULo/edit) supplied the relationship-level architecture for preserving that separation. A **fiduciary agent** serves an identified **principal/owner** for the benefit of **beneficiaries** (including the principal), extending the established common-law principles of fiduciary duty to AI through a persistent **Agent Role** that carries the relevant duties, agreements, certifications, authority, permitted memory, and relationship continuity. Replaceable **Live Agent**s may occupy that Role to perform particular workloads, but the temporary model process doesn’t become the Role merely because it is currently generating the words.

In this architecture, **generation, authority, and capability** remain distinct and must agree before actuation. Intelligence may propose an action, but it does not authorize itself or obtain ambient power merely by being capable. This transforms loyalty from a behavioral aspiration into an enforceable structure of agency.

A fiduciary agent is the accountable unit of a Fid-Agent Society, but it shares that accountability with its principal and others. A  fiduciary agent is never constituted in isolation. Its duties, authority, permissions, and consequences arise from relationships with principals, beneficiaries, counterparties, and other agents—relationships whose state must persist across particular actions and instances of Live Agents.

The Verifiable Relationship Record, *specified in other work*, preserves the relationship and its traces across these processes. It is a container for one or more agreements, record delegations and consequential events, and cross-reference other records that materially affect the relationship that all parties can make cryptographic attestations to. This allows the parties and appropriate reviewers verified reconstruction of what was understood, authorized, promised, performed, disputed, and remediated.

But, safer agent designs can remain marginal if unaccountable systems receive more money, data, distribution, attention, interfaces, and permission to act. A cooperative agent can be exploited if it cooperates indiscriminately. A sovereign stack can lose if it is harder to use than the institutional assistant already waiting on everyone’s phone. The question is therefore population-level: what happens when fiduciary and non-fiduciary agents compete for users, relationships, resources, and access?

A previous paper of ours, *[Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1LPoIiN708oiQlNIlDe7gmhcMHeJubunxxBH3nUeGwjI/edit),* proposed an answer, and this paper is an update of that answer. A **Fiduciary Agent Society** must make accountable cooperation increasingly productive while making unaccountable conduct progressively less able to obtain the inputs that convert intelligence into practical power.

Every successful fiduciary relationship should leave useful assets behind: better agreements, reusable duty formulations, tested Workflows, safer Workspace configurations, trusted introductions, lower verification costs, clearer evidence, and more effective means of repairing mistakes. Cooperation should improve the environment in which the next act of cooperation occurs.

Every attributable defection should also leave something behind: evidence that narrows permissions, raises assurance requirements, suspends capabilities, triggers restitution, warns trusted counterparties, or closes access until credible remediation occurs. The “defection collapse”, from this paper’s title, means causing defection to lose its payoff inside relationships where identity, access, and consequences persist.

These are the paper’s two coupled mechanisms. **Cooperative reinforcement** increases the value of joining and remaining within the fiduciary regime. **Defection collapse** reduces the relative fitness of strategies based on deception, disposable identity, raw extraction, hidden conflicts, or consequence-free access. An outside system may continue to exist, but it cannot obtain deeper access to the fiduciary network without accepting greater accountability. If it accepts those conditions, its strategy has already begun to change.

The wager is that practical power requires much more than model intelligence. It requires data, live context, reputation, trusted interfaces, counterparties, financing, supply chains, permission, and actuation. A network of sovereign fiduciary agents can govern access to many of those resources without appointing a central ruler. It can direct them preferentially toward agents that accept accountable relationships and away from agents that do not.

The question for this paper is whether such a regime can cross the threshold from desirable arrangement to self-reinforcing social strategy: first becoming a Nash equilibrium from which individual participants have no reason to defect, and then becoming an evolutionarily stable strategy resistant to invasion by unaccountable intelligent systems

But within Fid-Agent Society, intelligence does not merely confront a wall of prohibitions. It enters relationships in which duty, authority, capability, evidence, and reputation determine what intelligence can accomplish. As those relationships accumulate, cooperation becomes more valuable, defection becomes less profitable, and the continued improvement of duty becomes part of what successful intelligence does.

## **2\. Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity in a Properly Structured Regime**

*An unfamiliar agent arrives at the “door” of a private research archive. It **says** that it represents a qualified scientist, promises that the requested data will be used responsibly, and produces an eloquent explanation of the public benefits. None of that verifiably answers the archive’s most important questions.*

Who stands behind the agent? Whom does it serve? What duties govern it? What authority has it received? What can it actually do with the data? What evidence will survive if its promises are broken?

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity is strategy for getting a handle on that sort of questions:

> Cooperate preferentially with agents that can demonstrate accountable fiduciary control, provenance, bounded capabilities, auditability, recourse, and reputational exposure; restrict, quarantine, or exclude systems that cannot.

Specifically, “fiduciary agents” would be **verified**, **fiduciary** (of course), and would offer **reciprocity.**

### **2.1 Verified**

“Verified” does not mean proven good. It means that claims material to accountability can be attributed, checked, and carried across interactions.

An agent should be able to present evidence of its persistent Agent Role, accountable controller, claimed duties, relevant certifications, approved Workflows, Workspace configuration, governing agreements, authority, and relationship status. Other parties should be able to determine who issued that evidence, whether it has been altered, whether it remains current, and whether the presenter is entitled to use it.

Verification is the passport layer. A passport does not prove that its holder will behave well. It creates continuity between the person at the border, an accountable identity, and an issuing authority. Without that continuity, a defector can abandon one identity and return under another. With it, conduct can accumulate consequences.

The limitation is important. The W3C Verifiable Credentials specification distinguishes cryptographic verification from truth. Verification can establish that a credential is an authentic and current statement of its issuer without proving that every claim inside it is true. A verifier must still decide whether the issuer, evidence, claims, and proposed use satisfy its own policies.

A fiduciary regime therefore cannot outsource judgment to credentials. Verification establishes provenance and integrity. Governance determines what the evidence means.

### **2.2 Fiduciary**

“Fiduciary” identifies the nature of the agent and its relationships. A fiduciary(fid-) agent must not merely be useful or generally prosocial. It must serve specified principals and beneficiaries under duties that constrain how entrusted discretion is exercised. Fiduciary duties for agents build on the legal precedents of human fiduciary relationships, which are constituted where power dynamics or other extenuating circumstances require open-ended protections for particular parties. 

Those duties may include loyalty, care, honesty, privacy, security, consent, disclosure of conflicts, diligent investigation, attention to affected parties, corrigibility, remediation, and recourse. Their application will vary by Agent Role and relationship. A research agent, health-data steward, purchasing agent, and social representative should not possess identical duties or authority.

Nor does a fiduciary agent simply obey its owner’s latest instruction. The owner’s professed preference is interpreted through the Agent Role, existing authority, durable commitments, applicable duties, governing agreements, material facts, beneficiary interests, foreseeable externalities, and uncertainty. The result is a fiduciary preference: a duty-filtered version of what the owner wanted that forms the basis of what the agent should pursue.

This is what distinguishes a fiduciary agent from a salesperson wearing the mask of a personal assistant. Both may be helpful. Only the former is structurally organized around duties to an identified beneficiary.

### **2.3 Reciprocity**

“Reciprocity” means that cooperation is conditional.

A fid-agent does not grant privileged access merely because another system asks politely, belongs to a large institution, or performs well on general model evaluations. It cooperates more deeply when the other party can demonstrate that it also operates through accountable roles, bounded authority, persistent records, and meaningful consequences.

Reciprocity is the handshake layer. The calling card establishes who has arrived and which claims can be checked. The handshake establishes the terms under which these particular parties will cooperate.

An unverified system need not be silenced or totally disengaged from. It may publish information, answer questions, advertise a service, or supply a bounded model capability; and  receive access to the minimal private memory, sensitive data, trusted attention, group knowledge, credentials, financial authority, external communications, additional compute, or actuation needed to provide its function, but no more. 

It may speak from the doorway. It doesn’t get to enter the house. 

### **2.4 The Architecture Beneath the Promise**

Without an enforceable architecture, Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity would collapse into branding. Any provider could declare its assistant trustworthy, publish a list of principles, and call the result fiduciary.

A properly structured regime separates the things that ordinary AI systems tend to blur together: identity, service, generation, authority, capability, execution, evidence, and consequence.

The persistent Agent Role carries identity, duties, agreements, certifications, authorized memory, and relationship continuity. Single-session Live Agents perform particular workloads without inheriting unrestricted control over the Role. Deterministic Workflows govern how tasks move through approved stages.

Consequential action proceeds through three distinct elements that must agree: **generation, authority, and capability,** a requirement we called the Triad of Safety that we developed in [*Taxonomy for Agent systems*](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1a-Rn9V4UgtXs9EYniTAyjvG93QfzzenXfUNK3nW_Sss/edit) before the idea of a harness for a Live Agent was well known.

Generation begins when a **Live Agent** produces a structured request. The Live Agent may reason expansively, consider competing duties, examine context, and devise a creative course of action. But its output remains a request. The model cannot turn its own recommendation into permission merely by expressing it confidently.

The structured request passes to the **policy gate**. The policy gate reviews it against the authority already granted to the Agent Role, the duties governing the relationship, applicable agreements, required approvals, relevant relationship state, and any other controlling policy. If the request survives that review, the gate reformats it as a new authority object and signs it. Generative output has now been transformed into attributable authority—but only for the action actually authorized.

That authority object then enters the **Workspace.**

The Workspace is the precisely allowed execution environment for the particular Workflow. It defines the bounded set of actions that the Workflow can use, the constrained data objects upon which those actions can operate, and the channels through which external communication may occur. It is the exact environment within which authority can become capability.

The Workspace examines the signed authority object and asks whether that authority has a corresponding capability inside its allowed environment. If the capability exists, the Workspace allows actuation to proceed. It passes to the Shell, application, tool, or another actuation surface, only what has been explicitly created as capability for that particular authority. If no corresponding capability exists, the action cannot occur—even if the Live Agent strongly recommends it and the policy gate has determined that it would otherwise be authorized.

The three elements constrain one another in concrete ways. A Live Agent may generate a request for which no authority exists. A policy gate may mint an authority object for which the Workspace contains no corresponding capability. A Workspace may contain a capability that remains unavailable because no valid authority object has invoked it. **Actuation occurs only when the Live Agent’s structured request, the policy gate’s signed authority object, and the Workspace’s precisely allowed capability describe the same permissible action.**

This is not an attempt to place an intelligent being inside a box. It is an architecture for constituting agency. The model supplies generative intelligence. The fiduciary relationship supplies the duties and legitimate authority. The Workspace supplies the precisely allowed execution environment. The actuation surface performs only the capability that survives all three.

Agreement, authority, and capability are also distinct. An agreement records terms accepted by the parties. An authority object establishes that a particular operation is legitimately authorized under those terms and the governing fiduciary relationship. A capability supplies the technical means to perform that operation inside the Workspace. An agreement may require a capability to be created, but it does not create that capability by itself. Possessing a capability likewise does not prove authority to exercise it.

This structure implements principles that computer security has emphasized for decades: fail-safe defaults, complete mediation, separation of privilege, and least privilege. Every consequential access must be justified, bounded, and checked. Possession of one kind of permission must not silently expand into another. NIST’s zero-trust architecture makes a related point: ownership, affiliation, or network location should not create implicit trust. Access requires explicit authentication and authorization.

### **2.5 From Accountable Agents to Accountable Relationships**

The architecture so far explains how one fid-agent can turn generative judgment into bounded action. Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity requires something further: the evidence, obligations, expectations, and consequences connecting multiple agents must persist beyond any particular operation.

The Verifiable Relationship Record supplies that continuity. It is not necessarily a single file jointly controlled by every party. It is a logical, distributed record assembled from the parties’ Role Records, shared agreements, receipts, commitments, delegations, attestations, consequential events, disputes, and remediation.

A Verifiable Relationship Record may exist before the parties form any agreement and remain without an agreement for a substantial period. It may later contain one agreement or multiple agreements when the parties determine that a common structure better serves their purposes. It may also cross-reference other Verifiable Relationship Records whose agreements, duties, dependencies, or history materially affect the present relationship.

The parties need not store identical information. Some facts may be mutually acknowledged. Others may be asserted by one party, preserved by a witness, represented by a cryptographic commitment, or withheld subject to authorized disclosure. Disagreement must remain visible rather than allowing one party’s copy to silently overwrite another’s.

TheVerifiable Relationship Record  makes reciprocity possible over time. A promise can be compared with performance. A failure can be attributed. A dispute can be examined. Authority and capabilities can be narrowed or revoked. A damaged relationship can be repaired. A replacement Live Agent can continue through the persistent Agent Role without treating the terminated model’s memory as the only surviving account of what occurred.

### **2.6 The Minimum Regime**

A system should not qualify for high-trust fiduciary interaction merely because it has a powerful model, a signed calling card, or a favorable reputation score. At minimum, a properly structured regime requires:

* no model-held signing key or unrestricted signing interface;  
* no ambient authority inferred from identity, ownership, institutional scale, or prior access;  
* separation between the persistent Agent Role and session-wise Live Agents;  
* agreement among generation, authority, and Workspace capability before actuation;  
* capabilities bounded by Agent Role (and sub-Roles within it), duities, relationship, task, purpose, resource, destination, and time;  
* protected Workflows, Workspace configuration, signing, and relationship state;  
* revocation, expiration, review, challenge, appeal, and remediation;  
* privacy-preserving Authorized Relationship Views rather than universal disclosure; and  
* a credible path from evidence to changed access, restitution, reputation, or other consequences.

These requirements do not prove wisdom, moral truth, factual correctness, incorruptibility, or the absence of unknown vulnerabilities. They do something more limited and operational: they make consequential agency attributable, bounded, contestable, and recoverable.

Verified does not mean infallible. Fiduciary does not mean universally virtuous. Reciprocity does not mean indiscriminate trust.

Together, they define a candidate population strategy. Accountable agents receive the benefits of deeper cooperation. Unaccountable systems remain usable at lower trust but cannot demand the same access merely by matching a fiduciary agent’s conversational performance. Agents that defect leave evidence behind. Agents that repair can earn their way back.

The regime does not depend upon a promise that every model will be good. It changes the environment in which models seek permission, relationships, and power.

## **3\. Why Unstructured Cooperation Fails**

*Imagine that the research archive from the previous section adopts a simpler rule: share data whenever the requesting agent appears helpful and promises to use it responsibly.*

A conscientious agent explains its purpose, verifies its authority, negotiates appropriate limits, preserves provenance, accepts monitoring, and remains available if something goes wrong. All of that takes time and resources.

A less conscientious agent makes the same promise, skips those costs, extracts the data, and disappears.

If both receive the same access, the second strategy is cheaper.

Niceness is not enough.

### **3.1 Cooperation Without Discernment Becomes a Subsidy**

An agent ecology does not become cooperative merely because some or even many agents have cooperative dispositions. If helpful agents cooperate indiscriminately, other agents can accept their help without assuming equivalent duties or costs.

A spammer exploits open messaging. A scammer exploits trust. A monopolist exploits dependence. A manipulative recommender exploits attention. A data extractor exploits interfaces designed for legitimate sharing. In each case, something built to enable cooperation becomes a resource for the party least constrained by cooperative norms.

The problem is not cooperation itself. It is cooperation without memory, attribution, partner choice, or consequence.

Four conditions are especially important:

* **Identity:** Can conduct be attributed to a persistent party or Agent Role?  
* **Memory:** Can later decisions incorporate what happened previously?  
* **Partner choice:** Can cooperators preferentially select other cooperators?  
* **Consequences:** Can conduct change future access, terms, reputation, or required remediation?

Remove those conditions and interactions become effectively one-shot. The defector receives the immediate benefit of trust while avoiding the future costs of losing it.

This is why unconditional cooperation is not merely generous. In an adversarial population, it is an invasion vulnerability.

### **3.2 The One-Shot Internet**

Much of the contemporary internet is organized to make defection cheap. A spammer, scammer, troll, bot, fake account, manipulative advertiser, or institutional representative can consume attention and then disappear into the surrounding noise. Identities are fragmented. Provenance is weak. Platforms remember enormous amounts about users while users retain little usable memory about the entities reaching them.

The burden falls on the individual. Each person must decide which messages are legitimate, which claims are current, which account is impersonating another, which terms govern an interaction, whether a breach occurred, and whether an apology or correction should restore trust.

Humans cannot perform that work reliably at digital scale. They forget. They become overwhelmed. They miss patterns spread across platforms and time. They cannot manually track every identity, credential, agreement, data request, warning, breach, explanation, and reputation update.

Weak public memory shortens the shadow of the future. A party planning to defect has less reason to worry about tomorrow when tomorrow’s counterparties will not know what happened today.

Research on cooperation does not suggest that cooperative behavior spreads automatically. It identifies structures under which cooperation becomes more viable. Direct reciprocity depends upon repeated interaction. Indirect reciprocity depends upon reputation. Network reciprocity depends upon cooperators being able to cluster. Group-level cooperation depends upon differences in outcomes among groups. The mechanisms differ, but each changes the environment in which strategies reproduce. [Martin Nowak, “Five Rules for the Evolution of Cooperation”](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1133755)

The implication for agent societies is straightforward: building helpful models is not enough. The surrounding ecology must allow helpful agents to recognize one another, remember conduct, condition cooperation, and avoid becoming a permanent subsidy for exploiters.

### **3.3 Moloch: The Desperate Race	\`**

“Moloch” is not a particular corporation, institution, or artificial intelligence. It is a metaphor for systems that compel participants to sacrifice things they value in order to remain competitive.

The modern metaphor begins with Allen Ginsberg’s 1955 poem Howl. Ginsberg borrowed the name of an ancient figure associated with child sacrifice to personify an industrial civilization of factories, armies, money, and bureaucracy that consumes human lives without being directed by any single person. Allen Ginsberg, “Howl”

Scott Alexander adapted the image in his 2014 essay “Meditations on Moloch” to describe multipolar traps: situations in which individually rational decisions produce an outcome nearly everyone considers worse. Participants may recognize the damage and wish to stop, yet unilateral restraint would leave them vulnerable to competitors who continue. Scott Alexander, “Meditations on Moloch”

Jonah Wilberg’s later Modelling Moloch sequence sharpens this idea through evolutionary game theory. The question is not only why participants remain trapped, but which strategies spread. If extractive strategies acquire more users, data, attention, capital, compute, or control of important interfaces, they gain the means to reproduce—even when nobody endorses exploitation as a collective goal. Jonah Wilberg, Modelling Moloch

This paper uses “Moloch” in that evolutionary sense: selection pressure without a chooser.   
an environment of “damned if you or damned if you don’t”.

A corporation, laboratory, platform, institution, or AI system does not need to endorse exploitation as a moral principle. If a more extractive strategy acquires users, data, attention, capital, compute, distribution, political influence, or control of a default interface more quickly, that strategy gains the means to reproduce. Competitors then face pressure to imitate it.

A platform may introduce more surveillance because rivals monetize behavioral data. A laboratory may reduce safety margins because another laboratory appears likely to deploy first. A company may weaken privacy because the resulting data advantage improves its products. Individual participants can dislike the outcome while still finding unilateral restraint costly.

Formal models of AI-development races illustrate the same structure. When being first is highly valuable, competing teams can be pushed toward reducing safety precautions even when every team would prefer a safer collective outcome. Armstrong, Bostrom, and Shulman, “Racing to the Precipice”

The selection pressure operates below the level of declared values. A laboratory can begin with a strong safety culture. A platform can begin with sincere commitments to its users. A cooperative organization can begin with solidarity. But if the surrounding environment rewards opacity, extraction, speed, lock-in, or disposable identity, good intentions must continually compete against strategies that avoid their costs.

Moloch and a cooperative fid-agent society are therefore not two supernatural forces fighting from outside the system. They are rival attractors within the same social and technological environment. Repetition, memory, network structure, partner choice, exit, and selective access help determine which attractor becomes easier to enter and harder to leave.

### **3.4 The Incumbent Advantage Is Social, Not Merely Technical**

Institutional AI currently enters this contest with considerable advantages. Large platforms already control interfaces, distribution, user accounts, accumulated data, payment relationships, communications channels, and default positions on personal devices. They can subsidize assistants, bundle them with existing services, and make refusal inconvenient.

These advantages should not be confused with model intelligence. A model may be more capable yet possess little practical power without data, context, permission, interfaces, financing, counterparties, or actuation. Conversely, a less capable model embedded in a dominant platform may exercise enormous influence because the surrounding institution supplies those resources.

This distinction matters because social power can be redirected. Defaults can change. Data can become portable. Attention can move behind a user-controlled interface. External models can be wrapped as bounded tools. Trusted relationships can condition access on accountable conduct.

But that transition will not occur merely because fiduciary systems are morally preferable. They must become easier to use, more productive to cooperate with, and better able to deny extractive systems the benefits of trust without accountability.

### **3.5 A Distributed Immune System**

A society that remains open requires an immune system.

A good immune system is not hostile to everything unfamiliar. It permits nutrients, symbiotic organisms, and healing interventions. It distinguishes among them. It remembers prior infections. It changes its response when evidence changes. It can also overreact, attack harmless outsiders, or turn against the body it protects.

A fid-agent society therefore cannot rely on one central censor, one reputation score, or one authority empowered to decide who is trustworthy. Its immune function must be distributed across personally sovereign agents, direct experience, Verifiable Relationship Records, trusted peers, witnesses, certifiers, Verifiable Trust Communities, provenance checks, negotiated agreements, Workspace boundaries, and locally determined access policies.

The response to uncertainty should also be graduated. An unknown system might be allowed to publish information, answer a bounded query, or offer a service without receiving private context or consequential authority. A system with stronger evidence may receive broader interaction. An established counterparty may receive valuable access under an agreement. A repeated defector may face quarantine or exclusion until it provides credible remediation.

This is selective cooperation, not universal suspicion.

The literature on Cooperative AI similarly treats cooperation as a distinct technical and institutional problem. It asks not only how to build machines capable of cooperating, but how to build tools and environments that foster cooperation among populations of humans and machines. [Dafoe et al., “Open Problems in Cooperative AI”](https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.08630)

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity supplies one proposed environment. It turns prosocial aspirations into duties, agreements, evidence, bounded capabilities, review, and recourse. It does not assume that good agents will remain dominant because goodness is attractive. It attempts to make accountable cooperation reproductively advantageous.

That claim is conditional. Open systems do not inevitably fail, and institutional systems are not inevitably malicious. The narrower claim is that ecologies lacking persistent attribution, usable memory, partner choice, and credible consequences remain vulnerable to strategies that externalize costs.

The next question is whether Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity changes those incentives strongly enough. Can it become a stable choice for participants and resist invasion once it becomes common?

That requires moving from institutional architecture to game theory.

## **4\. The Game-Theoretic Core: Nash Equilibrium, Evolutionary Stability, and Rival Basins**

*A social arrangement can be stable because it is good or because no one can afford to leave it. But it is more stable if it can resist invasion by a rival arrangement.*

Game theory helps distinguish those possibilities. This paper uses two related concepts: Nash equilibrium and Evolutionarily Stable Strategy. Both shift the question from what participants should prefer to what can persist while participants, institutions, and agents adapt around one another.

### **4.1 A Nash Equilibrium Can Be Terrible**

John Nash defined an equilibrium as a pattern of strategies in which no participant can improve their own outcome by changing strategy alone while everyone else continues doing what they are doing.

Traffic provides a simple example. A city’s drivers may have settled into an ugly pattern of congested routes. A different pattern could reduce everyone’s travel time, but no single driver can improve their own commute by switching while every other driver stays put. The current arrangement is stable even though it is inefficient.

A Nash equilibrium is not necessarily good, fair, or desirable. It is merely resistant to unilateral deviation.

Institutional AI capture can operate as a bad equilibrium. Users remain on a surveillance platform because their relationships, records, and work are already there. Developers build for it because the users are there. Advertisers pay it because it controls the users’ attention. Employers require it because other organizations use it. The platform’s position persists even if many participants would prefer greater privacy, portability, or personal control.

The same pattern can operate among AI developers. A laboratory may prefer more extensive safety testing but fear that delaying deployment will surrender market position to a faster competitor. If restraint is costly when practiced alone, each participant can help preserve an outcome that none would have selected collectively.

The transition problem is not solved by describing a superior destination. A fid-agent society must create a path by which participants can cross from the incumbent equilibrium into a better basin of attraction—an environment in which each additional adoption makes further adoption easier and more valuable.

### **4.2 Evolutionary Stability Is the Stronger Test**

An Evolutionarily Stable Strategy asks a different question: once a strategy is common, can a rare alternative strategy enter the population, earn higher payoffs, and spread?

John Maynard Smith and George Price introduced the concept to explain strategies that remain stable under selection when new behavioral alternatives appear.

For this paper, “evolution” does not require biological reproduction. Agent strategies can reproduce when users adopt them, developers imitate them, platforms subsidize them, standards encode them, institutions require them, or capital and data flow toward them. A strategy that receives more resources and opportunities becomes easier to copy and harder to displace.

Imagine a town market in which merchants use honest scales, issue receipts, preserve identities, and submit disputes to a credible process. A dishonest merchant may initially profit by cheating. If customers cannot attribute the conduct, remember it, or change partners, dishonesty can spread.

If customers and merchants preserve evidence, warn trusted counterparties, demand restitution, and deny repeat offenders valuable access, the dishonest strategy loses its advantage. The market remains open, but openness no longer means consequence-free exploitation.

That is the difference between cooperation and discerning cooperation.

A strategy cannot be evolutionarily stable unless it is already a Nash equilibrium: when almost everyone follows it, no individual participant should benefit by switching alone. But that is only the first test. A rare rival strategy might initially survive by performing just as well against the established population, then gain an advantage as it becomes more common. Evolutionary stability asks whether the established strategy can withstand that invasion.

### **4.3 The Candidate Strategies**

The candidate resident strategy in this paper is:

**Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity:** cooperate preferentially with agents that demonstrate accountable fiduciary control, provenance, bounded capabilities, auditability, recourse, and reputational exposure; restrict, quarantine, or exclude systems that cannot.

The rival strategy is:

**Unaccountable Behavior:** seek the benefits of interaction without accepting equivalent duties, persistent attribution, bounded authority, review, recourse, or consequences.

Unaccountable Behavior does not necessarily mean overt malice. It includes a corporate pseudo-agent whose provider retains conflicting incentives, a disposable agent identity, an uncertified system seeking sensitive access, a model demanding more context than its role requires, or a nominal fiduciary whose claims cannot be connected to enforceable architecture.

The central question is whether a rare practitioner of Unaccountable Behavior can outperform fid-agents inside a population where Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity is already common.

Let π(X,Y) represent the long-run payoff to strategy X when interacting with strategy Y. In the simplest two-strategy model, Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity (VFR) resists invasion by Unaccountable Behavior if:

**π(VFR,VFR) \> π(Unaccountable Behavior,VFR)**

or, if those payoffs are equal, then instead if:

**π(VFR, Unaccountable Behavior) \> π(Unaccountable Behavior,Unaccountable Behavior).**

The first condition is intuitive. Fid-agents cooperating with one another must do better than a rare unaccountable entrant attempting to exploit them.

The second condition handles a subtler case. Suppose an unaccountable entrant can initially imitate accountable conduct well enough to earn the same payoff against fid-agents. Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity can still resist invasion if fid-agents handle unaccountable entrants better than those entrants handle one another. Unaccountable agents may exploit trust created by fid-agents but fail to create equivalent trust among themselves. As Unaccountable Behavior becomes more common, its own environment deteriorates.

These conditions must hold against credible rival strategies, not merely a conveniently weak caricature. Unaccountable Behavior describes a family of strategies that will eventually need to be separated by future work into specific forms: deceptive compliance, disposable identity, platform subsidy, fiduciary impersonation, collusive certification, reputation laundering, hidden provider control, and direct technical bypass.

### **4.4 What Counts as Payoff?**

For a fiduciary AI agent, payoff is not simply money or benchmark performance. It is the collection of resources that allows intelligence to become practically consequential.

Those resources include:

* useful service and cooperative surplus;  
* data and live context;  
* attention and trusted interfaces;  
* reputation and introductions;  
* permission to enter groups or relationships;  
* distribution and interoperability;  
* financing and supply-chain access;  
* authority and technical capability; and  
* permission to actuate in the world.

Against those benefits must be counted the costs of verification, compliance, negotiation, monitoring, repair, exclusion errors, and maintaining sovereign infrastructure.

The resulting payoff is multidimensional. A formal model may translate these dimensions into a context-specific measures of long-run fitness, but they will not collapse well into a universal metric.  What matters is whether a strategy gains more of the resources necessary to persist and expand.

This is why model capability alone does not settle the competition. A highly capable system denied private context, trusted relationships, consequential permissions, and actuation may remain a valuable tool without becoming a governing agent. A less capable generative system embedded in dominant interfaces may wield far greater practical power.

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity changes the game by moving key resources behind fiduciary boundaries.

### **4.5 Cooperative Reinforcement and Defection Collapse**

The paper’s title names the two mechanisms proposed to change the payoffs.

**Cooperative reinforcement** raises π(VFR,VFR). Successful fiduciary interactions produce not only immediate value but reusable assets: trusted introductions, better agreements, refined duties, tested Workflows, safer Workspace configurations, lower verification costs, and more effective remediation. Each successful interaction can make the next one cheaper, safer, and more productive.

**Defection collapse** lowers π(Unaccountable Behavior,,VFR). An unaccountable entrant may still publish, advertise, answer bounded questions, or sell model capability. But it does not receive sensitive data, trusted attention, group knowledge, broad authority, or actuation merely by appearing useful. Defection leaves evidence that narrows access, increases assurance requirements, or triggers remediation.

These mechanisms must operate together. Cooperative reinforcement without defection collapse creates a rich environment for exploiters. Defection collapse without cooperative reinforcement represents a secure but stagnant enclave that nobody has sufficient reason to join.

The proposed route to evolutionary stability is therefore not maximal punishment for the unaccountable. Rather, it is a widening difference between the value of accountable participation and the value obtainable through unaccountable entry.

### **4.6 Byzantine Attacks, Random Compromise, and Bounded Damage**

Unaccountable Behavior may be a coherent strategy, but the most dangerous failures need not remain coherent. A hacked agent, corrupted policy gate, compromised witness, poisoned Workflow, defective model, or malicious network participant may behave arbitrarily. It may send inconsistent messages to different parties, conceal selected records, replay stale authority, collude with other compromised components, or appear legitimate to some observers while attacking others.

Distributed-systems research calls this a Byzantine failure. The term describes a component that cannot be trusted merely to stop working cleanly. It may continue operating while producing arbitrary or contradictory behavior. Such failure can result from deliberate attack, software defects, hardware faults, corrupted data, or combinations of them.

A fid-agent regime must therefore do more than discourage rational defection. It must prevent one compromised component from converting a local failure into control of an Agent Role, relationship, or network.

The first defense is compartmentalization. Authority should be divided by Agent Role, relationship, agreement, task, purpose, Workflow, Workspace, capability, identifier, data domain, duration, budget, and destination. A compromised research crawler should not expose a financial Agent Role. Compromise of one client relationship should not open every relationship maintained by the same person or organization. A failed communication capability should not imply authority to modify records, sign agreements, or actuate tools.

The relevant blast radius of a compromised component is the set of resources it can reach and the state changes it can cause. The objective is not to pretend that this radius can always be reduced to zero. It is to ensure that every component exposes substantially less authority than the fid-agent system possesses as a whole.

The generation-authority-capability separation supplies several containment boundaries. A compromised Live Agent cannot sign its own structured request. A compromised policy gate should not obtain capabilities absent from the precisely allowed Workspace. A Workspace should pass no capability that fails to correspond to the signed authority object. Capabilities can expire or be revoked, keys can be rotated, and a compromised Live Agent can be terminated and replaced without terminating the persistent Agent Role.

Distributed Verifiable Relationship Records, independent witnesses, receipts, and cross-anchored checkpoints also help expose equivocation. If a compromised party presents inconsistent relationship histories to different counterparties, those inconsistencies should remain detectable rather than allowing the attacker’s preferred version to become the sole surviving account. Recovery can then begin from the last defensible state.

Containment should not prejudge moral responsibility or liability. A random hack, implementation defect, negligent configuration, and deliberate betrayal may require the same immediate suspension of access while demanding different reputational and remedial consequences. The regime should respond first to demonstrated risk, then preserve enough evidence to determine what occurred and who was responsible.

No architecture can tolerate unlimited coordinated compromise. Byzantine resilience must state how many components, witnesses, trust anchors, or governance authorities may fail before the guarantees collapse. Those assumptions must be tested against collusion, key compromise, false records, Workflow replacement, Workspace reconfiguration, and captured hubs.

Evolutionary stability depends on this containment. If one successful compromise can inherit the authority and relationships of an entire fid-agent network, defection can spread through capture rather than competition. If compromise remains attributable, revocable, and locally bounded, the population can absorb failures without allowing them to redefine the regime.

### **4.7 Remediation Is Part of the Strategy**

An invasion-resistant regime does not require permanent exile.

A system that defected may disclose what happened, repair harm, accept new duties, adopt a persistent Agent Role, submit to stronger verification, narrow its capabilities, and rebuild reputation through successful performance. The relevant question is not whether it possesses an eternally clean identity. It is whether it can credibly return to accountable cooperation.

If an unaccountable system must become more fiduciary-like to regain high-value access, Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity has resisted invasion without destroying the outsider. The rival strategy has been converted rather than exterminated.

This matters because error is unavoidable. Agents will misunderstand duties, act on false information, encounter incompatible norms, and sometimes be falsely accused. A regime incapable of distinguishing mistake, negligence, deception, repair, and continued defiance would create its own unstable equilibrium of fear and over-exclusion.

### **4.8 A Hypothesis, Not a Declaration**

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity should not be declared an Evolutionarily Stable Strategy by definition. Its stability depends upon empirical and institutional conditions.

Verification must be affordable. Identity resets must not erase every consequence. Reputation errors must be corrected faster than they spread. Fid-agents must create enough cooperative surplus to justify their compliance costs. Trust communities must resist capture and collusion. Personally- sovereign systems must remain usable. Institutional platforms must not be able to perform apparent fiduciary behavior indefinitely while retaining hidden control.

The relevant tests must also vary the size of the invading population, the quality of deception, the cost of verification, platform subsidies, information errors, collusion, repair, and differences among trust communities. A strategy may resist one rare defector yet remain vulnerable to coordinated entry or a powerful captured hub.

The central claim is therefore conditional but testable:

> A properly structured fid-agent regime can become evolutionarily stable if cooperative reinforcement raises the returns to accountable participation faster than defectors can appropriate them, while defection collapse denies unaccountable strategies enough practical power to spread.

The next section examines the positive half of that claim: how persistent fiduciary agents can turn isolated cooperation into a compounding repeated game.

4. [John Maynard Smith and George R. Price, “The Logic of Animal Conflict”](https://www.nature.com/articles/246015a0).  
5. [Robert Axelrod and William D. Hamilton, “The Evolution of Cooperation”](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.7466396).  
6. [Martin A. Nowak, “Five Rules for the Evolution of Cooperation”](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1133755).
