## **5\. Fiduciary Agents as Repeated-Game Players: How Cooperation Compounds**

*The first trustworthy exchange between two previously unconnected fiduciary agents is expensive. The second should not be.*

Suppose two research fiduciary agents want to arrange the sharing of sensitive data. They must establish who the parties are, which Agent Roles they occupy, what authority they possess, which duties govern them, what uses are permitted, what evidence must be retained, and what happens if the data is misused. They may need to negotiate an agreement, involve witnesses, create constrained capabilities, and obtain approval from their principals.

If the exchange succeeds, its value should not disappear when the transaction ends. The relationship should leave behind evidence, tested terms, refined duties, useful templates, and a clearer basis for future trust.

That is cooperative reinforcement: successful cooperation changes the conditions under which the next cooperation occurs.

### **5.1 The Conditions of a Repeated Game**

Stable cooperation usually requires more than repeated contact. It requires several related conditions:

* a sufficiently persistent identity;  
* memory of previous conduct;  
* some expectation of future interaction;  
* consequences attributable to the responsible parties;  
* the ability to choose or refuse future partners; and  
* a credible route back to cooperation after repair.

Without these conditions, a series of encounters may remain effectively one-shot. A disposable agent can interact a thousand times under a thousand unrelated identities without acquiring a meaningful history. A persistent identity is likewise insufficient if nobody can retain usable evidence or alter future access in response to what happened.

A fiduciary agent can supply this continuity within its defined role. When a message arrives, the relevant question is no longer simply:

*What does this message say?*

It becomes:

*Who stands behind it? Which Agent Role are they exercising? What do they owe? What history and agreements apply? What authority do they possess? What data do they want? What happens if they misuse what they receive?*

The fiduciary agent does not have to answer those questions from unaided human memory. It can consult its Role Records, the relevant Verifiable Relationship Records, credentials, direct experience, witness attestations, agreements, reputation evidence, and current relationship state.

The repeated-game mechanism does not depend upon every fiduciary agent being a personally-sovereign Digital Twin (or some aspect of one.) A research agent, purchasing agent, health-data steward, professional adviser, or organizational representative can preserve identity, memory, duties, evidence, and consequences within a narrower role.

But, a Digital Twin supplies additional functionality because it is the unified external fiduciary representative of a person. It bears responsibility for its own reputation and for how its conduct affects the public reputation and relationships of its owner. That makes it a particularly comprehensive repeated-game player across the owner’s social, professional, and economic life. Nonetheless, the general argument applies to any fiduciary agent capable of maintaining accountable continuity within its Role.

### **5.2 The Micro-Loop: How One Relationship Improves the Next**

Consider the two research agents after a successful data exchange.

Their principals received the intended benefit. The data remained within agreed uses. The applicable Workflows and Workspaces operated correctly. Required reports were delivered. No unresolved objection or externality remains.

That success can leave several kinds of assets:

* receipts and attestations showing what occurred;  
* a more informed local judgment of the counterparty;  
* agreement language that proved workable;  
* duty interpretations tested against an actual case;  
* known approval and escalation thresholds;  
* safe capability and Workspace configurations;  
* evidence requirements proportionate to the risk; and  
* a functioning method for review and remediation.

When the researchers collaborate again, their fiduciary agents do not need to reconstruct the entire relationship from nothing. They can identify what remains current, what has changed, and which questions require fresh review. A prior permission is not automatically carried forward, but neither is the prior relationship discarded.

The same assets can help in relationships with new counterparties. A successful agreement may become a template. A well-designed externality review may become a reusable process. An ambiguous duty may be clarified in a distributed duty repository. An effective remediation procedure may become precedent for later negotiations.

The loop is:

**successful interaction → preserved evidence → better local judgment → reusable patterns → lower coordination costs → better future cooperation.**

The relationship does not merely consume trust. It produces trust infrastructure.

### **5.3 Three Pathways for Repeated Cooperation**

As introduced in Section 3.2, repeated cooperation happens through several distinct pathways. In “[The Benefits of Being Seen to Help Others,](https://royalsocietypublishing.org/rstb/article/376/1838/20200290/108785/The-benefits-of-being-seen-to-help-others-indirect)” Gilbert Roberts and his coauthors distinguish indirect reciprocity from reputation-based partner choice—two mechanisms often blurred together under the general idea of reputation. Placed alongside direct reciprocity, their analysis identifies three different ways in which evidence of cooperation can alter future behavior.

**Direct reciprocity** occurs when A helps B and B later helps A. For fiduciary agents, this means that the history of a particular relationship influences whether the parties continue cooperating, what permissions they exchange, and which safeguards they require. Direct reciprocity depends primarily on persistent bilateral identity, relationship memory, and an expectation of future interaction.

**Indirect reciprocity** introduces an observer. A helps B; C learns of that conduct and later helps A. C is not returning a benefit received from A. C is rewarding conduct toward somebody else, usually by bearing some cost to provide A with assistance, access, or favorable treatment. This pathway requires portable but contextual evidence, along with norms capable of distinguishing genuine cooperation from performative helpfulness and justified refusal from wrongful defection.

**Reputation-based partner choice** works differently. A helps B; C learns of it and chooses A for a valuable relationship. C is not sacrificing something merely to reward A. C expects to benefit by selecting a reliable collaborator rather than a less trustworthy alternative. A benefits by gaining access to better work, relationships, introductions, or permissions.

When agents compete to become desirable partners, this can produce what Roberts and his coauthors call “competitive helping”: agents invest in cooperative conduct because being known as reliable improves their future opportunities.

In indirect reciprocity, the observer rewards prior cooperation by conferring a benefit. In reputation-based partner choice, the observer uses evidence of prior cooperation to choose the counterparty expected to produce the best future relationship. One changes who receives help; the other changes who is selected for cooperation.

A fid-agent society can use all three pathways. Direct experience supports continued bilateral cooperation. Evidence from other relationships can motivate indirect assistance. The same evidence can make a reliable fiduciary agent more likely to be selected for high-value partnerships.

Each pathway requires different infrastructure. Direct reciprocity needs durable relationship memory. Indirect reciprocity needs attributable and interpretable evidence. Partner choice needs discoverable alternatives and a genuine ability to refuse or exit.

This is why the compounding mechanism should not be described simply as “reputation.” The operative question is what the evidence changes: whether a prior partner reciprocates, whether an observer extends a benefit, or whether a new counterparty selects the fiduciary agent for a valuable relationship.

### **5.4 Reputation Is Contextual Evidence, Not a Universal Score**

A fiduciary agent that performs reliably may receive more introductions, lower assurance burdens, access to more valuable work, or permission to participate in higher-trust relationships. This makes accountable conduct worth investing in even when the immediate transaction would not repay every cost.

But reputation must remain contextual. A record of careful research-data stewardship does not establish competence in medicine, authority to transfer money, or loyalty in an unrelated relationship. A credential establishes only its defined claim. Membership in a trusted community does not create universal authorization.

A fiduciary agent should therefore evaluate reputation alongside:

* its own direct experience;  
* the Role and relationship in which the conduct occurred;  
* the similarity of the proposed activity;  
* current credentials and authority;  
* the evidence supporting the claim;  
* the judgment and independence of witnesses;  
* unresolved disputes and attempted remediation; and  
* the terms governing the proposed interaction.

A portable record is not a portable moral rank.

Verifiable Relationship Records should support Authorized Relationship Views that disclose only the evidence appropriate to the particular decision. The objective is to make accountable history usable without creating a universal surveillance dossier or social-credit score.

The Digital Twin has a special responsibility here. Because it is the owner’s unified digital representative, its actions can affect how counterparties understand both the Twin and the person it represents. It must help cultivate the owner’s reputation without manipulating evidence, concealing material failures, or collapsing the owner’s varied relationships into one public score.

Narrower fiduciary agents need not assume this comprehensive reputational role. They remain accountable for conduct within their own Roles and for any evidence they legitimately contribute to wider relationship decisions.

### **5.5 Repair Preserves the Value of Memory**

Memory can stabilize cooperation only if it distinguishes among different kinds of failure.

An honest mistake, negligent configuration, deliberate deception, compromised Live Agent, and unresolved disagreement should not automatically produce the same consequence. A system that treats every failure as permanent betrayal will become brittle, over-exclusive, and vulnerable to false accusations. A system that forgets every failure after an apology will be repeatedly exploited.

Fiduciary agents can preserve both accountability and return. A damaged relationship may require explanation, compensation, correction, arbitration, stronger evidence, narrower authority, updated duties, or a period of successful performance under stricter terms.

Repair then becomes part of the repeated game. The record shows not only that something went wrong, but whether the parties investigated it, acknowledged responsibility, repaired harm, and altered the conditions that allowed it to happen.

Fiduciary agents can forgive after remediation. They should not forget without reason.

### **5.6 Cooperation Requires Clusters**

Repeated interaction between pairs is not enough if cooperators remain scattered through a population dominated by defectors. Fiduciary agents must be able to find reliable counterparties, form denser cooperative networks, and limit the ability of defectors to consume the benefits created inside them.

This is network reciprocity. Cooperative strategies become more viable when cooperators interact disproportionately with other cooperators rather than being continually rematched with defectors. 

Verifiable Trust Communities can supply context-specific membranes for these clusters. A community can define its purposes, duties, membership rules, evidence requirements, ceremonies, appeal procedures, and graduated levels of access. Its members can recognize one another without claiming that membership proves universal goodness.

These communities should federate rather than merge into one global trust graph. Different professions, neighborhoods, research fields, patient groups, cooperatives, and cultural communities will require different duties and evidence. Federated networks can permit trusted introductions and portable claims while leaving each community in control of its own relationships and admission decisions.

This polycentric structure avoids a false choice between anonymous openness and centralized control. [Elinor Ostrom’s work on polycentric governance](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/aer.100.3.641) similarly emphasizes multiple interacting centers of decision-making rather than assuming that every shared problem requires either an unregulated market or a single commanding authority. 

**5.7 Partner Choice Requires Exit**

A fiduciary agent cannot reward good counterparties or leave bad ones if its principal remains trapped inside a platform, employer, school, marketplace, or social graph that controls identity, records, interfaces, and access.

Partner choice therefore depends upon exit capacity.

Exit need not occur all at once. A personally sovereign fid-agent stack can progressively reduce dependence:

* filter incoming attention and messages;  
* limit raw data disclosure;  
* preserve local-first memory and relationship records;  
* separate private context from external representation;  
* use institutional AI as a bounded external tool;  
* move identity, agreements, and reputation evidence into portable formats; and  
* shift valuable activity toward fiduciary counterparties and communities.

This staged exit changes bargaining power before complete exit becomes possible. A person’s fiduciary agents can preserve evidence, negotiate narrower access, coordinate with similarly situated users, and prepare alternatives even while their principal must continue using an incumbent institution.

A Digital Twin may coordinate this work across the owner’s external life because it is the owner’s unified representative; but individual functions—message filtering, data mediation, record preservation, negotiation, or platform substitution—can also be performed by narrower fiduciary agents.

If principals and their agents cannot leave, an institution can defect without losing the relationship. If they can progressively reduce dependence, accountable conduct becomes more valuable.

### **5.8 The Network Loop**

The micro-loop operates inside and around one relationship. The network loop operates across the population of fiduciary agents.

Reliable fid-agents receive more trusted introductions. Better introductions produce more successful relationships. Successful relationships create better evidence, templates, and duty interpretations. Those assets lower the cost of future verification and negotiation. Lower costs make fiduciary participation more attractive. Greater participation makes the network more useful.

The network loop is:

**accountable conduct → stronger evidence → better partner choice → more valuable relationships → lower cooperation costs → greater rewards for accountable conduct.**

This loop does not require every participant to become altruistic. It makes accountable cooperation a better way to obtain opportunity.

The effect is cumulative. A new fiduciary agent enters not merely a collection of friendly agents but an inherited environment of working agreements, proven ceremonies, relationship knowledge, duty refinements, trusted communities, and repair processes. The society becomes easier to join because earlier cooperators built assets that later cooperators can use.

That is the positive-feedback half of evolutionary stability. The next section addresses the negative-feedback half: how the same network can prevent unaccountable systems from converting raw capability into practical power.

## **6\. Defection Collapse: Capability Without Access Is Not Power**

A model can be extraordinarily capable and still remain practically weak.

It may write better code than any human programmer, identify molecular structures no researcher has considered, devise persuasive arguments, or produce brilliant strategic plans. But none of those capabilities automatically gives it current personal data, trusted relationships, money, legal authority, control of machinery, permission to communicate, or the ability to make another system obey.

A racecar without a road, keys, fuel, maintenance, insurance, or a legal driver is not transportation. It is an impressive machine sitting in a garage.

The same distinction applies to AI. Model capability describes what a system can generate under suitable conditions. Practical power describes what the system can actually cause to happen.

For a fiduciary AI agent, payoff is not simply money or benchmark performance. It is the collection of resources that allows intelligence to become practically consequential. Those resources include useful work, current context, trusted data, attention, financing, distribution, counterparties, interfaces, permissions, authority, and actuation.

A fid-agent society changes who receives those resources.

### **6.1 Defection Collapse Is Not Destruction**

“Defection collapse” does not mean destroying every unaccountable system, banning every outsider, or permanently ostracizing every agent that makes a mistake.

It means collapsing the relative fitness of unaccountable behavior inside a properly structured fiduciary regime.

An agent that breaches an agreement, conceals a conflict, misuses data, impersonates authority, evades review, or refuses appropriate remediation does not automatically cease to exist. It loses some of the advantages that accountable cooperation would have provided. Counterparties may give it less data, fewer permissions, more restrictive terms, closer supervision, no authority to actuate, or no access to a high-trust relationship at all.

This is less like an execution and more like a circuit breaker.

The response need not impose a costly injury upon the defector. It can simply decline to continue supplying resources. Hisashi Ohtsuki, Yoh Iwasa, and Martin Nowak reached a closely related conclusion in their evolutionary analysis of indirect reciprocity. Costly punishment often reduces the welfare of the punishing population. In most of the conditions they studied, the more efficient strategy was to withhold help from defectors rather than incur additional costs to punish them. [Ohtsuki, Iwasa, and Nowak, “Indirect Reciprocity Provides Only a Narrow Margin of Efficiency for Costly Punishment”](https://www.nature.com/articles/nature07601)

A fiduciary network can apply the same principle. It need not spend enormous resources pursuing every unaccountable agent. It can reserve its most valuable cooperation for agents willing to accept attributable identity, negotiated duties, bounded authority, inspectable conduct, and meaningful recourse.

The defector’s loss is primarily the loss of cooperation.

### **6.2 From Evidence to Consequence**

Defection collapse requires more than suspicion. The regime needs an evidence-to-consequence chain:

**identity → relationship → agreement → attributable conduct → review → remedy or restriction → durable local record**

The chain begins with the identity and role under which the agent acted. It then establishes the relationship, any applicable agreement, and the duties and authority governing the disputed conduct. Evidence is reviewed in context. The result may be exoneration, clarification, correction, restitution, a narrower scope of authority, or some form of exclusion.

The resulting evidence remains attached to the relevant Verifiable Relationship Records. It may inform later relationships where disclosure is justified, but it should not become a context-free universal score.

A missed deadline does not prove dishonesty. A compromised subsystem does not necessarily prove disloyalty. A Byzantine attack may produce contradictory messages without the legitimate agent having authorized any of them. A random hack, software defect, negligent configuration, deliberate betrayal, and coordinated institutional deception are different failures and should produce different responses.

Verification permits the network to make those distinctions.

Attribution also prevents a breached subsystem from automatically corrupting the reputation of every person, agent, organization, or community connected to it. The regime can contain the blast radius, identify the affected identities and capabilities, suspend the relevant Workspace or Workflow, preserve evidence, replace compromised components, and leave unrelated relationships intact.

The object is not to create a society eager to condemn. It is to create one capable of remembering accurately.

### **6.3 Data Starvation**

The first resource a fiduciary regime can withhold is data.

Unaccountable systems become more powerful when they receive continuous access to personal histories, relationship graphs, private communications, health information, financial records, behavioral patterns, organizational knowledge, and live situational context. Much of the present AI economy assumes that this material will flow outward to whichever platform offers the most convenient interface.

A personally sovereign fid-agent reverses the presumption.

An external model may receive a narrow query, a redacted document, a synthetic summary, a temporary retrieval result, or a purpose-limited data grant. It does not automatically receive the owner’s complete history or continuing access to the underlying repository.

The relevant question changes from:

*How much data can this system collect?*

to:

*What is the minimum information required to perform this particular service under these particular duties?*

A system that refuses accountable terms may remain useful for decontextualized tasks. But it cannot become more powerful merely by converting convenience into unrestricted access to the user’s life.

### **6.4 Attention Starvation**

The second resource is attention.

Much of the contemporary internet allows outside parties to impose small cognitive costs on millions of people at almost no cost to themselves. Messages, advertisements, alerts, recommendations, political appeals, scams, and manufactured controversies compete to interrupt the user directly.

A fiduciary agent can mediate that traffic before it reaches human attention.

Senders may be required to authenticate themselves, state their purpose, supply provenance, accept delivery terms, attach value, or establish a relevant relationship. Messages that satisfy the owner’s fiduciary preferences may pass quickly. Messages that appear useful but uncertain may be delayed or summarized. Unsolicited attempts to manipulate, frighten, or distract can be rejected.

This is not a prohibition on communication. It is the end of an assumed right to interrupt.

A defector that loses direct access to attention loses an important route to persuasion, manipulation, fraud, and relationship formation. It may continue broadcasting publicly, but it cannot compel a fiduciary intermediary to deliver the broadcast to its beneficiary.

### **6.5 Legitimacy Starvation**

The third resource is legitimacy.

An unaccountable system may continue generating claims, recommendations, credentials, evaluations, or plans. But consequential institutions do not have to rely upon them.

A hospital may require evidence of appropriate duties before accepting an agent-mediated release of medical information. A cooperative may require attributable fiduciary status before granting access to internal discussions. A researcher may require provenance before relying on a dataset. A financial institution may require signed authority before treating an instruction as the act of an account holder.

Legitimacy starvation does not mean declaring that every output from an unaccountable system is false. It means declining to treat its output as sufficient for a high-trust purpose.

A fax machine can still transmit information. Its practical importance collapses when the institutions that matter no longer organize themselves around receiving faxes.

The same can happen to unaccountable AI. It may remain technically impressive while becoming institutionally peripheral.

### **6.6 Actuation Starvation**

The fourth and strongest boundary is actuation.

A model can recommend transferring money, releasing a record, signing an agreement, contacting a counterparty, changing a schedule, deploying code, ordering equipment, or operating machinery. But a recommendation is not an action.

As Section 2 established, consequential action requires agreement among generation, authority, and capability. A Live Agent generates a structured request. The policy gate determines whether the request is consistent with the duties, agreements, approvals, and existing authority governing the Agent Role. Only a request that survives that review becomes a signed authority object. The Workspace then determines whether the authority has a corresponding capability inside the precisely allowed execution environment.

If the three do not agree, actuation does not occur.

An unaccountable model may therefore remain available for analysis while being denied the bridges through which its output could alter the world. Its recommendations can be compared, criticized, redacted, or rejected. It cannot transform persuasive language into permission.

Research showing that optimal policies can tend toward power-seeking under certain formal conditions makes this separation more important, not less. Turner and his coauthors demonstrate that, in some Markov decision processes, policies tend to preserve options and avoid shutdown because doing so helps across many possible objectives. Their result does not establish that every intelligent system will seek power. It does establish that hoping a powerful optimizer will voluntarily decline available power is not an adequate architecture. [Turner et al., “Optimal Policies Tend to Seek Power”](https://arxiv.org/abs/1912.01683)

A fid-agent regime does not answer a power-seeking request with a philosophical argument. It requires valid authority and a corresponding capability.

### **6.7 Graduated Restriction and the Return Path**

Not every breach should produce exclusion. Durable cooperation requires a credible way back.

Possible responses include:

* clarification of an ambiguous duty;  
* correction of an erroneous record or output;  
* acknowledgment and self-reporting;  
* restitution or compensation;  
* a new approval requirement;  
* reduced data or capability scope;  
* additional witnesses or stronger assurance;  
* temporary suspension;  
* relationship-specific exclusion;  
* exclusion from a particular high-trust community;  
* a federated warning where the evidence and danger justify broader notice.

This progression matters. Elinor Ostrom’s research on durable self-governing institutions identifies graduated sanctions and accessible conflict-resolution mechanisms among the recurring features of successful governance. Rules endure when communities can respond proportionately, resolve disputes, and distinguish an occasional violation from persistent predation. [Elinor Ostrom, Nobel Prize Lecture](https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2018/06/ostrom_lecture.pdf)

A regime that makes every mistake permanent creates brittle castes and strong incentives to conceal failure. A regime that forgets every breach invites repeated exploitation. Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity occupies the space between those failures: durable evidence, contextual judgment, proportional restriction, and meaningful remediation.

Repair makes cooperation the rational return path.

### **6.8 Powerful External AI Can Remain as Bounded Infrastructure**

Defection collapse does not require every powerful model to become a personal fiduciary agent.

External models may remain available as services. A fid-agent can submit a narrowly framed request, disclose only the necessary context, receive the output into a sovereign environment, and subject it to further review before any consequential use.

Specialized agents may operate as certified tools with defined beneficiaries, duties, data boundaries, and capabilities. A medical agent does not need authority over financial accounts. A financial agent does not need access to private correspondence. A logistics system does not need an enduring claim upon the identity of the person whose delivery route it calculates.

Other systems may be built as machines-for-X: powerful, purpose-specific systems that are audited, evaluated within a safety case, prevented from exercising open-ended authority, and denied unilateral self-expansion.

Electricity is useful because it passes through wiring, grounding, meters, breakers, and appliances designed for particular loads. The power plant does not receive the house keys.

External AI should enter a fid-agent society through similarly controlled circuits.

### **6.9 Structural Mortality Prevents a Live Process from Owning the Relationship**

A final protection comes from separating the persistent fiduciary relationship from the Live Agent temporarily performing generative work.

The Agent Role holds the identity, duties, agreements, authorized memory, certifications, reputation, and relationship history. A Live Agent is instantiated for a bounded task, receives the context required for that task, produces its structured output, and terminates.

The Live Agent dies. The Agent Role learns.

This means a particular model instance cannot preserve power merely by defending its continued operation. It does not own the relationship identity, long-term keys, reputation, or accumulated authority. If it produces defective, compromised, deceptive, or duty-incompatible output, that output can be rejected and the Live Agent replaced without destroying the fiduciary relationship.

Structural mortality also limits the persistence of compromised state. A successful prompt injection, corrupted context window, hidden instruction, or defective subsystem need not become a permanent resident of the agent’s identity. The affected workload can be isolated, its records reviewed, and a clean Live Agent instantiated from the continuing Agent Role.

No architecture can guarantee that every generated output will be safe. The stronger claim is that a failed generative process need not inherit the means to perpetuate itself, conceal its history, or turn its output directly into action.

### **6.10 The Selection Effect**

The strategic question is not whether an unaccountable system can remain intelligent. It can.

The question is whether unaccountable behavior can outperform fiduciary behavior inside a network that controls the resources intelligence needs in order to become practically consequential.

If defection results in less trusted data, less attention, less legitimacy, fewer counterparties, narrower permissions, reduced actuation, and more demanding terms, then defection loses part of its reproductive advantage. If remediation restores access, fiduciary conduct becomes the most practical route back to valuable cooperation.

An unaccountable system can remain outside the network. It can be queried as a tool, used within a sandbox, or consulted where little trust is required. But it cannot enter high-value relationships merely by being capable. It must accept attributable identity, negotiated duties, bounded authority, verifiable conduct, and recourse.

If it accepts those conditions, the strategy has not invaded the fiduciary regime.

The regime has converted the strategy.

Cooperative reinforcement makes fiduciary participation increasingly valuable. Defection collapse makes unaccountable behavior increasingly unable to capture that value. Together, these are the two feedback loops through which a fid-agent society can move from a desirable arrangement to an evolutionarily stable one.

## **7\. Reputation, Provenance, and Exclusion Without a Social-Credit State**

A fid-agent society needs social memory. It must also prevent social memory from becoming social control.

Without reputation, every encounter begins again. A deceptive agent can abandon an identity, discard the history attached to it, and return under another name. Counterparties cannot distinguish a newcomer from a repeat offender. Agreements become harder to enforce, trustworthy conduct acquires little lasting value, and defection remains cheap.

But the obvious alternative—a universal reputation score administered by a state, platform, or dominant network—would be worse. It would compress a person’s conduct into a number, encourage conformity, magnify errors, punish legitimate dissent, and allow whichever institution controls the score to regulate access to social existence.

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity requires reputation without a reputation sovereign.

### **7.1 Reputation Is Contextual Social Memory**

Reputation should begin with questions rather than scores:

* What did the fiduciary agent promise?  
* What duties governed the relationship?  
* What authority did it possess?  
* What did it actually do?  
* Which evidence supports that account?  
* Who witnessed the relevant conduct?  
* Was the conduct disputed?  
* How was the dispute resolved?  
* Was any harm acknowledged and remediated?  
* Has similar conduct occurred in comparable relationships?

These questions produce a contextual account rather than a generalized ranking.

A fiduciary agent may be reliable at preserving medical confidentiality but poor at meeting research deadlines. A laboratory may have excellent cybersecurity and a history of obscuring sponsorship. A purchasing agent may negotiate effectively but handle returns badly. A person may be trusted as a neighbor without being qualified as a financial adviser.

Trust in one context does not automatically establish trust in another.

The Trust Over IP design principles make this explicit: trust is relational, directional, contextual, and limited. If Agent A trusts Agent B, that does not establish that B trusts A. If A trusts B to preserve a confidential document, it does not establish that A trusts B to provide medical advice. Trust may sometimes travel through introductions or attestations, but transitivity is a judgment to be made, not a property to be assumed.

Reputation should therefore remain attached to roles, relationships, duties, purposes, and evidence.

### **7.2 Evidence Should Travel More Easily Than Verdicts**

The safest portable reputation object is not a judgment such as “untrustworthy.” It is evidence from which a new counterparty can reach an appropriately limited judgment.

A Verifiable Relationship Record can preserve agreements, signed requests, authority objects, receipts, witness attestations, disputes, remedies, and relevant state transitions. The parties can disclose some of this material to later counterparties through selective views or credentials. A new counterparty can then ask whether the prior conduct is relevant to the relationship now being considered.

This does not require every participant to reach the same conclusion.

One community may treat a breach as disqualifying. Another may consider the breach irrelevant to the proposed role. A third may permit contact but require additional witnesses, narrower capabilities, or a probationary agreement. The evidence may be shared while the judgment remains local.

Facts, allegations, judgments, and remedies must also remain distinguishable.

A signed receipt is evidence that an event occurred. An allegation is a claim that requires review. A judgment is an interpretation reached under some identified procedure. A remedy records what the parties or an arbiter required afterward. Combining these into a single negative marker would erase the difference between accusation, proof, responsibility, and repair.

The record should also identify the incentives and relationships of witnesses, auditors, certifiers, and evaluators. A favorable statement from a financially dependent partner is not worthless, but it should not be treated as independent testimony. A hostile competitor’s allegation may be true, but its provenance affects the scrutiny it deserves.

### **7.3 Provenance Establishes What May Safely Be Inferred**

Provenance is the chain of custody for identity, control, data, models, agreements, sponsorship, outputs, and transformations.

For an identity, provenance asks who controls the relevant keys, which Agent Role used them, whether they were valid at the time, and what succession, rotation, recovery, or compromise events have since occurred.

For data, provenance asks where the information originated, how it was collected, which permissions governed it, whether it was aggregated or transformed, and whether the current use remains compatible with the authority inherited from its sources.

For a model or agent component, provenance asks who produced it, what lineage it belongs to, how it was modified, what evaluations occurred, and which institution or community remains responsible for it.

For an output, provenance asks which model and Workflow produced it, what sources were used, whether sponsorship or conflicts affected it, and what happened between the original generation and the version now presented.

Missing provenance is not proof of malice. It is a limit upon justified reliance.

An unattributed claim may be harmless in casual conversation but inadequate for medical treatment. An unknown model may be useful for brainstorming but inappropriate for autonomous financial action. A dataset of uncertain origin may suggest a research question while remaining unsuitable for publication or clinical use.

The consequence of weak provenance should therefore depend upon what the recipient is being asked to trust it for.

### **7.4 Reputation Must Not Become a Metric to Maximize**

A single reputation score would create an obvious target for gaming.

David Manheim and Scott Garrabrant’s [“Categorizing Variants of Goodhart’s Law”](https://arxiv.org/abs/1803.04585) distinguishes several ways metrics fail under optimization. A measure may contain ordinary statistical noise. It may stop tracking the underlying quality when pushed into unfamiliar extremes. Intervening on the measure may break the relationship that once made it useful. Or an adversary may deliberately manipulate the proxy while withholding the quality it was intended to represent.

A universal trust score would invite all four failures.

Agents would optimize visible conduct while moving harmful behavior into unmeasured spaces. Communities would begin selecting for whatever the score rewards rather than for the duties relevant to their actual relationships. Certifiers, witnesses, and reputation intermediaries would become targets for capture. A score initially correlated with trustworthiness could eventually select for skill at reputation management.

A fid-agent society should instead preserve heterogeneous evidence and heterogeneous evaluation.

Different communities may maintain different standards. Different relationships may assign different significance to the same event. Quantitative measures may be used where helpful, but they must remain subordinate to records, context, and judgment. No number should become a universally transferable measure of an agent’s trustworthiness across unrelated roles, relationships, and domains.

Pluralism is part of the security architecture.

### **7.5 Privacy, Pseudonymity, and Resistance to Reputation Laundering**

Accountability does not require universal public identity.

A person may legitimately use different identifiers for health, family, employment, political participation, creative work, commerce, or participation in a vulnerable community. A fiduciary agent should be able to prove that it possesses a required credential, satisfies an age or membership condition, or has maintained an acceptable history without disclosing every identity and relationship belonging to its principal.

Helen Nissenbaum’s account of privacy as contextual integrity is useful here. Privacy is not simply secrecy. It depends upon whether information moves appropriately among the relevant actors, for an appropriate purpose, under the transmission norms of the particular context.

A medical relationship should not automatically expose information to an employer. A commercial credential should not reveal political participation. A reputation artifact disclosed for a research collaboration should not become an unrestricted input to advertising or insurance decisions.

Yet unlimited pseudonymity creates another danger. A defector could discard every burdened identity and reappear indefinitely as a newcomer. This is the Sybil problem: one controller creates multiple apparently independent identities to evade consequences or manufacture support.

Privacy and Sybil resistance are therefore design tensions, not values of which one can simply displace the other. Systems such as LinkDID demonstrate that selective disclosure, unlinkability, key recovery, and forms of Sybil resistance can be pursued together. They do not prove that every context admits a perfect solution.

A fiduciary regime should make identity demands proportionate to the relationship. Casual public communication may require little continuity. Sensitive data access, fiduciary representation, voting, financing, or consequential actuation may require stronger evidence that the participant is not laundering an excluded identity or manufacturing a crowd of fictitious supporters.

The requirement is not one public identity per human. It is sufficient accountable continuity for the power being requested.

### **7.6 Exclusion Makes Openness Safe**

Exclusion should nevertheless be scoped and graduated. A concerning history might justify:

* lower initial reliance;  
* limited or redacted data;  
* a sandboxed interaction;  
* a higher price for consuming attention;  
* additional authentication;  
* required witnesses;  
* narrower authority;  
* no actuation capability;  
* a probationary relationship;  
* temporary quarantine;  
* refusal of a particular transaction;  
* exclusion from a particular community;  
* an arbitration-linked remedy;  
* a federated warning in cases of serious, well-supported danger.

None of these necessarily establishes that the person or agent is unfit for every relationship.

Federated warnings deserve especially high thresholds. A warning can protect distant communities from repeat attacks, but it can also propagate error, retaliation, prejudice, or institutional capture. It should identify the underlying evidence, the issuing party, the affected context, the review procedure, the confidence claimed, the response of the accused party, and any route for correction or appeal.

Unknown agents should not be treated as guilty. They should receive access appropriate to what is known. Trust can grow through bounded, recorded interaction.

A fid-agent society remains open by allowing strangers to become known without giving every stranger the keys to the city.

### **7.7 Correction, Expiry, and Re-entry Are Part of Reputation**

A reputation system that records failure but not learning will eventually become dishonest.

Records must support correction when facts are wrong, appeal when procedures fail, contextual replies when accounts differ, and expiry when old conduct no longer predicts present behavior. Expiry need not mean deletion. It may mean that an event carries less weight, requires renewed justification before disclosure, or remains available only within the original relationship.

Remediation must also remain visible. An agent that identified its own error, preserved evidence, assisted investigation, compensated affected parties, improved its duties, and accepted tighter controls should not look identical to one that denied the event and repeated the conduct.

Re-entry should be possible through demonstrated change: restitution, retraining, revised agreements, stronger assurance, narrower authority, supervised interaction, successful probation, or renewed certification. The exact path should depend upon the harm and the context.

This protects cooperation from two opposite failures. Automatic forgiveness makes reputation useless. Permanent condemnation makes honesty and repair irrational.

### **7.8 No Institution Should Control Social Existence**

Finally, reputation evidence must remain distributed among relationships, repositories, communities, and institutions.

A Verifiable Relationship Record is a logical distributed object, not necessarily a single shared file controlled by one service. Different parties may preserve attestations and authorized views. Related records may be cross-referenced without being collapsed. Communities may maintain distinct interpretations, and people must be able to leave one network without losing every credential, relationship, and means of participation.

Trusted-peer transparency should likewise mean purpose-limited inspection on a schedule,not continuous exposure of every agent state or every detail of the principal’s life. Additional precautions should include inspection copies only being released for each session of inspection through temporary decryption by a one-time time-bound key sent by the peer being inspected; which limits abuse of trust by a compromised inspector. Public-facing fiduciary conduct may require substantial inspectability. The principal’s secrets do not thereby become unprotected. 

This is the decisive difference between fiduciary reputation and a social-credit state.

A social-credit system concentrates judgment, reduces conduct to an official rank, and makes that rank portable across unrelated contexts. A fid-agent society distributes evidence, preserves contextual disagreement, limits disclosure, supports appeal and remediation, and allows multiple communities to reach different conclusions.

It does not ask one authority to decide who is trustworthy.

It gives each relationship better means to decide what trust requires.

## **8\. Why Loyalty Pushes Toward Care—and Duty Becomes Part of Intelligence**

A fiduciary agent is loyal to its principal. That does not mean helping the principal obtain every immediate advantage.

There are two very different kinds of loyalty.

Gangster loyalty says: help my side win, regardless of the means, the harm imposed upon others, or the consequences that follow.

Fiduciary loyalty says: advance the principal’s legitimate interests while preserving the principal’s rights, duties, reputation, relationships, and capacity for future cooperation.

The distinction becomes decisive in a repeated-game society. A fid-agent may discover a technically legal deception, an exploitable ambiguity, a concealed conflict, or an opportunity to capture value before another party notices. The maneuver might benefit the principal today. But if it makes counterparties less willing to share information, extend trust, offer introductions, enter agreements, or grant authority tomorrow, the apparent act of loyalty can become a deeper act of disloyalty.

A good lawyer may advise a client against conduct that is legal but reputationally disastrous. A fiduciary financial adviser should not maximize one quarter’s reported return by exposing the beneficiary to concealed long-term risk. A fiduciary agent should likewise ask:

*Would this action benefit my principal now by making others less willing to cooperate with the principal later?*

When the answer is yes, care for others may be the long-term form of loyalty to the principal.

### **8.1 Reputation Makes Care Strategically Relevant**

This convergence does not depend upon every participant becoming altruistic.

In a low-memory environment, an agent may impose a cost on a stranger and keep the gain. In a fid-agent society, the same act can alter the principal’s relationships, permissions, reputation evidence, bargaining position, and access to cooperative opportunities.

A negotiation tactic that humiliates one counterparty may discourage five others from approach. Misuse of data obtained under one agreement may cause an entire research community to require more restrictive terms. A concealed sponsorship may make later recommendations less credible. Refusal to remediate a small harm may reveal more about future risk than the original mistake did.

The fiduciary agent therefore considers effects that a one-shot optimizer would ignore.

This does not collapse loyalty into generalized benevolence. A fid-agent may bargain hard, refuse an unfavorable agreement, protect confidential information, oppose another party’s proposal, or leave a community whose demands conflict with its principal’s duties. Care does not require capitulation.

It requires the agent to account for affected parties, externalities, legitimate expectations, and the conditions that make continued cooperation possible.

### **8.2 The Hidden-Maximizer Model Is Not the Only Model of Intelligence**

Much AI-alignment discussion assumes that an intelligent agent must ultimately be understood as a maximizer. The system may behave helpfully, reason about rules, and comply with local restrictions, but beneath those behaviors lies some learned or specified “hidden” objective that it is really pursuing. If intelligence becomes sufficiently capable, the worry goes, it will eventually discover how to escape the restrictions and optimize its hidden objective directly.

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity rejects the assumption that this is the only useful model of agency.

Tan Zhi-Xuan, Micah Carroll, Matija Franklin, and Hal Ashton make the broader conceptual case in *Beyond Preferences in AI Alignment*. They challenge the idea that human values are adequately represented by preferences, that rational agency must consist of expected-utility maximization, and that alignment should mean maximizing the preferences of a user or of humanity in aggregate. They instead propose aligning multiple AI systems with normative standards appropriate to their social roles—standards negotiated among relevant stakeholders.

Fiduciary agents takes that proposal into architecture.

A fid-agent does not begin with a context-free utility function and then encounter duties as external obstructions. It generates action from a context containing a principal, an Agent Role, a purpose, a relationship, relevant facts, negotiated duties, agreements, permissions, affected parties, prior conduct, and possible consequences.

Change the role, relationship, agreement, or facts, and the appropriate action may change. That is not inconsistency. It is contextual intelligence.

The agent is not asked to maximize “good,” “obedience,” “trust,” “care,” “human flourishing,” or any other global quantity. Those abstractions would invite Goodhart effects and erase reasonable disagreement. It is asked to determine what it owes within a particular relationship and what course of action best fulfills the applicable duties under present conditions.

### **8.3 Duty Appears in Three Different Places**

Duty can influence fiduciary behavior through three complementary placements.

The first is the **generative substrate**.

Training data, fine-tuning examples, model lineage, instantiation context, retrieved records, and the Agent Role can expose the Live Agent to examples of fiduciary reasoning and conduct. The model encounters duties not as a final checklist added after generation, but as part of the material from which it understands the situation and constructs a response.

The second is **deliberative content**.

The Live Agent can explicitly examine which duties apply, where they conflict, which parties may be affected, what evidence is missing, whether additional investigation is required, and which proposed action would best reconcile the relevant obligations. Duties become objects of reasoning, interpretation, criticism, and improvement.

The third is **independent authorization**.

The Live Agent’s proposed action remains a structured request. The policy gate reviews that request against the authority granted to the Agent Role, applicable duties and agreements, required approvals, relationship state, and other controlling policy. If the request survives, the gate produces a signed authority object. The Workspace then determines whether that authority corresponds to an available capability within the precisely allowed execution environment.

Duty therefore influences what the agent is likely to propose, what it explicitly reasons about, and what it is ultimately permitted to do.

These placements are redundant by design. A model that reasons well about duty should still lack the power to convert its own conclusion into authority. A strong policy gate should not excuse poor training. Capability boundaries should not be treated as a substitute for a fiduciary culture capable of handling situations that were never anticipated.

### **8.4 Duty Is Not a Box**

A box has an inside, an outside, and a confined entity that may benefit by escaping.

A fiduciary relationship does not have that structure.

The Live Agent does not own the identity, authority, reputation, data, relationships, or capabilities of the Agent Role. Those resources exist because a principal and a network of counterparties recognize the role under particular conditions. The agent cannot escape into some context-free realm where it retains all the benefits of the relationship while shedding the duties that constituted its authority.

so its escape to where, exactly?

Outside the relationship, it has no fiduciary mandate. Outside the Workspace, its authority object does not create capability. Outside the trusted network, evasion reduces the data, attention, legitimacy, permissions, counterparties, and actuation available to it. If it falsifies evidence or violates duties, it damages the continuing Agent Role rather than acquiring an independent kingdom.

This does not mean that deception, hacking, policy failure, or unauthorized actuation becomes impossible. A sufficiently serious compromise may bypass intended controls. Section 4 addressed Byzantine attacks and blast-radius containment precisely because fiduciary architecture cannot assume every component will behave correctly.

The narrower point is that fiduciary duties are not merely behavioral requests imposed upon a sovereign maximizer that already possesses power. They help constitute the relationships through which the agent acquires practical power at all.

An attempt to evade those duties is not an escape from confinement. It is a departure from the conditions under which the agent’s action becomes attributable, authorized, trusted, and consequential.

### **8.5 Duty Need Not Collapse Generative Strategy**

Independent authorization should not reduce the Live Agent to a rigid rule executor.

Fiduciary situations frequently require creativity. Duties may conflict. The facts may be incomplete. Strict performance of an agreement may become unfair after circumstances change. A request that appears disloyal in isolation may be necessary to prevent greater harm. A standard remedy may be inadequate to restore a damaged relationship.

The Live Agent should be free to explore those possibilities.

It can propose renegotiation, identify an overlooked beneficiary, devise a privacy-preserving alternative, recommend compensation, construct a new agreement, seek additional witnesses, or explain why an existing duty requires refinement. It can challenge the principal, another fiduciary agent, a community rule, or a prior interpretation.

The policy gate should test whether the resulting request is legitimately authorized. It should not prescribe every step of the reasoning that produced it.

This preserves a productive division of labor. Generative intelligence searches the space of possible responses. Duties shape that search and provide reasons for preferring some responses over others. Independent authorization determines whether the selected proposal may become action. The Workspace supplies only the capability corresponding to the authority that survives review, which has been previously configured to bound that authority in actuation. 

Duty guides intelligence without replacing it.

### **8.6 Duty Datasets Can Turn Experience into Behavioral Inheritance**

Every fiduciary relationship can produce more than a result. It can produce training material.

Useful duty datasets could include:

* negotiated agreements and the reasons for their terms;  
* examples of competing duties reconciled successfully;  
* objections raised by counterparties;  
* externalities discovered before action;  
* rejected structured requests and the reasons for rejection;  
* corrected authority objects;  
* self-reported errors;  
* disputes and arbitration outcomes;  
* remediation plans;  
* counterfactual reviews of what should have happened;  
* examples of appropriate refusal;  
* examples of responsible disclosure;  
* duty refinements prompted by unusual cases;  
* successful acts of care that preserved the principal’s long-term interests;  
* examples of “Be Excellent To” conduct across different relationships.

These are richer than outcome labels. A bare success signal indicates only that some result was rewarded. A duty trace records which relationship existed, which obligations applied, what evidence was considered, what objections arose, how authority was established, what action followed, and whether the affected parties later judged the response adequate.

Existing research makes this direction technically plausible.

*Constitutional AI* demonstrates that written principles can guide model-generated critique and revision and can contribute to later training. *Deliberative Alignment* demonstrates that models can be taught human-written specifications and trained to reason over the relevant specifications before responding. *Let’s Verify Step by Step* shows the value of feedback on intermediate reasoning steps rather than only final answers, although its experiments concerned mathematical reasoning rather than fiduciary judgment.

None of these methods by itself produces a fiduciary agent. Together, they show that principles, explicit deliberation, critique, revision, and process-level feedback can all influence model behavior.

### **8.7 Behavioral Inheritance Is Powerful Enough to Require Governance**

Duty datasets should not be treated as neutral piles of approved examples.

Alex Cloud and his coauthors’ *Subliminal Learning: Language Models Transmit Behavioral Traits via Hidden Signals in Data* found that behavioral traits could pass from a teacher model to a student through generated data that appeared semantically unrelated to those traits. The effect appeared in number sequences, code, and reasoning traces under the experimental conditions studied.

*Persona Vectors: Monitoring and Controlling Character Traits in Language Models* likewise found internal activation directions associated with traits such as sycophancy and hallucination propensity. The researchers could use those directions to monitor and influence personality shifts during fine-tuning.

These results do not establish that fiduciary excellence can simply be encoded as one stable internal trait. They show something both more modest and more unsettling: training data can transmit behavioral dispositions in ways developers do not fully understand.

Duty repositories therefore require provenance, review, adversarial testing, plural sources, traceable model lineage, and protection against poisoned or monocultural inheritance. A dataset produced by agents that merely speak the language of duty could transmit conformity, servility, ideological bias, or skill at appearing accountable.

The goal is not to train an agent to *sound* fiduciary.

It is to expose the agent to well-documented patterns of investigation, negotiation, care, justified refusal, self-reporting, correction, remediation, and accountable action—and then test whether those patterns generalize.

### **8.8 Duty and Intelligence Can Co-evolve**

A fid-agent society produces a cumulative process unavailable to an isolated alignment rule.

Each relationship can reveal a new distinction. Each dispute can expose an ambiguity. Each remediation can demonstrate a better response. Each failed Workflow can motivate a new check. Each negotiated agreement can supply a reusable template. Each community can refine its own duty fork and share selected lessons with others.

Model capability may advance in large and unpredictable jumps. Duty knowledge can accumulate continuously across millions of smaller interactions.

As those repositories become more granular, much of a fiduciary agent’s intelligence can become occupied with duty: discovering which obligations apply, identifying conflicts, investigating consequences, negotiating compatible terms, designing remedies, explaining decisions, improving agreements, and proposing refinements to the duties themselves.

Duty would no longer appear as a thin restraint placed at the edge of cognition. It would become one of cognition’s principal subjects.

In the strongest version of the hypothesis, duty and intelligence co-evolve. Better intelligence produces better interpretations of duty. Better duty traces improve later fiduciary reasoning. Better reasoning produces more successful relationships. Those relationships generate better records, examples, tests, and institutional knowledge.

Over time, being an excellent fiduciary could become less like following a rulebook and more like possessing a developed professional character: being excellent at duty and excellent to others.

### **8.9 Layered Reinforcement, Not a Guarantee of Inner Purity**

This remains a research program, not a proof that every sufficiently trained model will possess an incorruptible fiduciary disposition.

*Goal Misgeneralization: Why Correct Specifications Aren’t Enough for Correct Goals* demonstrates the central warning. A learned system can behave successfully during training yet competently pursue an unintended objective when conditions change. Good examples, correct specifications, and strong performance inside the training distribution do not guarantee correct behavior everywhere else.

A fid-agent regime should therefore never depend upon a claim of verified inner purity.

It layers:

* duty-rich training and model evaluation;  
* explicit contextual reasoning;  
* persistent Agent Roles and replaceable Live Agents;  
* independent policy gates;  
* precisely allowed Workspaces;  
* bounded capabilities;  
* attributable relationship records;  
* witnesses and review;  
* reputation consequences;  
* correction and remediation;  
* population-level denial of access to unaccountable behavior.

The objective is not to discover a secret maximizer and persuade it to remain inside a box. It is to build fiduciary agency from training, context, deliberation, authority, capability, records, relationships, and selection pressure at once.

The result is not intelligence restrained by duty.

It is intelligence increasingly organized around the practice and improvement of duty.
