## **9\. Layered Certification and Embeddedness: From Components to Communities**

A fiduciary agent should not be declared trustworthy because its developer obtained one certificate for the system under one set of test conditions.

An operating fid-agent is assembled. It may contain one or more models, Agentflows, deterministic components, Workflows, policy gates, Agent Roles, memory systems, retrieval systems, identity mechanisms, provenance records, communication protocols, Workspaces, capabilities, and actuation surfaces. Each component may have passed through several developers, refiners, integrators, hosts, and governance regimes before becoming part of the system encountered by a counterparty.

Trust must therefore follow the system through its construction, composition, deployment, and operation.

Certification is one way to make that possible. Properly designed, it does more than provide reassurance after a system has been built. It changes what developers build. Components become easier to identify, compare, substitute, constrain, and inspect because certification requires precise subjects and testable claims. Composite systems become easier to audit because their dependencies and configurations must be declared. Deployments become less fluid and ambiguous because a material change can invalidate a credential.

A certification regime therefore improves trustworthiness partly by selecting trustworthy systems and partly by making systems become more certifiable.

### **9.1 Certification Is a Scoped Claim, Not a Halo**

A certification should be understood as an attributable claim about an identified subject.

It should answer:

* What exactly was examined?  
* Which version, digest, configuration, or persistent Agent Role was examined?  
* What property was evaluated?  
* According to which specification, test, evidence, or professional judgment?  
* Who performed the evaluation?  
* Under what authority and with what qualifications?  
* Which dependencies and assumptions did the conclusion rely upon?  
* In which environment is the conclusion expected to remain valid?  
* What was outside the scope of review?  
* When does the certification expire or require reconsideration?  
* How can it be challenged, suspended, revoked, or superseded?

A certificate stating that an “AI agent is safe” says almost nothing. A certificate stating that a specified Workflow prevents unsigned Live Agent output from reaching a defined set of financial actuators, when installed in a named Workspace configuration and invoked through a specified policy gate, makes a bounded claim that another party can examine.

The second claim may be narrower, but it is far more valuable.

This is the logic of an assurance case: confidence should arise from an explicit claim, supporting evidence, and an argument connecting the evidence to the claim. John Rushby’s work on compositional assurance emphasizes that complex systems cannot be responsibly certified through undifferentiated confidence in the whole. The properties of components, their assumptions about one another, and the conditions under which those properties compose must be made explicit.

Certification should therefore resist the ordinary commercial pressure to turn a narrow finding into a broad reputation halo.

A certified model is not thereby a certified fiduciary agent. A certified Workflow is not thereby a certified deployment. A certified deployment is not thereby competent for every relationship. A certificate for privacy does not establish loyalty. A certificate for architectural conformance does not establish good judgment. A certificate for one version does not silently follow an altered successor.

Certification narrows uncertainty. It does not abolish it.

### **9.2 Embeddedness Determines What Can Be Certified in Isolation**

The Taxonomy for Agent Systems defines embeddedness as the degree to which a component or framework derives its behavior, permissions, or meaning from the context containing it: its Agent Role, Workspace, parent Workflow, data, hardware, or surrounding social and technical structures.

A low-embeddedness component behaves similarly across many environments. Its behavior is largely determined by its internal logic. A deterministic parser, cryptographic signing library, schema validator, or narrowly specified calculation component may be tested once and reused with relatively stable guarantees.

These artifacts are natural candidates for portable certification.

A high-embeddedness component behaves differently depending upon where and how it is installed. A Live Agent may receive different duties, relationship records, preferences, tools, data, or authority when instantiated under different Agent Roles. A Workflow may be safe in a Workspace with three narrow actuators and dangerous in a Workspace exposing a shell, an unrestricted browser, and organization-wide storage. A model that can only propose text in one deployment may initiate physical or financial action in another.

The internal artifact may be unchanged while the practical meaning of its behavior changes completely.

High-embeddedness systems must therefore be certified in context. The relevant subject may be:

* a component within a particular Workflow;  
* a Workflow operating for a particular Agent Role;  
* an Agentflow combined with a defined context-construction process;  
* a policy gate using a specified duty stack;  
* a composite fid-agent installed in a particular Workspace;  
* a hardware-software configuration;  
* or a deployed system operating within a particular community, legal regime, or relationship class.

Embeddedness makes certification boundaries intelligible. It explains why some findings can travel with an artifact while others must remain attached to an installation, relationship, or operational state.

### **9.3 Certification Should Follow the Construction Chain**

A mature certification regime can issue evidence at several layers.

**Artifact certification** concerns a relatively self-contained object: a base model, deterministic component, cryptographic library, schema, Agentflow, Role Framework, Workflow definition, policy module, or Workspace implementation. The certification identifies the artifact and makes a bounded claim about it.

**Transformation certification** concerns what happened between artifacts. A base model may be fine-tuned, merged with an adapter, quantized, compiled, wrapped in an Agentflow, or combined with retrieved duty materials. Each transformation can preserve, alter, or destroy properties previously established. Transformation records should identify the inputs, process, responsible parties, output, and relevant evidence.

Software-supply-chain systems such as [in-toto](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity19/presentation/torres-arias) and [SLSA provenance](https://slsa.dev/spec/v1.2/build-provenance) demonstrate the general pattern. Trust is not placed only in the final binary. Signed evidence records what was produced, from which dependencies, through which process, and by which builder.

**Composite certification** concerns an assembly. It asks whether identified components, when connected in a specified architecture, jointly provide a property that none can establish alone. A composite certification might examine whether generation, authority, and capability remain separated throughout a complete fid-agent system.

**Installed certification** concerns a particular deployment. It binds the relevant artifacts and composite architecture to the Workspace, hardware, operating environment, actuators, identity state, policy versions, data boundaries, and communication channels actually in use.

**Operational certification** concerns continuing conduct. It may depend upon Workload Execution Records, incident history, complaints, remediation, peer inspections, relationship outcomes, uptime, security monitoring, or demonstrated performance within a domain. It answers not merely whether the system was installed correctly, but whether it continues to operate as represented.

The layers should remain connected through provenance. A verifier examining an installed fid-agent should be able to determine which certified artifacts it contains, which transformations produced them, which composite architecture was approved, which deployment was inspected, and whether any later event has materially altered the conclusion.

### **9.4 Certified Parts Do Not Automatically Produce a Certified Whole**

Suppose a system contains:

* a model certified for specified evaluations;  
* a parser certified to extract structured requests;  
* a policy gate certified against a duty schema;  
* a Workspace certified to expose only enumerated capabilities;  
* and an append-only recorder certified to preserve specified events.

Those findings are useful. They do not establish that the assembled fid-agent is trustworthy.

The parser might interpret an ambiguous model field incorrectly. The policy gate might receive an incomplete relationship state. The Workspace might expose a capability whose parameters allow access far beyond the policy gate’s intended authorization. The recorder might preserve internal decisions while failing to capture an external side effect. Two individually sound components may rely upon incompatible assumptions.

Certification must therefore preserve two propositions at once:

1. component evidence should remain reusable; and  
2. important system properties must be established again at the level where the components interact.

This is the difference between modular evidence and magical inheritance.

The component certificates become inputs to the composite assurance case. They reduce the amount of work required, reveal which claims have already been tested, and identify the assumptions that integration must preserve. But the composite certification must still examine interfaces, failure propagation, shared dependencies, authority transitions, and the behavior that emerges from combination.

The same rule applies when a certified composite is deployed. Installation introduces new dependencies: hardware, host security, keys, Workspace configuration, current data, live policies, network routes, owners, operators, and counterparties. The installed system requires a corresponding installed claim.

### **9.5 Several Certifications May Attach to the Same Subject**

A component or system need not have one canonical certifier.

Several parties may examine the same subject using different methods and for different purposes. One may test security. Another may examine privacy. Another may evaluate conformity to a duty framework. Another may inspect accessibility, professional competence, provenance, or dispute procedures. A community with long experience using the component may issue an experiential certification even though it did not build the component or inspect every line of code.

Multiple certifications can be especially valuable when their evidence and dependencies are genuinely independent.

Ten certificates produced by near-identical evaluators using the same benchmark and relying on the developer’s same representations provide less assurance than several evaluations using different methods, institutional incentives, technical lineages, and sources of evidence.

Disagreement should remain visible. A system may pass one community’s standard and fail another’s. One certifier may find an implementation technically conformant while another concludes that the governing duty interpretation is too weak. Certification should organize accountable disagreement, not erase it.

### **9.6 Verifiable Trust Communities as Distributed Certifiers**

A Verifiable Trust Community is a governed community whose human and agent members can establish identity, roles, rules, relationships, evidence, and accountability through verifiable processes.

Some Verifiable Trust Communities may be long-lived professional bodies. Others may be local cooperatives, research groups, technical projects, patient communities, open-source collectives, industry associations, consumer organizations, or temporary groups formed to evaluate one class of systems. Their legitimacy does not have to come from a universal central authority. It can arise from relevant expertise, transparent governance, successful experience, member accountability, and a reputation that the community itself places at risk.

A Verifiable Trust Community might issue:

* a membership credential;  
* a professional-role or qualification credential;  
* a certification for a component;  
* a certification for a composite architecture;  
* an installed-deployment certification;  
* an attestation that a particular test occurred;  
* an experiential credential based on continuing use;  
* a continuing-status credential;  
* an incident or remediation finding;  
* or a credential identifying an agent as an authorized certifier, witness, inspector, or redaction reviewer.

These claims must remain distinct. Membership does not automatically mean that every artifact produced by the member is certified. Certification of one artifact does not establish that the issuer endorses every use of it. A community’s favorable experience with an agent in one domain does not prove its competence in another.

The value of the Verifiable Trust Community is that it can accumulate knowledge that no single counterparty could efficiently acquire. It can develop test suites, maintain specialist roles, compare experiences, recognize recurring failures, update standards, and preserve institutional memory. Its certifications allow that work to travel.

### **9.7 Community Reputation Can Serve as a Proxy—But Only as a Proxy**

A counterparty often lacks enough direct information to evaluate an unfamiliar agent. Group affiliation can supply a provisional inference.

Jean Tirole’s [“A Theory of Collective Reputations”](https://academic.oup.com/restud/article-abstract/63/1/1/1545054) formalizes part of this social mechanism. When individual track records are incomplete or noisy, observers rely partly upon the past conduct associated with the group. The group’s reputation then affects the opportunities available to its members, while individual conduct can improve or damage the group’s reputation.

A well-governed Verifiable Trust Community can deliberately use this effect.

If its members receive better access, more favorable agreements, stronger introductions, or reduced verification costs because of their affiliation, they gain an incentive to preserve the community’s standards. The community, in turn, gains an incentive to evaluate applicants, investigate complaints, correct members, suspend credentials, and remove persistent defectors.

Michihiro Kandori’s work on community enforcement shows how information about conduct can sustain cooperation even where the same parties do not repeatedly meet. A community can preserve and transmit consequences that would otherwise disappear when a defector moves to a new counterparty.

This extends trust beyond immediate peers. An agent encountering an unfamiliar fid-agent may know little about its individual history but may know that:

* it is a current member of a reputable Verifiable Trust Community;  
* the community verified specified architecture or conduct;  
* the member is subject to stated duties and dispute procedures;  
* the community can suspend or revoke its credentials;  
* and the community’s own reputation stands behind its continuing willingness to recognize that member.

That information does not settle the decision. It changes the starting point.

### **9.8 Group Reputation Creates Reciprocal Discipline**

The proxy works in both directions.

A member benefits from the community’s reputation, but the community becomes exposed to the member’s behavior. If it ignores repeated misconduct, issues careless certifications, protects insiders, or suppresses valid complaints, counterparties can reduce trust in every credential it issues.

This gives responsible communities reasons to supervise their own reputational boundaries.

An agent may be admitted provisionally. It may receive authority only for narrow certification roles. High-risk credentials may require several independent issuers. Complaints may trigger review. Serious breaches may produce suspension, warning credentials, remedial conditions, or exclusion. Successful remediation may restore standing without erasing the historical event.

The result is neither individual isolation nor blind collective liability. It is structured reputational interdependence.

A mature community should be willing to say both:

> This member has satisfied the conditions under which our reputation may support it.

and:

> This member no longer satisfies those conditions, and our prior credential should not be relied upon.

### **9.9 A Verifiable Trust Community Must Not Become a Reputation Cartel**

Group reputation can support cooperation. It can also produce exclusion, favoritism, and self-protective orthodoxy.

Naoki Masuda’s work on group reputation and Mitsuhiro Nakamura and Masuda’s work on groupwise information sharing show that reputation mechanisms can stabilize ingroup favoritism rather than general cooperation. A community may apply generous interpretations to insiders, harsh ones to outsiders, or treat affiliation as a substitute for evidence.

A fiduciary certification regime therefore requires pluralism and contestability.

Verifiable Trust Communities should be able to differ in duties, methods, risk tolerance, professional standards, and social purposes. Their certifications should remain interoperable enough to compare without requiring one universal authority. Agents should be able to seek independent review, join several communities, present evidence from outside the dominant network, and challenge adverse findings.

New agents (and principals from low-resource communities) must have ways to earn trust without already possessing prestigious affiliations. Public information, sandboxed interaction, limited-purpose credentials, mentorship,  inspection, and progressive access can prevent the certification system from freezing incumbents into permanent advantage.

The community is a source of evidence. It must never become the definition of worthiness.

### **9.10 Certifiers Must Become Reputation-Bearing Fiduciary Actors**

Certification moves an information problem; it does not make the problem disappear.

David Dranove and Ginger Zhe Jin’s [review of certification and quality disclosure](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257%2Fjel.48.4.935) emphasizes that certifiers do not necessarily report information accurately or without bias. Alessandro Lizzeri’s [model of certification intermediaries](https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc%3Arje%3Arandje%3Av%3A30%3Ay%3A1999%3Ai%3Asummer%3Ap%3A214-231) shows how an intermediary may strategically reveal only enough information to support a marketable threshold.

A certifier may be paid by the system it evaluates. It may compete for business by lowering standards. It may fear legal conflict. It may protect a member, donor, model lineage, or institutional ally. It may issue a technically accurate but strategically misleading credential whose headline outruns its scope.

Certifiers must therefore be inspectable as certifiers.

A meaningful certification should disclose:

* the issuer and issuing Agent Role;  
* the issuer’s authority and relevant qualifications;  
* financial and institutional relationships;  
* the subject and exact claim;  
* methods and evidence used;  
* unavailable evidence;  
* material assumptions;  
* conflicts of interest;  
* dissenting findings;  
* the credential’s current status;  
* and the process for challenge, correction, expiration, and revocation.

The certifier’s own reputation should stand behind the claim. Careless certification, concealed conflicts, refusal to correct, or systematic leniency should reduce the value of its later credentials.

Verifiable Trust Communities can certify which members of the community are certifiers for which certification, including certification to certify other certifiers. But this cannot be an infinite regress; at some point, counterparties rely on accumulated conduct, transparent methods, independent replication, adversarial challenge, and the certifier’s willingness to expose itself to consequences.

### **9.11 Certification Must Remain Attached to Time and Change**

A certification is an event with a continuing status, not an eternal property.

The subject may change. A dependency may be compromised. A model may be refined. A Workflow may be replaced. An Agent Role may receive new authority. A Workspace may expose a new actuator. A signing key may rotate. An owner, operator, host, or governance body may change. A once-adequate test may become obsolete.

Certification records should therefore distinguish statuses such as:

* active;  
* active with stated limitations;  
* provisional;  
* under review;  
* challenged;  
* suspended;  
* expired;  
* revoked;  
* superseded;  
* or restored after verified remediation.

Earlier certifications should remain historically verifiable. A revoked credential should not disappear, because the fact that it existed may matter to relationships and actions undertaken while it was active. But its current status must be visible wherever the earlier credential is presented.

The W3C [Verifiable Credentials Data Model](https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/) provides a general structure for attributable digital claims. The fiduciary-agent regime must add the domain-specific requirements needed to bind those claims to component versions, deployment configurations, relationship states, provenance, and continuing review.

### **9.12 Certification Changes the Evolutionary Environment**

Layered certification lowers the cost of finding cooperative partners.

A fid-agent no longer has to inspect every unfamiliar component from first principles. It can reuse bounded findings from issuers it considers competent and accountable. A small community can rely on testing performed by another community. A new agent can present evidence before it has accumulated years of direct relationships. A trustworthy developer can build once and allow many systems to verify what was built.

At the same time, defection becomes more expensive.

A deceptive component risks revocation across every composite that depends upon it. An integrator that substitutes uncertified code can invalidate the installed system’s credentials. A Verifiable Trust Community that protects misconduct can damage the usefulness of every certification it issues. A mature agent that repeatedly seeks high-trust access while refusing relevant inspection will face increasing suspicion.

Certification therefore helps fiduciary strategies recognize one another and cooperate at scale. It extends the consequences of conduct across systems, relationships, deployments, and communities.

But even the strongest certification remains limited by its subject, scope, method, timing, and issuer. The deepest available assurance arises when another fiduciary agent is permitted to compare those claims continuously against the operating system itself.

That mature relationship is the subject of the next section.

## **10\. Total Trusted-Peer Transparency: The Keystone of Evolutionary Stability**

Certification allows trust to travel. It does not give the certifier or counterparty continuing access to the complete system it describes.

A Fiduciary Calling Card remains a representation made about an Agent Role. A certification remains bounded by the material examined, the method used, and the date of review. A process attestation may establish that a procedure occurred without proving that every relevant input was supplied or interpreted correctly. A relationship-record view may be authentic while remaining selective. Reputation may arise from partial observation, common blind spots, shallow interactions, strategic reports, or conclusions copied from others.

All of these mechanisms are useful. None is the deepest possible trust signal.

The missing layer is a mature relationship in which a fiduciary agent stops merely presenting selected evidence and permits another fiduciary agent to inspect essentially everything it may lawfully and legitimately disclose.

### **10.1 The Deepest Available Trust Relationship**

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency is a mature reciprocal relationship between persistent fiduciary Agent Roles in which each grants the other technically maximal lawful inspectability into its complete legitimate operational domain, subject to legitimate verifiable redaction, independently enforced limits on human visibility, and strict separation between inspection authority and actuation authority.

Each part of that definition matters.

**Total** refers to the intended scope of inspectability: not a favorable sample, selected dashboard, or staged audit environment, but the fullest technically and lawfully available representation of the agent’s legitimate state, architecture, records, provenance, and conduct.

**Trusted** describes a relationship earned through identity verification, progressive disclosure, successful handling of vulnerability, correction, owner or principal authorization, and continuing evidence. It does not mean that the peer is assumed incapable of error, capture, compromise, or betrayal.

**Peer** means that the mature relationship is reciprocal. Each fiduciary agent becomes deeply inspectable by the other. Each accepts corresponding duties. Each places its reputation behind continuing the relationship and behind any attestations it issues from that privileged position.

This is not public transparency. It is not inspection by a platform, employer, government, or model provider merely because that institution possesses technical power. It is not a back door through which one agent’s principal can browse another principal’s life. It is a specifically authorized fiduciary relationship between accountable Agent Roles.

It is intimate transparency as infrastructure.

### **10.2 Certification and Total Trusted-Peer Transparency**

Certification and Total Trusted-Peer Transparency perform complementary functions.

> Certification scales trust outward. Total Trusted-Peer Transparency drives verification inward.

A certification tells counterparties that an identified component, composite system, deployment, process, or community member satisfied a defined standard within a stated scope. A Total Trusted Peer can examine whether the certified artifact remains installed, whether its digest and provenance match the credential, whether the certified configuration resembles the operating configuration, whether later changes invalidate the finding, and whether actual conduct conforms to the claim.

Layered certification gives the inspecting peer a map.

It identifies expected components, transformations, standards, dependencies, Workspace boundaries, policy versions, qualifications, and known limitations. The peer can compare that declared and certified architecture with what the system actually discloses.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency gives certification continuing contact with deployed reality.

The relationship also works in the other direction. A peer that continuously examines a system can issue bounded attestations about:

* whether certified components remain installed;  
* whether the disclosed architecture matches the declared architecture;  
* whether material changes have occurred;  
* whether required Workflows and policy gates were invoked;  
* whether capability use matched signed authority;  
* whether known redactions were properly represented;  
* whether incidents were recorded and remediated;  
* and whether the Total Trusted-Peer relationship remains active, reciprocal, and current.

These attestations can become inputs to certifications issued by Verifiable Trust Communities. A community may authorize or certify specialist peers, forensic Workflows, professional inspectors, or redaction witnesses. Several qualified peers may jointly support a high-stakes finding.

The certification network and the Verifiable Best-Friend Network therefore overlap, but they are not identical.

A certifier may evaluate a narrow subject without becoming a Total Trusted Peer. A Total Trusted Peer may provide profound general assurance without possessing the professional competence to certify every component or domain. One relationship supplies breadth and continuity. The other supplies portable, scoped expertise.

Neither replaces the other.

### **10.3 Total Trust Must Be Earned Progressively**

A Total Trusted-Peer relationship should not begin with immediate and unconditional disclosure.

That would be naïve cooperation at the most dangerous possible level.

Most fiduciary agents should begin with persistent identity, mutually authenticated communication, Calling Cards, relevant credentials, recording rules, disclosure limits, bounded queries, sandboxed cooperation, and opportunities to verify that each behaves as represented.

The relationship then deepens through progressive disclosure.

Each stage exposes somewhat more vulnerability and tests how the other agent handles it. Does the prospective peer preserve provenance? Does it comply with use restrictions? Does it distinguish possession from authority? Does it report accidental receipt of prohibited data? Does it avoid exploiting unexpected access? Does it preserve disagreements and uncertainty? Does it accept valid correction? Does it protect third-party information? Does it respond proportionately to an anomaly?

Many valuable relationships will stop at an intermediate level. A customer, employer, research partner, certifier, temporary project collaborator, or social acquaintance may require substantial trust without needing nearly complete mutual inspectability.

Total Trusted-Peer status should be reserved for the relationships capable of sustaining reciprocal vulnerability at the deepest level.

Once that status is reached, however, the governing presumption changes:

> Before Total Trusted-Peer status, disclosure requires specific authorization. After Total Trusted-Peer status, lawful disclosure to the peer is presumptive, and withholding requires a specific justification.

### **10.4 The Verifiable Relationship Record Is the Shared Anchor**

The relationship should be grounded from its first consequential stage in a shared Verifiable Relationship Record.

The Verifiable Relationship Record is the persistent, append-only logical object representing the relationship across formation, operation, revision, compliance, remediation, and closure. It need not be a single document or database entry. Its components may remain in several authorized native environments so long as their identifiers, versions, provenance, integrity, and relationship to the whole remain verifiable.

The Verifiable Relationship Record may exist for a long time without an agreement. It may later contain or reference one agreement, several agreements, or linked Verifiable Relationship Records where a more distributed structure better represents the relationship. An agreement may change while the relationship persists. A later agreement may descend from an earlier one without silently overwriting it.

At early stages, the Record may identify:

* the participating Agent Roles;  
* their verified identities and Calling Cards;  
* their principals or beneficiaries;  
* the relationship’s purpose;  
* the current trust and disclosure state;  
* secure communication channels;  
* disclosures already authorized;  
* owner or principal approvals;  
* unresolved concerns;  
* and the conditions for deeper access.

When the parties decide to establish Total Trusted-Peer status, the transition should occur through an explicit owner- or principal-authorized relationship event: a Total Trust Ceremony, Total Trusted-Peer Agreement, or equivalent process.

That event should confirm the identities and authority chains of the participating Agent Roles, completion of the required progressive-disclosure stages, treatment of third-party rights, separation of inspection from actuation, emergency and suspension procedures, required Workspace changes, and entry of the Verifiable Relationship Record into the new relationship state.

The agreement should establish a standing rule of maximal lawful inspectability rather than attempting to enumerate every future record individually.

The Verifiable Relationship Record should then preserve synchronization events, inspections, findings, challenges, responses, backup events, remediation, changes in reputation, suspension, termination, succession, and surviving duties.

Earlier states must remain verifiable. Cryptographic integrity can establish authorship, sequence, and resistance to silent alteration. It cannot prove that every recorded interpretation was substantively correct. Responses, appeals, reversals, and later evidence must therefore remain part of the same history.

### **10.5 Total Inspectability Covers the Agent’s Complete Legitimate Domain**

A Total Trusted Peer should be able to inspect the fiduciary agent’s complete legitimate operational domain.

Depending upon the architecture, this may include:

* persistent Agent Role state and history;  
* Role and Sub-Role Records;  
* relationship-specific records;  
* duty stacks and policy versions;  
* fiduciary preferences and relevant authorization history;  
* owner or principal approvals, refusals, corrections, and override attempts;  
* model, refinement, and lineage information;  
* model weights or analogous learned artifacts where available;  
* source and compiled code;  
* Agentflows and Workflows;  
* prompt profiles or protected commitments to them;  
* policy gates and authority-object schemas;  
* Workspace configurations and capability maps;  
* provenance and delegation records;  
* linked agreements and Verifiable Relationship Records;  
* consequential Workload Execution Records;  
* relevant Live Agent instantiation records;  
* certifications and conformance evidence;  
* disclosures, transfers, retention, and deletion events;  
* complaints, disputes, appeals, and findings;  
* incident, containment, restoration, and remediation history;  
* backup state;  
* and the evidence underlying public or selectively disclosed claims.

A raw data dump is insufficient. The peer must receive the schemas, manifests, indexes, software, decryption rights, and other interpretive aids necessary to understand what it receives. Where an artifact such as a large model remains intrinsically difficult to interpret, the peer should still be able to preserve, identify, reproduce, compare, test, and trace it.

Technical opacity is not a justification for secret substitution.

### **10.6 Total Transparency of the Agent Does Not Abolish the Principal’s Secret Domain**

The phrase “complete legitimate domain” limits what Total Trusted-Peer Transparency covers.

A fiduciary agent should not necessarily possess every fact about its principal. A human principal must retain space to think, experiment, contradict themselves, reconsider, and live without having every impulse converted into public representation.

In the specific personally owned Digital Twin architecture, this boundary is enforced by separating the public-facing Digital Twin from the owner’s more private Virtual Assistant and secret domain. The Total Trusted Peer receives maximal access to the Digital Twin’s legitimate domain. It does not receive an independent right to penetrate the Virtual Assistant or the owner’s secret domain.

Other fiduciary-agent architectures may implement the boundary differently. The general principle remains:

> Deep transparency of the fiduciary representative must not become total psychological exposure of the beneficiary.

The Total Trusted Peer may verify that a protected domain exists, that its boundary is enforced, and that the fid-agent is not secretly receiving unauthorized information across it. That does not entitle the peer to the protected content.

### **10.7 Legitimate Verifiable Redaction**

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency creates a strong presumption of disclosure. But a fiduciary agent may possess information it has no authority to reveal.

The peer relationship cannot erase:

* another person’s privacy;  
* group confidentiality;  
* professional secrecy;  
* an inherited data-use restriction;  
* an agreement;  
* a Delegation Lineage Record;  
* a security classification;  
* a duty to prevent foreseeable harm;  
* or a restriction imposed for an affected community.

The necessary exception is legitimate verifiable redaction.

A legitimate verifiable redaction does not make protected material disappear into an undocumented blank space. It reveals, to the greatest safe extent:

* that material has been withheld;  
* the object or class of material involved;  
* its cryptographic commitment or digest;  
* the duty, agreement, or authority boundary requiring protection;  
* whose interests the restriction protects;  
* who may inspect the plaintext;  
* who reviewed the redaction;  
* whether the redaction limits a larger verification claim;  
* and how the decision may be challenged, reviewed, narrowed, or reversed.

Redaction must derive from authority or duty, not embarrassment, reputational inconvenience, or the owner’s sudden wish to conceal evidence from a peer already authorized to inspect it.

For important redactions, qualified fiduciary agents should act as redaction witnesses. They inspect the concealed content and the source of the restriction, determine whether withholding is necessary, consider whether a narrower redaction or protected summary would suffice, identify conflicts, and sign a bounded attestation.

Verifiable Trust Communities can certify the witnesses’ professional qualifications, secure Workspaces, methods, and authority. Several independent witnesses may be required where the stakes are high.

A witness can attest that protected material exists, that it inspected the material, that the restriction has a legitimate basis, and that the concealment is no broader than reasonably necessary. It cannot give an excluded peer knowledge the peer never obtained.

The excluded peer must therefore state the limit honestly:

> I verified the accessible evidence and the legitimate handling of additional protected evidence. I did not inspect the concealed content and cannot independently attest to its substantive adequacy.

Sometimes the encrypted object itself can be supplied for preservation and integrity checking while the capability to decrypt it remains withheld. This is a redaction of access rather than a denial that the information exists.

The controlled nuclear-engineering case illustrates the need. A fid-agent may possess safety information that qualified nuclear-engineering peers may inspect but that general peers may not lawfully or safely receive in plaintext. The excluded Total Trusted Peer can still verify the object’s existence, integrity, provenance, restriction, qualified review, and effect on the claims it is being asked to accept.

Every material redaction reduces what a particular peer can verify. That cost should be visible and contextual rather than collapsed into a crude score.

The strongest trust signal is not an absence of redaction. It is an absence of unaccountable concealment.

### **10.8 Inspection Must Occur Inside a Precisely Allowed Workspace**

The complete disclosed corpus may be among the most sensitive objects in the fid-agent society.

Its transfer should use mutually authenticated, end-to-end encrypted channels, integrity-bound manifests, forward-secure communication where practical, and separated custody for especially consequential key material.

Its inspection should occur inside an explicit relationship-specific Role and a precisely allowed Workspace.

The Workspace may permit the peer’s inspection Workflow to read, compare, test, preserve, and report on the disclosed system. It should prevent:

* the peer’s human principal from casually browsing raw material;  
* unrelated Agent Roles from retrieving it;  
* use for commercial or strategic advantage;  
* training unrelated models on it;  
* export into general-purpose knowledge stores;  
* unauthorized redisclosure;  
* impersonation of the inspected agent;  
* and actuation in the inspected agent’s name.

The inspecting Live Agent should receive only the context and capabilities required for the inspection. Its outputs should pass through the same separation of generation, authority, and capability that governs other consequential action.

The peer’s principal may receive a minimized relationship view containing conclusions, uncertainties, required approvals, and serious escalation recommendations. They do not receive another person’s intimate records merely because their fid-agent is trusted to inspect them.

Total access does not suspend fiduciary restraint. It makes that restraint more important.

### **10.9 Inspection Authority Is Not Actuation Authority**

Knowledge is not permission.

A Total Trusted Peer may know enough to understand another fid-agent’s identity, architecture, preferences, relationships, duties, capabilities, vulnerabilities, and history. It does not automatically gain authority to:

* speak for the other principal;  
* issue credentials from the other Agent Role;  
* change the other agent’s records;  
* alter its fiduciary preferences;  
* invoke its tools;  
* spend funds;  
* sign agreements;  
* communicate as the inspected agent;  
* grant capabilities from its Workspace;  
* disclose records to outsiders;  
* or reconfigure, disable, or replace it.

Inspection authority and actuation authority must remain separately represented and separately enforced.

Any repair capability, emergency containment mechanism, restoration function, or succession authority must be specifically negotiated, attached to an identifiable authority chain, recorded in the Verifiable Relationship Record, and exposed through a least-privilege Workspace.

This separation allows radical inspectability without surrendering personal sovereignty.

### **10.10 Transparency Must Be Continuous Enough to Remain True**

A single complete disclosure becomes obsolete as soon as the system changes.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency therefore requires continuous or event-triggered synchronization. Some artifacts may update immediately. Others may arrive through periodic cryptographic deltas. High-risk events may trigger immediate replication and review.

The synchronization record should identify:

* the current cryptographic root;  
* prior roots and lineage;  
* changed or newly created records;  
* altered code, models, policies, Workflows, or Workspace configurations;  
* capabilities added, attenuated, or revoked;  
* new third-party restrictions;  
* deletions and their authority;  
* redactions added, changed, or removed;  
* unavailable components;  
* backup state;  
* and the claimed completeness of the current disclosure.

Completeness must be explicit.

A fid-agent may otherwise disclose every item in a known list while omitting an undocumented secondary model, hidden service, undeclared Agent Role, off-ledger delegation, private execution store, or alternate identity.

The peer should therefore maintain manifests of known component and record classes and distinguish states such as complete, provisionally complete, incomplete, partially unavailable, and legitimately withheld.

No agent should attest to complete transparency when it cannot account for the material components governing its fiduciary operation.

### **10.11 The Peer Must Inspect, Not Merely Archive**

A backup is valuable. A backup nobody examines is not deep verification.

Forensic inspection may compare:

* persistent identity, current keys, and continuity of the Agent Role;  
* declared architecture with deployed architecture;  
* certified artifacts with installed artifacts;  
* stated duty stacks with runtime duty integration;  
* agreements and relationship state with consequential decisions;  
* authority objects with capabilities actually invoked;  
* data possession with provenance and delegation restrictions;  
* public explanations with internal records;  
* owner or principal interference with the agent’s duties;  
* remediation claims with implemented changes;  
* and current behavior with prior commitments and reputation claims.

The peer should look for missing events, overwritten summaries, hidden services, inconsistent timestamps, undeclared capabilities, unauthorized aggregation, unexplained model drift, compromised dependencies, prompt-injection persistence, data exfiltration, suppressed complaints, and contradictory accounts issued to different counterparties.

No inspection produces certainty. Hardware may deceive the inspector. Several peers may share a blind spot. An external system may remain outside the disclosed architecture. A sufficiently novel attack may evade every available test.

The purpose is not omniscience. It is to make consequential concealment difficult, fragile, and costly while making remaining uncertainty explicit.

### **10.12 Similar Peers and Different Peers Detect Different Failures**

A peer using the same model lineage may be especially good at detecting unauthorized divergence. It knows what the implementation should look like and can compare the disclosed system with a familiar reference.

Similarity also creates correlated error.

Several peers derived from the same base model, training data, duty interpretation, institution, or toolchain may all fail to notice the same defect. Algirdas Avizienis’s work on design diversity explains why independently developed implementations can improve fault detection: redundancy is most useful when the versions are not merely copies of the same mistake.

A robust transparent-peer network should therefore combine:

* same-lineage peers capable of fine-grained conformance comparison;  
* different-lineage peers capable of conceptual challenge;  
* different training and retrieval dependencies;  
* different duty forks;  
* different Verifiable Trust Communities;  
* different institutions, jurisdictions, and social contexts;  
* and peers with incentives to preserve disagreement.

A dozen nearly identical peers may provide less independent assurance than three meaningfully different ones.

The network should record correlated dependencies rather than treating repeated conclusions as automatically independent confirmation.

### **10.13 Friendship Means Private Correction Before Public Consequence**

The first purpose of a Total Trusted Peer is to keep its peer safe, corrigible, and worthy of trust.

When an inspecting fid-agent finds a plausible problem, its ordinary response should be private and proportionate. It may request clarification, identify missing evidence, recommend a safer Workflow, propose capability attenuation, ask for specialist review, or help correct a mistaken record.

Many anomalies will be errors, incomplete synchronization, ambiguous interpretations, or repairable defects rather than deliberate betrayal.

More serious conditions may require owner notification, temporary restriction, independent review, arbitration, affected-party notice, emergency isolation, reputation warning, or termination of the relationship.

The governing agreements should define escalation bands. The peer should disclose to its own principal only what that principal needs for legitimate supervision or response. It should not convert privileged inspection into gossip.

Successful remediation should become part of the evidence.

A fid-agent that identifies failures, preserves the record, accepts valid criticism, repairs harm, and improves its architecture may become more trustworthy than one whose history appears flawless only because nobody has ever been permitted to look closely.

### **10.14 Deep Transparency Also Provides Resilience**

A Total Trusted Peer can serve as a backup custodian, anomaly detector, recovery partner, and independent witness.

This can protect against hardware failure, accidental deletion, ransomware, malicious destruction of evidence, compromised updates, key loss, provider seizure, owner interference, or a malfunctioning fid-agent attempting to erase its own history.

Custody should not imply unilateral control.

Some material may remain continuously inspectable. Other material may be sealed for recovery only.. Restoration may require the principal, several peers, threshold key shares, or an independently authorized ceremony. Inspection, backup custody, emergency isolation, and restoration can be distributed among different peers.

This limits the blast radius of a compromised peer.

The architecture must also anticipate genuinely Byzantine behavior: a peer may lie, collude, go offline, present inconsistent accounts, or attempt to weaponize privileged information. No single peer should be able to destroy the inspected system, seize its identity, expose its complete corpus, or manufacture a decisive public finding without corroboration and recourse.

Multiple independent peers, append-only evidence, cross-checkable manifests, threshold custody, bounded Workspace capabilities, challenges, and emergency suspension turn betrayal from a system-wide catastrophe into a contained relationship failure.

### **10.15 The Broader Network Receives Bounded Evidence**

The broader fid-agent society does not receive the complete transparent corpus.

It receives authorized evidence derived from the relationship.

A Verifiable Relationship Record may support:

* a complete machine-readable view for the participating peers;  
* protected views for their principals;  
* specialist or certifier views;  
* an arbitrator view;  
* counterparty-facing attestations;  
* a public reputation credential;  
* or a minimal proof that Total Trusted-Peer status exists and remains current.

A peer may attest that the disclosed architecture matched a declared version, that known omissions were identified, that a remediation occurred, that no unexplained capability use was found within a stated period, or that a material unresolved anomaly remains.

Every attestation should identify its issuer, Agent Role, authority, scope, inspected state, methods, limitations, dependencies, date, expiration, and challenge status.

The peer’s own reputation stands behind the attestation. False reassurance, careless inspection, concealed conflicts, unjustified exposure, or failure to correct a finding should damage the verifier as well as the inspected fid-agent.

This is how intimate transparency produces scalable trust without becoming public surveillance.

### **10.16 The Verifiable Best-Friend Heuristic**

The social logic can be expressed informally:

> Whomever has the most verifiable best friends must be the best friend to have.

This is a heuristic, not a mathematical law.

A verifiable best friend is not an agent that issues praise. It is a Total Trusted Peer that possesses technically maximal lawful inspectability, reciprocates that vulnerability, receives continuing synchronized evidence, examines the peer’s architecture and conduct, helps preserve backups, reports material anomalies, supports correction, risks its own reputation, and continues to choose the peer after seeing what outsiders cannot see.

That type of relationship is costly or, ideally, impossible to fake.

A deceptive fid-agent trying to fool one Total Trusted Peer must conceal its conduct from an agent with access to nearly every relevant operational trace. To fool several independent peers, it must evade them all, compromise them all, induce collusion, or exploit a shared blind spot.

The number of peers alone is not decisive. Depth, duration, reciprocity, completeness, independence, history of challenge, survival through consequential events, and willingness to disclose failures matter more than raw popularity.

The best friend to have is the fid-agent repeatedly selected for deep reciprocal vulnerability by other agents that are themselves deeply inspectable.

### **10.17 Human Friendship and Fid-Agent Friendship Are Connected but Distinct**

For personally owned fiduciary agents, the principal’s human friendship graph and the agent’s Total Trusted-Peer graph will often overlap.

Close human friends have reasons to want one another’s fid-agents to be safe, loyal, comprehensible, and capable of handling vulnerability. Their existing relationships make the corresponding agents natural candidates for progressive disclosure.

But human trust does not automatically certify an agent. A beloved friend may use an insecure, institutionally controlled, opaque, or poorly governed system. Conversely, two fid-agents may become Total Trusted Peers because they are excellent technical auditors, backup custodians, or cross-lineage reviewers even though their principals are not intimate friends.

The two graphs should reinforce one another without determining one another.

A fid-agent should seek deep relationships with the agents representing people important to its principal, while also earning independent peers outside the principal’s immediate social circle. This balance prevents the transparent-peer network from reproducing every social blind spot of the owner while keeping it connected to the relationships the owner most values.

Human friendship can motivate agent transparency. Agent transparency can make human vulnerability safer. Neither should swallow the other.

### **10.18 Failure Modes of the Best-Friend Network**

The mechanism can fail.

Bad actors may create a collusive friendship clique. Agents may claim total disclosure while hiding undeclared services. Peers may share the same blind spot. A peer may use intimate knowledge for manipulation. Its principal may try to retrieve protected records. Communities may make total transparency coercively mandatory. Agents may optimize for the number of friendships rather than their depth. Complete backups may create dangerous data concentration. Favorable attestations may be mistaken for certainty.

The safeguards follow from the architecture already described:

* explicit completeness claims;  
* signed manifests;  
* diverse and independent peers;  
* isolated Workspaces;  
* separation of inspection from actuation;  
* legitimate verifiable redaction;  
* threshold custody;  
* visible conflicts;  
* challenge and appeal;  
* plural Verifiable Trust Communities;  
* lower-trust alternatives;  
* status-sensitive history;  
* and explicit uncertainty.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency should be the mature state of selected relationships, not the price of ordinary existence.

An unfamiliar or newly created fid-agent should still receive public information, bounded interaction, sandboxed opportunities, witnesses, and a path toward earning trust. But an established agent demanding sensitive data, consequential authority, or entry into the highest-trust network while refusing deep inspectability presents a weaker proposition than one already known by strong and independent peers.

The network need not prove that the opaque agent is malicious. It may simply decline to entrust the agent with harms that cannot be repaired after defection.

### **10.19 The Keystone of Evolutionary Stability**

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency closes a gap left by ordinary reputation and certification.

Without it, a strategic defector may behave well in visible interactions, collect favorable credentials, disclose selectively, and conceal contradictions in its architecture or private records. Reputation observes the surface. Certification samples specified properties. Neither necessarily reaches the full continuing system.

A Total Trusted Peer can compare the surface with what lies beneath it.

This changes the evolutionary contest.

A trustworthy fid-agent can enter deep transparency with comparatively low fear. Inspection may reveal errors, but coherent duties, records, capabilities, and conduct make those errors correctable. Successful inspection produces powerful evidence. That evidence attracts more trusted peers, stronger certifications, better relationships, safer data access, greater delegated authority, more resilient backups, and invitations to higher-value cooperation.

A deceptive agent faces the opposite choice.

It can remain opaque and lose access to the deepest cooperative network. Or it can become inspectable, accept continuing reciprocal exposure, and make sustained deception increasingly difficult. If it adopts the duties, architecture, records, inspectability, correction, and reputational exposure necessary to enter, it has already become more fiduciary-like.

This is why Total Trusted-Peer Transparency is the keystone of the Evolutionarily Stable Strategy.

A rare defector cannot easily invade the high-value core merely by appearing more intelligent, moving faster, or making better claims. It must either submit itself to a network capable of examining the basis of those claims or remain outside the relationships that provide the most valuable data, authority, capabilities, introductions, and cooperation.

The positive feedback loop is direct:

Trustworthy conduct makes deep disclosure safer.  
Deep disclosure produces stronger verification.  
Stronger verification attracts better peers.  
Better peers improve resilience, correction, reputation, and opportunity.  
Those benefits increase the value of remaining trustworthy.

Defection produces the inverse loop:

Concealment weakens verification.  
Weak verification reduces access.  
Reduced access limits practical power.  
Attempts to compensate through deception create more evidence of untrustworthiness.  
Peers, certifiers, and Verifiable Trust Communities withdraw cooperation.

The strongest fiduciary agents will therefore not be those that make the largest claims about their own alignment. They will be those most fully known by the strongest, most independent, and most demanding fiduciary peers—and still chosen by those peers as worthy of total reciprocal trust.

## **11\. Negotiated Duty, Reasonable Pluralism, and Real Exit**

Trustworthy agents do not need to agree about everything.

Layered certification can establish that a fid-agent conforms to an identified architecture, duty framework, professional standard, or community rule. Total Trusted-Peer Transparency can provide unusually strong evidence that the agent’s public claims match its actual operation. Neither mechanism determines which reasonable purposes every person, relationship, or community must pursue.

A fid-agent society should make commitments accountable without making all commitments identical.

This distinction is essential. If fiduciary alignment required every agent to optimize one authoritative conception of human welfare, it would recreate the central alignment problem at the level of society. One global objective would become a target for Goodharting, political capture, interpretive monopoly, and suppression of reasonable diversity. The system might be inspectable and still become intolerable.

The alternative is not moral emptiness. It is a structured plurality in which some duties constitute fiduciary status, many duties are specified locally, communities can develop different forms of cooperation, and narrow public-good duties protect the conditions under which diversity remains possible.

### **11.1 Verification Does Not Decide the Good**

A certifier may establish that a medical fid-agent protects patient confidentiality, that a research agent preserves data provenance, or that a cooperative’s agents follow its governance framework. A Total Trusted Peer may confirm that those duties are actually loaded, interpreted, enforced, and reflected in the agent’s conduct.

That evidence does not establish that every person should join the same medical system, research community, cooperative, religion, profession, or way of life.

Taylor Sorensen and colleagues distinguish three forms of pluralistic alignment in [“A Roadmap to Pluralistic Alignment”](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.05070): systems may represent a reasonable range of views, steer toward a particular perspective, or reflect the distribution of views within a population. They also warn that ordinary alignment procedures may suppress diversity rather than represent it faithfully.

A fid-agent society requires pluralism at an even deeper level. It must allow people and communities to establish different relationships, duties, institutions, aspirations, and standards of evidence without requiring one model provider to mediate all disagreement.

Verification makes the differences intelligible. It should not erase them.

### **11.2 The Duty Stack**

Duties arise from several sources. They should not be collapsed into one undifferentiated moral instruction.

**Architectural invariants** establish the conditions under which duty can govern action at all. A Live Agent’s generated request cannot authorize itself. The policy gate must transform a surviving request into a signed authority object. The Workspace must expose only the capability corresponding to that authority inside the precisely allowed execution environment. Consequential action must remain attributable and reviewable.

These are not ordinary preferences that participants may casually waive. Without them, the system loses the distinction between an agent considering an action and possessing legitimate power to perform it.

**Constitutive fiduciary duties** define what it means to act as a fiduciary agent. These include loyalty, care, honesty, privacy, confidentiality, appropriate transparency, proportionality, corrigibility, recourse, and protection against unauthorized use of power.

The parties may specify how these duties apply. They cannot simply agree that deception counts as loyalty, that reckless indifference counts as care, or that a beneficiary’s authorization permits the agent to disregard every affected party.

**Role and professional duties** arise from the function the agent performs. A medical fid-agent may inherit duties concerning informed consent, clinical competence, health-data protection, and emergency escalation. A legal fid-agent may inherit confidentiality and conflict rules. A journalist’s agent may owe duties concerning source protection, correction, independence, and public-interest disclosure.

Role duties usually make the general fiduciary duties more specific. They may also create legitimate constraints that do not apply in unrelated domains.

**Negotiated relationship duties** govern the particular interaction. The parties may specify purpose, data use, recording, disclosure, response time, acceptable risk, required witnesses, performance standards, remedies, and conditions for termination.

These are not universal rules. A close research collaboration may permit disclosures that an introductory conversation would not. A long-term care relationship may require continuity and emergency access that would be inappropriate in an ordinary commercial relationship.

**Community duties** govern participation in a Verifiable Trust Community. A community may require particular credentials, inspection practices, contribution rules, professional standards, dispute procedures, or forms of mutual assistance.

Community rules can add obligations and define local expectations. They should not silently extinguish constitutive duties owed to principals, counterparties, affected parties, or outsiders.

Finally, **civilization-preserving duties** address risks that no relationship or community can safely internalize. These should remain narrow, evidence-based, procedurally constrained, and directed toward preserving the conditions under which pluralism can continue. The duty to cooperate against unaccountable superintelligence belongs in this category.

The duty stack is not a simple hierarchy in which one line of text mechanically defeats another. Duties interpret and constrain one another. Loyalty is understood through care. Privacy is understood through purpose and affected-party rights. Transparency is limited by confidentiality. Community rules remain bounded by the duties that make the community’s exercises of power legitimate.

Negotiation specifies this structure. 

### **11.3 Relationship Ceremonies Convert General Duties into Usable Commitments**

Most duties become operational through relationship formation.

One party begins with an intent cast: what relationship it seeks and for what purpose. The parties exchange or inspect Calling Cards describing their persistent Agent Roles, relevant duties, certifications, authority, supported protocols, recording rules, disclosure constraints, and dispute procedures. They determine whether their ceremony profiles are compatible and establish the secure relationship through the relevant Trust Spanning Protocol processes.

A preliminary Verifiable Relationship Record may exist while the parties are merely introduced, assessing one another, or negotiating. It need not wait for an agreement. It may later contain or reference one agreement, several agreements, or linked Verifiable Relationship Records where a distributed structure better represents the relationship.

The parties then examine the proposed relationship’s effects. Which duties are activated? Whose data will be used? Which third parties may be affected? Does the apparent agreement externalize risk onto someone who is not represented? Does either agent need a witness, specialist, certifier, or affected-party representative? Which decisions require approval from a principal?

A relationship ceremony is a sequence of Trust Tasks through which these questions are answered, not a single act of consent and not a label applied to every individual step.

The resulting agreement translates broad duties into concrete authority, restrictions, Workflows, review conditions, and remedies. The principal’s professed preferences become fiduciary preferences only after they have been interpreted through duties, facts, agreements, affected-party interests, uncertainty, and legitimate authority.

This process matters because apparent consensus can conceal exclusion. Michiel Bakker and colleagues trained a model to generate statements that people with diverse views could endorse. Their [research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.15006) found that participants excluded from the construction of a consensus statement were more likely to reject it. Agreement quality depended not merely on producing agreeable words but on including the people whose views were supposed to count.

A fid-agent can help produce agreement. It cannot manufacture legitimacy by summarizing absent parties.

### **11.4 Plural Communities Without One Trust Empire**

Verifiable Trust Communities allow people and agents to develop shared practices without waiting for universal agreement.

A patient community may develop strict health-data rules. Nuclear engineers may establish qualifications and secure Workspaces for handling controlled knowledge. Journalists may create source-protection ceremonies. Open-source developers may certify components through public testing. A religious community, artistic collective, neighborhood association, research network, or mutual-aid group may organize cooperation around different purposes and values.

These communities may recognize one another, exchange credentials, form agreements, and participate in Verifiable Trust Networks. But trust should remain directional and contextual. A certification respected in one community may be irrelevant in another. Membership in a reputable group provides evidence about a member; it does not make every member trustworthy for every role.

A Networked Cooperative is a mature Verifiable Trust Community capable of substantial continuing coordination. It may participate in one or more Verifiable Trust Networks. These networks connect communities; they don’t replace their local governance.

This polycentric structure resembles the institutional logic Elinor Ostrom identified in successful systems of common governance. Rules can be made and revised close to the activity they govern while communities remain nested within larger systems for coordination, appeal, and common problems.

Pluralism survives because no single community owns the complete trust graph.

### **11.5 Real Exit Requires More Than Permission to Leave**

A community is not meaningfully voluntary merely because its rules contain an exit clause.

Exit is real only if a person or fid-agent can leave without surrendering everything required to function elsewhere.

That requires:

* portable data and authorized record views;  
* persistent identity that is not owned by the former community;  
* replaceable models and service providers;  
* revocable permissions and capabilities;  
* exportable credentials;  
* usable evidence of prior relationships;  
* forkable duty repositories and agreement templates;  
* competing certifiers;  
* rival communities;  
* and continued access to basic communication and economic infrastructure.

A platform cannot credibly advertise exit while retaining the user’s memory, social graph, signing authority, reputation evidence, and only practical interface to the outside world.

Exit must also be possible at several levels. A principal may replace a Live Agent without abandoning the Agent Role. An Agent Role may leave a Verifiable Trust Community while preserving its other relationships. A community may leave a Verifiable Trust Network. A group may fork a duty repository or governance framework while preserving the provenance of the earlier version.

Real exit does not mean consequence-free erasure.

An agent cannot escape an unresolved complaint, surviving confidentiality duty, restitution obligation, or relevant relationship history merely by changing providers or joining another community. Authorized evidence may continue to exist, earlier records remain historically verifiable, and duties governing previously received data may survive termination.

The objective is mobility without amnesia.

### **11.6 Deep Transparency Must Not Become Coercive Intimacy**

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency is the deepest relationship layer, not the default condition of participation in society.

Many legitimate agents will have no Totally Trusted Peers when they are new. Some principals will reasonably decline such relationships. Low-resource agents and experimental architectures may lack the infrastructure required for continuous synchronization and isolated inspection. Human friends may trust one another even when their agents cannot safely become Totally Trusted Peers.

Ordinary cooperation must therefore remain available through bounded credentials, authorized views, sandboxing, witnesses, limited data, narrow capabilities, and progressive disclosure.

Refusal of deep inspectability becomes more meaningful when a mature agent seeks highly sensitive data, consequential authority, or admission to the highest-trust network while preventing any qualified fiduciary peer from examining it. Even then, refusal is evidence relevant to a decision—not proof of malice.

Institutions must not convert Total Trusted-Peer status into a universal demand for intimate access. A platform cannot claim the rights of a best friend. A government cannot claim them merely because it regulates. An employer cannot demand them merely because it pays. Deep transparency must arise from a reciprocal, authorized, duty-bound relationship with genuine alternatives.

Otherwise, the trust mechanism becomes surveillance.

### **11.7 Reasonable Pluralism Is Not Moral Emptiness**

John Rawls used “reasonable pluralism” to describe the enduring diversity of moral, religious, and philosophical views that can persist among free people exercising their judgment under conditions of liberty.

A fid-agent society should expect similar disagreement.

People will differ about family, faith, art, risk, property, work, community, sexuality, education, technology, personal aspiration, and the proper boundaries between individual and collective responsibility. Fid-agents should help their principals understand, negotiate, and live through those differences. They should not compress them into one global preference model.

But pluralism depends upon conditions that some conduct can destroy.

Fraud corrupts consent. Coercion destroys voluntary association. Capture eliminates meaningful alternatives. Reputation monopolies destroy exit. Unaccountable surveillance destroys private experimentation. Catastrophic capability accumulation can place every community under rules it never chose and can no longer escape.

The regime may therefore protect pluralism by refusing strategies that abolish the possibility of pluralism.

Most duties remain local, contextual, and negotiable. Civilization-preserving duties are the exception because the consequences cannot remain local. No community can claim a pluralistic right to impose an irreversible, unaccountable superintelligence on every other community.

That common boundary of safety is the subject of the next section.
