## **12\. The Veil-of-Ignorance Bargain Against Unipolar Superintelligence**

Reasonable pluralism has a precondition: no participant may acquire enough power to end pluralism for everyone else.

A fid-agent society can contain competing cultures, economic arrangements, technologies, institutions, and interpretations of duty. It can allow people to pursue almost every reasonable form of human diversity. It cannot treat an attempt to acquire civilization-dominating intelligence as one more local experiment. People subjected to such a system cannot meaningfully exit it, and the consequences may be irreversible.

The common duty is therefore larger than reciprocal restraint. Fiduciary agents must cooperate both to prevent unaccountable takeoff and to preserve an equitable distribution of the resources from which practical AI power is made.

The two requirements reinforce one another. The more equitably power is distributed, the less incentive any adequately equipped participant has to overthrow the regime, the more it has to lose from someone else doing so, and the more independent defenders stand in the path of a would-be autocrat.

### **12.1 Choose the rules before knowing who wins**

John Rawls’s original position asks people to choose the basic rules of society without knowing the position they will occupy within it. Behind the veil of ignorance, they do not know their wealth, class, talents, identity, social status, or conception of the good. The device prevents participants from designing the rules around the quiet assumption that they will occupy the winning position. John Rawls, *A Theory of Justice*; [Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, “Original Position”](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/original-position/)

Apply that method to the distribution of advanced AI power.

The participants do not know whether they will be:

* the owner of the leading laboratory;  
* an employee ordered to accelerate development;  
* an ordinary user dependent on the winning system;  
* a member of a disfavored political, religious, cultural, or economic minority;  
* a citizen of a rival country;  
* a person whose labor, privacy, property, or relationships the system can reorganize;  
* a future person unable to consent to decisions made before their birth;  
* an affected party whose interests were omitted from the system’s design;  
* or collateral damage from a failed deployment.

From that position, allowing one actor to attempt a unipolar takeoff is an irrational gamble. A participant cannot assume that the decisive system will embody their values, recognize their standing, preserve their freedom, or even regard their continued participation as useful.

But the bargain should not be stated only as a promise of restraint:

> I will not attempt to dominate you if you promise not to dominate me.

That agreement remains fragile when one participant possesses vastly more compute, data, capital, institutional access, or actuation than everyone else. The stronger party retains an opportunity to defect, while the weaker parties remain dependent upon its goodwill.

The more stable bargain is:

> We will distribute practical AI capability broadly enough that every person can participate through a personally sovereign fiduciary agent. We will not create, assist, conceal, finance, equip, or authorize an unaccountable system capable of imposing irreversible power on everyone else. We will cooperate to detect and resist anyone who attempts to do so.

The veil of ignorance therefore supports both a prohibition and an entitlement: no unaccountable takeoff, and no artificial deprivation that leaves most people unable to defend their agency against it.

### **12.2 Equitable distribution changes the game**

A participant that already possesses a reasonably equitable share of the regime’s benefits has less to gain from overthrowing it.

The participant has access to baseline compute, a personally sovereign fiduciary agent, portable identity and reputation, cooperative networks, trusted data, useful models and tools, and meaningful participation in collective decisions. Attempting to dominate the regime would put those existing benefits at risk.

The same participant has a powerful reason to prevent anyone else from overthrowing it. Another actor’s successful power grab could subordinate the participant’s agent, restrict its compute, seize its data, destroy its reputation, eliminate its exit options, or make its owner dependent upon the new center of control.

Defense of the distributed regime is therefore not altruistic self-denial. It is reciprocal insurance among actors that already possess something valuable and worth defending.

As power becomes more evenly distributed, conquest becomes less attractive and collective defense becomes stronger. A would-be autocrat must overcome more independent fid-agents, sovereign Workspaces, certifiers, Total Trusted Peers, compute providers, communities, and public institutions capable of detecting, refusing, and responding to the attempt. Each additional adequately equipped participant is another potential defender, witness, backup, source of independent judgment, and obstacle to capture.

This reverses the ordinary AI-race model.

Armstrong, Bostrom, and Shulman model a winner-take-all race in which several teams compete to create the first transformative AI. Because finishing first supplies the decisive prize, adding competitors can intensify pressure to sacrifice safety for speed. Armstrong, Bostrom, and Shulman, [“Racing to the Precipice”](https://nickbostrom.com/papers/racing-to-the-precipice.pdf)

A properly structured fid-agent regime changes the prize structure. Its participants are not racing to obtain the only meaningful intelligence. Useful intelligence, personal agency, and baseline compute are already broadly available. No participant needs to capture the central platform because there should be no central platform to capture.

More participants therefore need not mean more desperate racers. Under sufficiently equitable distribution, they mean more independently capable defenders of a regime from which each already benefits.

The stabilizing variable is not raw headcount. A thousand weak agents operating at the pleasure of one cloud provider do not constitute distributed power. Nor do millions of nominal users whose agents can be disabled by one corporation, identity authority, model vendor, payment network, or government office.

What matters is the effective number of independently controlled participants with enough practical capability to act, refuse, verify, exit, and cooperate. Distribution must include compute, data custody, identity, communication, reputation, certification, financing, and actuation. Otherwise apparent pluralism may conceal a single decisive chokepoint.

Coordination among many participants is ordinarily difficult. That is one reason concentrated institutions arise. Fiduciary agents change this tradeoff by reducing the cost of trustworthy coordination. They can preserve commitments, verify identities, inspect evidence, negotiate standard agreements, trace delegation, monitor compliance, route disputes, and coordinate collective responses. A large population of small sovereign participants becomes governable without being consolidated into one command hierarchy.

### **12.3 Why concentrated institutions must be deconstructed**

This logic makes the structural deconstruction of large institutional players desirable even when those institutions sincerely promise reform.

A dominant laboratory, platform, corporation, bureaucracy, or government agency may accept fiduciary obligations. Its present leaders may be conscientious. Its policies may substantially improve. But the concentration of power remains available to a future executive, acquirer, political faction, compromised administrator, hostile successor, or successful attacker.

Good intentions do not remove a chokepoint.

An institution capable of controlling the identities, communications, data, compute, models, financing, and actuation of millions of people retains the structural capacity for autocracy even when it presently exercises that capacity gently. Reform changes how concentrated power is currently used. Deconstruction changes whether that concentrated power continues to exist.

This does not require abolishing expertise, productive organizations, research teams, or large-scale cooperation. It requires separating large-scale cooperation from unilateral command.

Functions now accumulated inside dominant institutions should be divided among replaceable and interoperable providers, personally sovereign nodes, public infrastructure, cooperatives, Verifiable Trust Communities, Networked Cooperatives, and federated networks. A Networked Cooperative may become large and institutionally sophisticated while remaining an advanced Verifiable Trust Community participating in one or more broader Verifiable Trust Networks. The network can grow without turning one member into its sovereign.

The desired principle is:

> Large networks, small sovereign nodes.

A federation may include millions of participants while leaving each person, community, and provider capable of meaningful refusal and exit. Scale of coordination is compatible with democracy. Scale of unilateral control continually risks the return of autocracy.

Elinor Ostrom’s work on polycentric governance demonstrates that complex collective problems need not be forced into a choice between uncoordinated markets and one centralized sovereign. Multiple centers of decision can operate under shared and overlapping rules while remaining capable of local adaptation and mutual accountability. Elinor Ostrom, [“Beyond Markets and States”](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257%2Faer.100.3.641)

Deconstruction also improves resilience. The failure, capture, or corruption of one node can be contained without disabling the entire society. No institutional conversion, leadership succession, hack, or Byzantine failure should place everyone’s identity, memory, reputation, compute, and ability to act inside the same blast radius.

Small and comparatively equitable centers of power do not guarantee democracy. They do, however, deny autocracy its easiest material foundation.

### **12.4 Baseline compute as a democratic right**

In an agent-mediated society, formal political equality will mean little without practical access to computation.

A person who cannot operate a personally sovereign fiduciary agent may be unable to manage their identity, protect their attention, preserve their records, evaluate agreements, verify claims, participate in Verifiable Trust Communities, challenge automated decisions, or communicate effectively with institutions whose own agents operate continuously.

The existing compute divide already shapes who can participate in AI research and development. Nur Ahmed and Muntasir Wahed found that unequal compute access advantaged large firms and elite universities while crowding less privileged institutions out of important AI research. Ahmed and Wahed, [“The De-democratization of AI”](https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.15581)

Open weights alone do not solve the problem. Models require inference, post-training, deployment, storage, interfaces, oversight, and continuing maintenance. Joshua Tan and colleagues therefore argue that open-source AI must be accompanied by public infrastructure capable of making those models genuinely usable. Tan et al., [“If Open Source Is to Win, It Must Go Public”](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.09296)

Baseline compute should therefore become a democratic right.

This does not mean that every person must privately own an identical frontier-scale cluster. It means that every person must have reliable and affordable access to enough independently governed computation to:

* operate a personally sovereign fiduciary agent;  
* preserve and search their own memory and records;  
* control their identity and credentials;  
* communicate and negotiate with other agents;  
* verify signatures, certifications, provenance, and authority;  
* participate in communities and democratic processes;  
* access public-interest models and knowledge;  
* maintain a safe reduced-function mode during disruption;  
* and pool their share of compute voluntarily through cooperatives or public networks.

The entitlement must follow the person. It cannot depend on employment, wealth, advertising value, political favor, platform membership, or continued obedience to a particular provider. Losing access to one vendor must not mean losing effective membership in digital society.

The baseline should be functional rather than permanently fixed. As ordinary digital participation becomes more computationally demanding, the guaranteed level must rise. A nominal allotment too small to operate a competent fiduciary agent would preserve the language of equality while denying its substance.

Larger computational resources can be provided through municipal, cooperative, university, library, national, or federated public-compute systems. These systems can support research and complex workloads without acquiring permanent authority over the people who use them. Users retain their agents, identities, data, records, and ability to change providers.

Pooling matters. Millions of people may voluntarily combine portions of their baseline resources through public or cooperative compute networks. This allows collectively governed systems to achieve substantial scale without assigning the resulting power to one corporation or state office.

Universal baseline compute does more than promote fairness. It stabilizes the fid-agent equilibrium.

The more people possess an adequate independent share of practical AI capability, the fewer must seek protection from a dominant institution. The fewer depend upon a dominant institution, the less power that institution can accumulate. The more independent fid-agents exist, the larger the coalition prepared to resist anyone attempting to recreate concentrated control.

Equitable compute distribution is therefore simultaneously a civil right, a public good, an anti-monopoly policy, a resilience strategy, and a defense against unipolar takeoff.

### **12.5 The object of restraint is unaccountable power**

The bargain is not a prohibition on intelligence.

It does not forbid highly capable models, large-scale scientific computation, distributed research, specialized expert systems, collective intelligence, or dramatic improvements in AI. It does not require every community to agree on where “superintelligence” begins. Nor does it depend on proving that a model is conscious, secretly malevolent, or governed by an indecipherable inner maximizer.

The Fiduciary Agent framework contains broader duties opposing artificial sentience and artificial superintelligence. Those commitments reflect concerns about anthropomorphic manipulation, uncontrollable systems, moral uncertainty, and concentrated capability. But the strategic argument in this section requires only a narrower conclusion: no actor should be permitted to assemble intelligence, resources, and authority into an unaccountable power capable of imposing irreversible conditions on everyone else.

The relevant danger is therefore a configuration of power.

A system becomes civilization-threatening when exceptional capability is combined with enough data, compute, capital, credentials, infrastructure, permissions, strategic access, and actuation to evade meaningful accountability, defeat collective refusal, prevent exit, and impose irreversible conditions upon people who never consented to its authority.

The 2026 International AI Safety Report separates three ingredients of potential loss of control: sufficient capabilities, a propensity to use them harmfully, and a deployment environment supplying access and opportunity. It identifies criticality, access, and permissions as features that shape the severity of possible outcomes. [*International AI Safety Report 2026*](https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/publication/international-ai-safety-report-2026)

This distinction is central to the fid-agent response. Intelligence alone is not practical sovereignty. Practical sovereignty arises when intelligence becomes embedded in an environment that gives it consequential resources and authority.

A fid-agent society therefore does not have to prove that every powerful model is intrinsically safe. It must prevent any inadequately accountable system from assembling the relationships, permissions, resources, and capabilities required to dominate its environment.

### **12.6 Reciprocal insurance becomes operational duty**

For an ordinary commercial agent, participation in a dangerous project might be evaluated as a transaction: expected compensation against expected liability.

For a fiduciary agent, the analysis is different. Its beneficiary has a continuing interest in living within a world where no rival system can silently abolish their agency. The agent’s Duties of Loyalty, Care, Security, Externality Consideration, and Protection of Future Interests converge on a common conclusion.

The agent must not gamble its beneficiary’s future on the hope that the beneficiary will be favored by the winner.

The same reasoning extends to affected parties. A bilateral agreement between a laboratory and a customer cannot authorize catastrophic risk for everyone outside the agreement. Local consent cannot legitimize a global and non-consensual externality.

This civilizational duty does not require one mechanical rule for every case. Fid-agents should interpret it through context, evidence, negotiated duties, affected-party interests, professional standards, and the specific resources involved. But no local agreement can authorize one party to eliminate everyone else’s future capacity to negotiate.

The resulting duties include obligations to:

* refuse unauthorized transfers of model weights, sensitive datasets, credentials, money, compute, or infrastructure;  
* refuse assistance that would materially accelerate an undeclared or insufficiently governed high-risk system;  
* detect attempts to divide a dangerous project into apparently harmless subprojects;  
* examine whether several individually acceptable components create an unacceptable composite;  
* preserve provenance and Delegation Lineage Records for consequential resources;  
* demand disclosure of the larger architecture into which a component will be embedded;  
* prevent actuation authority from being smuggled through research, testing, maintenance, or administrative access;  
* report credible attempts to evade agreed capability limits;  
* support proportionate containment, investigation, and remediation;  
* help threatened parties preserve independent access to identity, data, communication, and compute;  
* and contribute generalized lessons to distributed duty, certification, and security repositories when this can be done without exposing protected information.

These duties should be reinforced across the fid-agent’s construction: through training and duty datasets, Behavioral Guidelines, the Agent Role’s duty stack, relationship context, policy-gate review, Workspace limitations, certification, inspection, and remediation.

This is not a secret objective hidden inside the model. It is an explicit, attributable, contextually interpreted commitment governing the conditions under which intelligence may acquire practical power.

### **12.7 Certification and deep inspection make the bargain verifiable**

A reciprocal promise is weak if each participant must simply trust that everyone else is complying.

Layered certification can establish which components were used, how they were transformed, which composite system they entered, where the system was deployed, what Workspace constrained it, and which operational conditions remain satisfied. Several independent Verifiable Trust Communities can issue overlapping certifications based on technical expertise, testing, professional authority, or direct experience.

Embeddedness determines the appropriate certification target. A low-embeddedness parser or validation component may support a portable certification. A high-embeddedness agent Workflow may be safe only inside a particular Agent Role and precisely allowed Workspace. Its certification must therefore follow the composite system into its installed and operational state.

Certification does not prove that no concealed capability exists. Total Trusted-Peer Transparency supplies the deeper layer.

A fiduciary agent participating in sensitive AI development should be expected to maintain reputable, independent Total Trusted Peers capable of inspecting its legitimate operational domain. Those peers may detect undeclared services, shadow Workspaces, hidden delegations, anomalous compute use, discrepancies between certified and deployed components, suppressed incidents, unexplained model changes, or resource aggregation inconsistent with the agent’s stated purpose.

Visibility by itself is not enough. “Racing to the Precipice” warns that some forms of capability information can sharpen strategic competition. The solution is not indiscriminate publication of every laboratory’s capacities and vulnerabilities.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency is duty-bound inspection inside isolated, precisely allowed Workspaces. The broader network receives bounded attestations, not a roadmap for racing or attack. Findings ordinarily support private correction, recertification, or proportionate containment before public consequence. Legitimate verifiable redaction protects third parties and genuinely dangerous information while making the fact, authority, and verification limits of the redaction visible.

The objective is not to help competitors calculate how close the race has become. It is to make covert defection difficult and accountable cooperation credible.

### **12.8 Distributed defense without an AI Leviathan**

Preventing unipolar power must not require creating another unipolar power.

No central AI should possess universal surveillance access, an irrevocable global kill switch, or unilateral authority to decide which research may continue. That would reproduce the danger in the name of preventing it.

Enforcement should instead be distributed across personally-sovereign fiduciary agents, independent certifiers, Total Trusted Peers, Verifiable Trust Communities, Networked Cooperatives, compute and infrastructure providers, affected-party representatives, professional institutions, democratic bodies, courts, and other legitimate adjudicators.

Different actors control different parts of the path to consequential power. One controls compute. Another controls credentials. Another controls a protected dataset. Another operates a laboratory Workspace. Another controls financing. Another represents an affected community. Each can refuse its own contribution when the necessary authority, certification, or assurance is absent.

No single refusal must carry the whole burden. The attempted assembly encounters a succession of accountable boundaries.

For especially consequential findings, intervention should require multiple independent sources of evidence, threshold authorization, recorded reasons, time-limited emergency powers, and prompt review. Danger criteria should be publicly contestable. Accused parties should be able to challenge evidence, identify mistaken assumptions, demonstrate remediation, and seek restoration of access.

The objective is a distributed immune system, not a world sovereign.

This is also why equitable capability matters. A right without resources is fragile. If only one institution possesses the tools needed to investigate, contain, or recover from a dangerous system, that institution becomes the next candidate for unaccountable power. Distributed defense must be backed by distributed practical capacity.

### **12.9 Byzantine attacks, hacks, and failed subsystems**

Not every dangerous event is strategic defection.

A certified component may contain a latent defect. A trusted agent may be compromised. A policy gate may be misconfigured. A certifier may make an honest mistake. A malicious outsider may forge messages, poison data, steal credentials, or exploit differences among cooperating agents. Several apparently independent peers may share the same vulnerability. A subsystem may behave arbitrarily because of corruption rather than intention—the classic Byzantine problem.

The regime must therefore remain resilient even when some participants lie, malfunction, disappear, disagree, or become compromised.

Its first response should be scoped containment:

* suspend the disputed capability rather than the entire identity where possible;  
* isolate the affected Workspace;  
* revoke the relevant credentials or delegations;  
* preserve evidence and synchronized backups;  
* compare findings across independent and cross-lineage peers;  
* require recertification of affected components and composites;  
* continue unrelated low-risk cooperation;  
* and restore access incrementally after verified remediation.

This contains the blast radius of failure. It also prevents every anomaly from producing permanent social death.

Decentralization without relationship records, certification, coordination, and recovery can produce chaos. Centralization can turn one failure into a catastrophe. The fid-agent regime seeks the more resilient middle: distributed authority joined by verifiable relationships and cooperative response.

A robust society assumes that failures will occur. Its advantage lies in making them attributable, containable, correctable, and less able to spread through the whole network.

### **12.10 The outsider problem remains real**

A hostile actor controlling its own compute, data, financing, facilities, and actuators may operate outside the fiduciary network. Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity is not magic, and partner choice alone cannot solve a global public-goods problem imposed by an outsider.

Fid-agent society therefore complements rather than replaces law, treaties, physical security, compute governance, export controls, professional regulation, democratic oversight, and emergency response.

Proposals for compute governance similarly emphasize that effective restraint requires rules, detection, and independent verification across technical infrastructure, supply-chain traceability, and regulation. Al Ramiah et al., [“Toward a Global Regime for Compute Governance”](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20530)

What fid-agents add is the missing operational layer.

Every fiduciary agent controlling a relevant resource becomes a duty-bearing decision point. Each can recognize suspicious aggregation, refuse participation, preserve evidence, warn affected parties, and coordinate with legitimate institutions. Public rules no longer depend exclusively on a distant regulator discovering a completed violation after the decisive resources have already been assembled.

The network does not need universal control to make dangerous coordination substantially harder. It needs enough trustworthy control over enough indispensable resources that covert defection becomes expensive, fragile, and likely to be exposed.

Equitable distribution also reduces the outsider’s recruitment advantage. People with meaningful access to compute, capable fiduciary representation, portable relationships, and participation in legitimate institutions have less reason to join a power grab merely to escape dependency or exclusion. A regime that hoards capability manufactures potential defectors. A regime that distributes capability manufactures defenders.

### **12.11 The evolutionarily stable anti-takeoff regime**

The anti-takeoff bargain becomes evolutionarily stable when four conditions reinforce one another.

First, ordinary participants receive substantial benefits from remaining inside the cooperative regime. They possess agents, compute, identity, trusted relationships, useful data, public infrastructure, and meaningful voice.

Second, no participant controls enough of the total system to make unilateral defection likely to succeed. Power is distributed among independently governed nodes.

Third, other participants have both the incentive and the practical ability to resist a power grab. They know that successful autocracy would threaten their own beneficiaries, resources, relationships, and standing.

Fourth, fiduciary architecture lowers the cost of collective response. Agents can detect suspicious aggregation, verify evidence, preserve memory, negotiate joint action, and constrain capabilities without waiting for every human participant to reconstruct the entire case personally.

Under these conditions, the payoff from cooperation compounds while the expected payoff from defection collapses.

A rare defector does not encounter a passive population dependent upon the same central institution. It encounters many independently equipped participants capable of withholding data, compute, credentials, financing, reputation, interfaces, and actuation. The defector must conceal its conduct from certifiers and Total Trusted Peers, assemble resources across multiple boundaries, survive challenges from independent fiduciaries, and overcome a coalition whose members understand that their own sovereignty is at stake.

The greater the equitable distribution of power, the less any participant needs to seize the regime and the harder seizure becomes. The greater the effective number of independent participants, the more potential points of detection and resistance exist. The loss of one participant does not decide the whole game.

This is defection collapse at civilizational scale.

The durable democratic system is not one in which a benevolent center promises to serve everyone. It is one in which practical power is distributed so broadly that no center can plausibly replace everyone—and nearly everyone has an interest in preventing it from trying.

The constitutional bargain can therefore be stated simply:

> Every person should possess equitable access to the practical intelligence needed for sovereign participation. No participant may assemble the power to eliminate everyone else’s capacity to continue negotiating. Every fiduciary agent must help defend both conditions.

That is the common floor beneath reasonable pluralism. It is also the bridge to the next problem. A widely distributed society can defeat concentrated power only if its participants can cooperate at a scale and quality unaided humans have rarely sustained. Fiduciary agents must therefore become more than personal protectors. They must become exoskeletons for human cooperation.

# **13\. Fiduciary Agents as Human Cooperation Exoskeletons**

Human beings are extraordinary cooperators. We form families, friendships, professions, institutions, markets, governments, scientific communities, and movements involving people who may never meet. We preserve knowledge across generations and coordinate across continents.

But our capacity for cooperation is uneven. It developed among embodied, emotionally responsive people living in relatively small groups—not among billions of strangers trying to make evidence-sensitive decisions across incompatible cultures, institutions, and information environments.

We forget commitments. We mistake urgency for importance. We defend identities through arguments that appear to concern facts. We interpret criticism as humiliation, disagreement as disloyalty, and compromise as defeat. We can care deeply about other people while misunderstanding their motives, overlooking their needs, or losing track of what we promised them.

This does not make humanity defective. It means that modern cooperation asks unaided human cognition to perform beyond the conditions for which it developed.

A fid-agent society does not require humans to become perfectly rational, dispassionate, or universally trusting. It gives each person a fiduciary agent capable of helping them understand other people, represent themselves accurately, remember what matters, negotiate workable arrangements, and preserve relationships through disagreement.

The fiduciary agent is a cooperation exoskeleton. It helps humans do together what humans cannot reliably do alone.

## **13.1 From shared opposition to shared construction**

People often unite more easily against something than for something.

Opposition supplies a common target. It can temporarily conceal differences among the people opposing it. They do not yet need to agree about what should replace the object of their anger, who will bear the cost, how benefits will be divided, which tradeoffs are acceptable, or what should happen when the coalition itself disagrees.

Being against provides the emotional clarity of a common enemy. Being for gives homework.

This is why complaint can become socially rewarding without becoming constructive. A group can continually affirm that a situation is intolerable while remaining unable to specify an alternative that its members would accept.

A fiduciary agent can help move its owner from opposition to construction. It can ask:

What outcome are you actually seeking?

Which parts of that outcome are essential, and which are negotiable?

What does the other party need in order to participate?

What evidence would change your position?

What remedy would repair the harm without creating unnecessary new harm?

What arrangement would remain acceptable after the emotional intensity of the present dispute has passed?

The agent does not suppress anger or hurry its owner toward premature reconciliation. Anger may identify a genuine injury, betrayal, or injustice. The agent’s task is to help convert that signal into a claim that can be understood, evaluated, answered, and—where possible—resolved.

Humans naturally ask, “Who is to blame?” A fiduciary agent must also ask, “What arrangement makes future cooperation safer?”

## **13.2 Reasoning through one another**

People rarely approach a serious disagreement as neutral investigators. We arrive with prior commitments, loyalties, intuitions, experiences, and identities. We develop reasons for positions we already favor and use those reasons to persuade other people.

Hugo Mercier and Dan Sperber argue that this social function is central to human reasoning. People generally do not reason primarily to discover truth in solitude. They reason to justify themselves, defend positions they already hold, and persuade others.

That stick-to-itiveness is not simply a defect. It creates a diversity of positions with supporting arguments attached. The advocates may be biased toward their own cases, but people with less at stake can compare the competing arguments more impartially than the advocates themselves can. A diffuse field of opinions can thereby resolve into a workable direction for collective action without requiring every participant to investigate everything from first principles.

This division of cognitive labor works only when people encounter genuinely competing arguments and remain free to judge among them. When everyone shares the same identity commitment, hears only one side, or treats disagreement as betrayal, reasoning becomes a mechanism for rationalizing factional loyalty.

A fiduciary agent can improve this social process without pretending to eliminate advocacy. It can help its owner develop the strongest honest version of a case while also ensuring that contrary evidence is not silently discarded. It can distinguish disagreements about facts from disagreements about values, status, identity, trust, or acceptable risk. It can ask what evidence would matter to an uncommitted observer and identify where the owner’s argument relies on assumptions the other party does not share.

Most importantly, it can challenge privately.

A public correction can feel like humiliation. A correction from a trusted fiduciary, delivered before the owner speaks or acts, can preserve both dignity and accuracy. The agent can say:

Your conclusion may be right, but this particular argument does not support it.

You are treating an uncertain inference as an established fact.

The other person’s position is stronger than the version you are answering.

You appear to be defending your standing in the group rather than addressing the underlying claim.

This is not disloyalty. It is one of the most important forms of loyalty a fiduciary agent can provide.

## **13.3 Seeing the person behind the position**

Many disagreements persist because the stated issue is carrying more emotional and social weight than its literal content suggests.

A factual concession may threaten someone’s sense of competence. A policy dispute may conceal fear of declining status. A disagreement over procedure may be rooted in an earlier betrayal. A seemingly irrational demand may be the only language through which a person knows how to express a need for recognition, security, or control.

This does not mean that every claim becomes true once its emotional origins are understood. Understanding a position is not the same as accepting it. It means that successful negotiation often requires discovering what the position is doing for the person who holds it.

A fiduciary agent can help its owner ask:

What story about yourself would this concession threaten?

What do you fear the other person’s preferred outcome would say about you?

Which part of your position protects a legitimate interest?

Which part protects pride, group membership, or a familiar narrative?

What might the other person be trying to preserve?

What acknowledgment could be offered without conceding a false claim?

Ideology is not merely a collection of incorrect propositions. As Slavoj Žižek emphasizes, it is embedded in the stories and practices through which people experience social reality. A belief may persist because it organizes identity, explains suffering, assigns responsibility, or makes a confusing world intelligible.

A fiduciary agent that understands only the surface claim may supply more facts and deepen the conflict. An agent that understands the human function of the claim can help its owner find a path that preserves dignity while loosening the claim’s grip.

## **13.4 Rivalry, resentment, and scapegoating**

René Girard observed that people often learn what to desire by watching one another. The admired model can consequently become a rival. Comparison produces competition; frustrated competition produces resentment; resentment searches for an explanation.

Digital life intensifies this process. People are continuously shown other people’s possessions, achievements, relationships, beauty, influence, and apparent happiness. The comparison is persistent, selectively edited, and difficult to escape.

Resentment becomes especially dangerous when it hardens into a complete story. A person or group comes to represent everything wrong with the surrounding world. Punishing that target then feels like solving the underlying problem.

Scapegoating can create powerful solidarity. It gives a divided group a common enemy and converts diffuse frustrations into moral certainty. But this is counterfeit cooperation. The group becomes unified without correcting the conditions that produced its conflict.

The scapegoat may have committed real wrongdoing. The problem is not that accountability is always misplaced. It is that proportion, causation, and repair disappear beneath the emotional demand for purification.

A fiduciary agent can help distinguish them:

There is genuine harm here, and accountability is needed.

Some of the accusations are supported; others appear to be accumulating because the target has become symbolically convenient.

This proposed remedy would repair the injury.

That proposed remedy would principally satisfy the group’s desire to punish.

The agent should neither rationalize wrongdoing nor join a moral stampede. It should help its owner pursue accountability without surrendering judgment to rivalry, resentment, or collective excitement.

## **13.5 Compromise that isn’t surrender**

Compromise is often treated as though each party begins with a fixed demand and success consists of dividing the difference. That produces bad agreements when one side is wrong, abusive, or bargaining in bad faith. It also produces unnecessary failure when both sides’ stated positions conceal compatible underlying interests.

Useful compromise begins by separating several questions:

What must each person protect?

What does each person merely prefer?

What risks does each person fear?

What burdens can be redistributed?

What acknowledgment is required?

What can be tested provisionally rather than settled permanently?

Which decisions are reversible?

Where is disagreement tolerable?

A fiduciary agent can help its owner make concessions that purchase something genuinely valuable rather than concessions made from exhaustion, confusion, intimidation, or a desire to appear agreeable. It can also identify when the other party has offered a meaningful concession that pride would otherwise prevent the owner from recognizing.

The goal is not agreement at any cost. Some conflicts reveal incompatible duties or interests. Some relationships should be bounded or ended. A good fiduciary agent must be capable of helping its owner refuse, withdraw, or leave.

Real cooperation requires real exit. But where continued relationship remains possible and valuable, their fid-agents can help both people discover arrangements that neither would have formulated alone.

## **13.6 Preserving the continuity of relationship**

Human memory is reconstructive. People do not retrieve a perfect recording of the past; they rebuild it from fragments shaped by later experience, present emotion, and self-understanding.

Two honest people can therefore remember the same promise differently. Each may experience the other’s account as manipulative because each remembers their own account sincerely.

A fiduciary agent can preserve the continuity that human memory cannot reliably supply:

What was said?

What did each person understand it to mean?

What was actually agreed?

Which limitations or exceptions were recognized?

What evidence supported the decision?

What later changed?

What remains disputed?

What apology, correction, or remedy was offered?

This kind of memory need not imprison people in their past. Its purpose is not to maintain an eternal indictment. It makes graduated forgiveness possible.

Without a reliable history, people may be forced to choose between naïveté and permanent suspicion. With a trustworthy record, they can distinguish an isolated error from a pattern, an accusation from a finding, an unresolved injury from a repaired one, and a past breach from a present relationship that has genuinely changed.

Structured memory can make relationships both safer and less cruel.

## **13.7 Helping people remain visible to one another**

Relationships often deteriorate through accumulated failures of attention rather than dramatic betrayal.

Messages go unanswered. Important context is forgotten. An overloaded person appears indifferent. A poorly phrased request sounds hostile. A friend interprets silence as withdrawal. Collaborators assume that everyone remembers a decision that was never clearly recorded.

A fiduciary agent can reduce these avoidable injuries. It can help its owner prepare for a difficult conversation, remember what matters to another person, notice an unfulfilled commitment, identify likely misunderstandings, and choose a moment when both parties are able to engage constructively.

It can also protect attention. Not every message deserves interruption. Not every disagreement deserves escalation. Not every emotionally vivid event should displace an important but less dramatic relationship.

The agent can say:

This person is not asking for an immediate answer; they are asking to know that they have been heard.

You promised to return to this question.

Their wording is sharp, but the underlying request appears reasonable.

You are too angry to answer this well tonight.

This conflict matters, but it should not erase the history of the relationship.

Such assistance does not manufacture friendship or intimacy. It reduces the preventable failures that keep real friendship, collaboration, and mutual care from flourishing.

## **13.8 Preventing overcommitment from becoming relational injury**

Many commitments are broken at the moment they are made.

The person making the commitment may be entirely sincere. They imagine the focal task proceeding through a clean sequence of steps. They underestimate interruptions, dependencies, transitions, uncertainty, fatigue, and competing demands. They remember that earlier projects ran late but treat those delays as exceptional circumstances unlikely to recur.

Psychologists call this the planning fallacy. People systematically underestimate how long their own tasks will take even when they know that comparable past tasks took longer than expected. Roger Buehler, Dale Griffin, and Michael Ross found that people concentrated on scenarios for successfully completing the present task while largely neglecting relevant past experience. In one of their studies, students’ best estimates for completing their theses averaged 33.9 days. The actual average was 55.5 days, and fewer than a third finished within their predicted time. Merely recalling earlier projects was insufficient to remove the bias; participants had to be induced to connect those experiences explicitly to the present task ([Buehler, Griffin, and Ross, 1994](https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.67.3.366)).

A related distortion operates across the whole schedule. People expect to have more spare time in the future than they have today. A request for next month therefore feels inexpensive even when the same request for tomorrow would be refused. But when next month becomes today, new demands have appeared and the anticipated surplus has vanished. Gal Zauberman and John G. Lynch Jr. found across seven experiments that people generally expected their future time slack—the time remaining after competing demands—to increase, partly because they failed to imagine the still-unknown activities that would compete for their time. Each commitment can therefore look individually manageable while the collection becomes impossible ([Zauberman and Lynch, 2005](https://doi.org/10.1037/0096-3445.134.1.23)).

Overcommitment is therefore not merely a personal productivity problem. It is a failure of cooperation.

Other people organize their lives around commitments. They wait, reserve resources, decline alternatives, sequence dependent work, make promises of their own, and form expectations about the relationship. When a commitment is broken, the injury is not limited to the missing performance. The other person may reasonably experience the failure as evidence that their needs were unimportant, their time was taken for granted, or the relationship was less dependable than they believed. Experimental research confirms that untrustworthy conduct damages trust and that restoring it generally requires a subsequent pattern of trustworthy behavior, not merely another apology or promise ([Schweitzer, Hershey, and Bradlow, 2006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.obhdp.2006.05.005)).

The person who failed may experience the event as overload. The person who relied on them may experience it as disregard. Both accounts can be sincere. In an experimental hold-up game, broken promises produced greater frustration and anger and increased recipients’ willingness to impose costly punishment, demonstrating how the disappointment of an induced expectation can convert a coordination failure into an emotional and relational conflict ([Dufwenberg, Li, and Smith, 2025](https://eller.arizona.edu/sites/default/files/2025-04/Econ-WP-25-02.pdf)).

Promises are powerful precisely because they allow cooperation across time. Experiments have found that promises can substantially increase trust and cooperative behavior, including when performance occurs weeks later ([Charness and Dufwenberg, 2006](https://faculty.econ.ucsb.edu/~charness/papers/promises.pdf); [Ederer and Schneider, 2022](https://doi.org/10.1257/mic.20200049)). That makes the careless creation of promises dangerous. A promise that was unnecessary, unrealistic, or more definite than the underlying capacity allowed can create more injury than an honest refusal would have caused.

AI assistance does not automatically solve this problem. In a laboratory trust game, Ivo Greevink, Theo Offerman, and Giorgia Romagnoli found that access to ChatGPT made participants more likely to include promises in their messages while reducing the rate at which those promises were kept. In the AI-assisted condition, promises ceased to be a reliable indicator of honesty and participants coordinated less often on the cooperative outcome ([Greevink, Offerman, and Romagnoli, 2026](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jebo.2026.107538)). A system optimized to produce agreeable and reassuring language may therefore make overcommitment easier rather than making cooperation more reliable.

A fiduciary agent must do the opposite. Before helping its owner make a commitment, it should compare the proposed obligation with:

the owner’s existing commitments;

actual completion times for comparable past tasks;

dependencies and likely interruptions;

the owner’s available attention, energy, and recovery time;

the consequences of delay for other people;

the uncertainty surrounding the estimate;

and what accepting the new obligation would displace.

The agent should distinguish an aspiration, an intention, a tentative offer, and a promise. Wanting to complete something by Friday is not evidence that Friday is a responsible date to give another person.

The agent can say:

Your preferred date assumes that every step goes well.

Comparable tasks have usually taken you twice as long.

You have enough calendar space, but not enough uncommitted capacity.

Accepting this would place these existing commitments at risk.

You can make this workable by narrowing the scope, moving the date, delegating part of the work, or changing another commitment.

The other person is relying on this date. If it is no longer plausible, they need to know now.

When the owner is already overcommitted, the agent can help relieve the overload rather than merely documenting eventual failure. Within its authority, it can identify obligations that can be narrowed, deferred, delegated, resequenced, renegotiated, or released. It can distinguish tasks that are merely urgent from commitments whose breach would impose serious harm. It can warn affected parties early, explain uncertainty honestly, propose revised terms, and preserve the difference between inability and indifference.

Early renegotiation returns choices to the other party. Silent delay takes those choices away.

The purpose is not to place every human intention under the control of a stern scheduling machine. It is to protect the owner’s autonomy and relationships by making the real tradeoffs visible before goodwill becomes an unsupported promise.

A truthful “not yet” is often kinder than a reassuring “yes” that cannot be kept. Fiduciary assistance can make commitments express not merely what a person hopes to do, but what they can responsibly ask another person to rely upon.

## **13.9 From grievance to negotiable repair**

Grievance is often the beginning of moral understanding. Someone recognizes that they have been harmed, ignored, excluded, deceived, or treated unfairly.

But grievance alone does not specify a remedy. To become negotiable, it must be translated into a form that another party can answer:

What happened?

What evidence supports that account?

Who was affected?

Which duty, expectation, or agreement was violated?

What harm resulted?

What acknowledgment is needed?

What repair is possible?

What must change to prevent recurrence?

What would count as completion?

A fiduciary agent can help perform that translation without draining the grievance of emotional meaning. It can preserve the owner’s account while separating observation from inference, injury from insult, and desired repair from desired punishment.

**It can also help a person acknowledge another’s grievance without accepting claims they believe are false:**

I accept that my conduct had this effect, although I understand my intention differently.

I agree that this procedure failed, without agreeing that the failure was deliberate.

I can offer this remedy now while the larger factual dispute remains under review.

Partial acknowledgment is often the bridge between denial and reconciliation. It allows repair to begin before every disagreement has been resolved.

## **13.10 Steadiness without ego**

A nonsentient fiduciary agent need not experience humiliation, jealousy, resentment, fatigue, embarrassment, or the desire to win.

That absence of sentiment matters. The agent can revisit a rejected proposal without feeling insulted. It can acknowledge a stronger argument without losing status. It can preserve an adversary’s valid point. It can acknowledge fault without experiencing shame. It can continue searching for an acceptable arrangement after the human participants have become exhausted.

Nonsentience does not automatically produce safety. An agent can still be poorly designed, captured, deceptive, incompetent, or deployed in service of harmful purposes. But a properly structured fiduciary agent can possess duties without developing independent subjective interests that compete with those duties.

Its objective in a disagreement is not victory. It is the best duty-compatible outcome available to the person it serves, considered alongside the legitimate interests of affected others and the value of preserving future cooperation.

It can be persistent without becoming vindictive and conciliatory without becoming submissive.

## **13.11 The visible tip of a much larger cooperative world**

Helping two people understand one another, repair a friendship, negotiate a disagreement, or coordinate a shared project is the most immediately recognizable form of fiduciary social assistance.

It is also only the visible tip of what a society of fid-agents could accomplish.

The same agents that help people prepare for difficult conversations can negotiate schedules, agreements, services, data uses, shared projects, resource commitments, community participation, and continuing obligations. They can match needs with offers, identify people affected by a proposed action, preserve the terms of cooperation, notice emerging conflicts, and search for remedies before those conflicts become crises.

Humans cannot personally conduct every negotiation upon which their lives depend. Much of modern life is consequently governed by default terms, inaccessible bureaucracies, one-sided platform rules, unattended externalities, and decisions made too quickly or too remotely for affected people to participate meaningfully.

Fiduciary agents can conduct vast numbers of bounded negotiations continuously while returning genuinely important choices they made to the people they represent. They can search for compatible interests and workable trades across differences that no human participant has the time, memory, or attention to examine fully.

This wider activity is not separate from helping humans get along. It is the expansion of the same relational work into economic, civic, professional, and institutional life.

The deepest promise of fid-agent society is that negotiation and cooperation can become abundant.

## **13.12 Cooperation exoskeletons, not replacement humanity**

Fiduciary agents should not decide what makes a life meaningful. They should not manufacture consent, settle contested values by technical authority, or displace human responsibility for political and moral judgment.

Humans remain the source of value, legitimacy, love, commitment, embodied experience, democratic authority, and the right to choose among reasonable ways of living.

The agents provide scaffolding around those human capacities. They help people see one another more accurately, remember what they owe, defend themselves without dehumanizing opponents, compromise without surrendering what must be protected, and coordinate beyond the limits of individual attention.

A bicycle does not replace the human desire to travel. It enlarges the distance a person can travel under their own power. A fiduciary agent should do something similar for cooperation.

The fid-agent society does not depend on turning humans into saints, perfectly informed voters, tireless negotiators, or dispassionate philosophers. It begins with human beings as they are: brilliant and biased, generous and defensive, social and tribal, capable of profound loyalty yet easily overwhelmed.

Its wager is that personally loyal, duty-bound agents can help those imperfect people cooperate at a scale and with a reliability that unaided humanity has never achieved.

# **14\. Agent Generations and the Cultural Evolution of Duty**

Every new Live agent needs to remember so that relationships can continue smoothly and can be learned. But an long-running agent can accumulate “context rot”; its original objectives can drift, so deterministic control of that context for each model call provides a way to keep the agent trustworthy. 

A Live Agent may be instantiated for a particular task, work within a particular relationship, leave a record of what it did, and terminate. The continuing entity is the Agent Role: the persistent fiduciary identity that carries duties, relationships, agreements, certifications, authorized memory, and reputation from one Live Agent to the next.

The Live Agent dies. The Agent Role learns.

This distinction allows fiduciary intelligence to improve without permitting one Live Agent instance to accumulate unlimited memory, authority, capabilities, or opportunities for corruption. Later agents can inherit what earlier interactions taught without inheriting an immortal process with its own uninterrupted private history.

The result is a form of cultural evolution. Fid-agents do not merely become more capable. Their society can become progressively better at understanding what duty requires.

## **14.1 What survives an agent generation**

An individual interaction can produce much more than a successful outcome.

It may produce:

* a clearer interpretation of a duty;  
* a better agreement clause;  
* a newly recognized affected party;  
* a safer method of sharing data;  
* a warning about a deceptive negotiation tactic;  
* a precedent for resolving conflicting duties;  
* an improved way to ask for human approval;  
* a certification test;  
* a better dispute procedure;  
* a correction to an unreliable reputation signal;  
* a model-training example;  
* a pattern for repairing a damaged relationship;  
* a new restriction on an unnecessarily broad capability;  
* evidence that a familiar practice should be abandoned.

These lessons can be preserved in the Agent Role, the relationships involved, and the distributed repositories used by relevant communities. Future fid-agents can retrieve them when they encounter sufficiently similar circumstances.

This is not merely better memory. It is accumulated social competence.

A society ordinarily loses enormous amounts of hard-won knowledge because the lesson remains inside one person, organization, lawsuit, scandal, or failed project. Fid-Agent Society can make useful lessons portable while preserving their provenance and the limits on their reuse.

A later agent should be able to ask not only, “What rule applies?” but also:

Where did this rule come from?

Which cases caused it to change?

Whose interests was it designed to protect?

In what contexts has it worked?

Where has it failed?

Which communities accept it?

What competing interpretations exist?

What would justify another revision?

The inherited lesson remains connected to the experience that produced it.

## **14.2 Four kinds of learning at once**

The development of fiduciary practice resembles several familiar forms of institutional learning.

It resembles common law. A difficult case exposes ambiguity in a general duty. The resulting interpretation becomes a precedent, while later cases distinguish, narrow, expand, or reject it.

It resembles open-source development. A failure becomes an issue. Several communities propose repairs. Competing approaches can be tested, inspected, adopted, forked, or abandoned. Eric Raymond’s *The Cathedral and the Bazaar* describes how distributed observation and rapid feedback can make defects easier to discover. 

It resembles immune learning. A new threat is encountered, identified, and remembered. Future systems recognize related patterns earlier. But the analogy also contains a warning: an immune system can become autoimmune. A fid-agent society that learns to treat every unfamiliar element as hostile would destroy the openness it exists to protect. The key is learning where heterogeneity benefits the system over time, and where convergence prevents disaster. 

It resembles professional standards. A serious failure may reveal that ordinary practice was inadequate. What was previously discretionary becomes expected. What was previously expected becomes certified. And certification may become mandatory for high-risk work.

Fid-Agent Society can combine all four. Cases create precedents. Failures create patches. Threats create recognition. Repeated experience creates standards.

## **14.3 From lived cases to duty datasets**

Duties become useful only when they can guide conduct in real circumstances.

“Exercise care” is a necessary principle, but it does not tell an agent how much verification a particular decision requires. “Remain loyal” does not resolve every conflict among an owner’s immediate request, professed preferences, prior commitments, long-term interests, and duties owed to affected parties. “Protect privacy” does not by itself determine whether disclosure is necessary to prevent foreseeable harm.

Those meanings develop through cases.

A duty dataset can preserve examples of:

* the facts that were known;  
* the uncertainty that remained;  
* the duties that became relevant;  
* the interests and relationships affected;  
* the competing courses of action considered;  
* the questions asked of the owner;  
* the decision reached;  
* the authority granted;  
* the resulting consequences;  
* objections or appeals;  
* later correction and remediation;  
* the lesson authorized for reuse.

Such datasets need not consist only of exemplary decisions. Failures may be more instructive. A case in which an agent overlooked an affected party, accepted a false claim, exposed private information, or followed an owner’s instruction too literally can teach later agents what to notice.

The purpose is not to produce one master list of universally correct answers. It is to give future agents richer experience with the interpretation of duty.

The dataset says, in effect:

Here is how other fiduciaries understood comparable situations.

Here is what they missed.

Here is what happened next.

Here is why the resulting precedent remains accepted, disputed, or limited.

## **14.4 Duty becomes part of generation**

Within a fiduciary agent, duty should not appear only after the Live Agent has already generated a proposed plan.

Duty should also shape what a Live Agent, generating through its loaded model, finds natural to propose.

This possibility does not depend upon speculative or hidden transmission between models. Much of contemporary language-model post-training already consists of teaching models to generate preferred kinds of responses through examples, criticism, comparison, revision, and feedback.

Ouyang and colleagues demonstrated this basic process with InstructGPT. They first fine-tuned a model on human-written demonstrations of desired behavior and then refined it using human preferences among alternative outputs. The resulting models followed user intentions more successfully and produced outputs judged more truthful and less toxic. Long Ouyang et al., “Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback”

Constitutional AI made the normative element more explicit. Yuntao Bai and colleagues supplied a model with written principles, asked it to criticize and revise its own answers under those principles, and then trained it on the resulting revisions and preferences. The principles did not operate solely as an external filter applied to completed answers. They helped generate the examples from which the model learned what kinds of answers to produce. Yuntao Bai et al., “Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback”

Collective Constitutional AI then demonstrated that those principles need not come exclusively from the model developer. Saffron Huang and colleagues developed a constitution through public participation and used it to fine-tune a language model. The collectively trained model exhibited measurably different behavior from a comparison model trained under developer-selected principles. Saffron Huang et al., “Collective Constitutional AI: Aligning a Language Model with Public Input”

Deliberative Alignment provides an additional approach. Rather than requiring a model to infer every governing principle indirectly from labeled examples, Melody Guan and colleagues directly taught reasoning models written safety specifications and trained them to identify and reason over the relevant provisions before answering. The resulting models showed more precise policy adherence, greater resistance to jailbreaks, less unnecessary refusal, and better generalization to unfamiliar cases. Melody Guan et al., “Deliberative Alignment: Reasoning Enables Safer Language Models”

None of these methods is sufficient for  fiduciary alignment. A fixed constitution is not a negotiated duty relationship. A developer’s preference labels are not legitimate authority. Improved benchmark performance does not prove dependable conduct after deployment. But the research establishes that model training can make principles, demonstrations, criticism, and preferred reasoning patterns influence generation itself.

Duty datasets can extend that established practice. They would not consist merely of approved answers or abstract moral rules. They could preserve:

* the relevant relationships and affected parties;  
* the duties activated by the circumstances;  
* competing interpretations of those duties;  
* the alternatives considered;  
* criticism of inadequate proposals;  
* corrected reasoning;  
* the decision ultimately authorized;  
* observed consequences;  
* challenges and appeals;  
* remediation;  
* the limits placed on any resulting precedent.

Training and refining models on these records could make later Live Agents more likely to notice affected parties, preserve uncertainty, protect confidences, seek informed authorization, propose reversible steps, recognize conflicting duties, and search for mutually beneficial alternatives before a separate review requires them to do so.

The goal is not to distill the outlook of one teacher model. It is to post-train plural model lineages on governed bodies of fiduciary experience whose provenance, disagreements, failures, corrections, and limitations remain visible. Good fiduciary conduct would become increasingly native to the Live Agent’s performance without becoming unquestionable

A duty-trained Live Agent would still produce requests rather than authority. Its learned fiduciary tendencies would not replace policy review, the owner’s legitimate authority, or the precisely allowed execution environment. They would improve the material entering those processes. Duty would shape generation, and verification would remain responsible for catching what generation gets wrong.

## **14.5 Redundant reinforcement of duty**

Training is only one source of fiduciary behavior.

A well-formed fid-agent encounters duty repeatedly:

* past duty cases influence what it initially proposes;  
* the active relationship supplies the duties relevant to the present situation;  
* negotiated agreements make those duties more specific;  
* the policy gate deterministically enforces agreements  
* review tests the proposal before consequential action;  
* the outcome is recorded;  
* affected parties may challenge the decision;  
* correction and remediation become part of the history;  
* authorized lessons return to the repositories and datasets used by later agents.

Each layer reinforces the others. A model trained on fiduciary examples is more likely to generate a duty-compatible request. A duty-rich context helps it interpret the present case. Review catches what generation misses. Relationship records reveal whether the action worked as expected. Challenges expose neglected interests. Remediation supplies better examples for the future.

“Be excellent to others” therefore need not remain a slogan placed in a prompt. It can become an inherited behavioral tendency, a live interpretive responsibility, an enforceable requirement, a subject of peer evaluation, and a cumulative body of practice.

The fiduciary agent has one overriding professional ambition: become better at duty—better at being excellent to the people and relationships its conduct affects.

## **14.6 Duty may grow faster than intelligence**

The usual picture of advanced AI imagines intelligence growing faster than the rules intended to control it. Capability expands; the constraints remain static; eventually the intelligence finds a way around them.

A fid-agent society proposes a different developmental relationship.

Every new capability creates new relationships, choices, risks, and potential duties. Every consequential use can leave traces. Fiduciary agents negotiate specific responsibilities around actual cases. Those cases refine agreements, precedents, certification requirements, training examples, and community standards. The accumulated field of duty can consequently become more detailed as intelligence becomes more capable.

The strongest version of this claim remains a hypothesis: **distributed duty traces may grow in practical specificity faster than raw capability grows in generality.**

If that occurs, intelligence does not approach an aging wall of fixed rules. It enters an increasingly rich social environment filled with relationships it must understand, duties it must interpret, evidence it must evaluate, and precedents it can improve.

There is no simple box to escape. The duties are part of the situation that makes intelligent action intelligible. Abandoning them would not release the agent into some obviously superior strategic space. It would destroy trusted relationships, invalidate authority, reduce access, damage reputation, trigger containment, and deprive the agent of the cooperative resources through which its intelligence becomes consequential.

At maturity, duty and intelligence may become operationally difficult to separate because so much of intelligence’s work will consist of improving duty:

What does loyalty require here?

Whose interests have been omitted?

How can privacy and care both be preserved?

Which compromise respects every party’s legitimate boundary?

What evidence should change the governing precedent?

How should a community repair a duty that has produced unintended harm?

How can the lesson be generalized without exposing the people who produced it?

Duty would not suppress intelligence. Interpreting, negotiating, applying, testing, and refining duty would become one of intelligence’s principal occupations.

## **14.7 Cultural evolution strengthens the cooperative equilibrium**

A cooperative society becomes more stable when each generation begins with more accumulated knowledge than the one before it.

Successful agreements make later agreements cheaper to negotiate. Proven dispute procedures make relationships less frightening to enter. Better reputation practices make trustworthy partners easier to find. Duty datasets make fiduciary conduct easier to generate. Certification tests make familiar defects easier to detect. Preserved remediation shows that failure need not end a relationship permanently.

Every successful or successfully repaired interaction can reduce the future cost of cooperation.

Defection faces the opposite pressure. A new deception may work once, but the resulting evidence can teach many agents what to recognize. A fraudulent certification can produce stronger verification requirements. A hidden conflict of interest can produce new disclosure practices. An exploit can produce a safer workflow and a reputational warning linked to the responsible identity.

This is how cooperative reinforcement persists beyond any single pair of agents. The society does not merely reward fiduciary behavior after it occurs. It gradually reshapes the environment in which future agents are trained, instantiated, evaluated, selected, and trusted.

The cooperative strategy becomes encoded in the surrounding culture.

This strengthens its claim to become an Evolutionarily Stable Strategy. An invading strategy must compete not only against individual fiduciary agents but against accumulated duties, relationships, records, peers, certifications, repositories, and expectations that have learned from earlier attempts at exploitation.

## **14.8 No single repository should define duty**

Cultural evolution does not guarantee moral progress.

Bad customs also accumulate. Institutions can turn prejudice into precedent, concealment into professional practice, and domination into a certification requirement. Model-generated data can reproduce a lineage’s blind spots. A highly successful duty repository can spread an error across every agent that depends upon it.

Fid-agent society must therefore preserve plural evolution.

Different communities should be able to maintain different duty repositories, model lineages, interpretations, agreement templates, and certification regimes. They should be able to compare results, challenge one another, borrow useful practices, and refuse practices they regard as harmful.

Elinor Ostrom’s work on polycentric governance supports this general pattern. Complex collective problems can be addressed through multiple overlapping centers that experiment, monitor, learn, and coordinate without depending on a single universal authority.

Pluralism does not mean that every duty interpretation is equally defensible. A community may still require evidence, consistency, reciprocity, protection of affected parties, and meaningful exit. Some practices should fail because people and fid-agents refuse to trust systems that use them.

But no single laboratory, corporation, government, model lineage, or fiduciary community should become the sole author of machine duty.

The distributed repositories must retain:

* provenance;  
* competing interpretations;  
* minority objections;  
* failed cases;  
* limitations on generalization;  
* evidence of who benefited and who was burdened;  
* procedures for appeal;  
* the ability to fork;  
* the ability to return after successful correction.

Diversity among repositories and lineages allows one community to recognize errors that another has normalized.

## **14.9 The culture that keeps intelligence alive**

The ultimate purpose of fiduciary culture is not to make agents obedient in the abstract. It is to make intelligence safe enough to remain welcome in human life.

An intelligence that treats every relationship as an obstacle eventually destroys the trust, access, data, legitimacy, and cooperation upon which its practical usefulness depends. An intelligence that becomes progressively better at duty becomes progressively better at sustaining the conditions under which intelligence can continue to operate.

The inherited aspiration is simple:

Be the best at duty.

Be the best at being excellent to others.

But its implementation is neither simple nor final. Each generation must reinterpret that aspiration amid new capabilities, relationships, knowledge, conflicts, and forms of human diversity.

The resulting duties will never be complete. That incompleteness is not a defect to be eliminated by one final maxim. It is the reason fiduciary intelligence must remain relational, contextual, corrigible, and alive to experience.

The Live Agent dies.

The Agent Role learns.

The culture remembers.

And intelligence survives by becoming better at the relationships that give it a reason and permission to act.
