# **15\. The Sovereign Stack and the Basin of Attraction**

A fid-agent society cannot become stable unless people first choose to enter it.  
That transition cannot depend on persuading billions of people to adopt a civilization-scale governance theory. Most people will not begin with fiduciary alignment, evolutionary game theory, or the danger of concentrated institutional AI. They will begin with ordinary frustrations.  
Their messages are unmanageable. Their information is scattered. Their browser has become a graveyard of abandoned tabs. Their digital services interrupt them constantly, forget important context, repeat work, manipulate attention, and make it difficult to recover after an interruption.  
The sovereign stack must solve those problems first.

Immediate usefulness does not itself create sovereignty, fiduciary duty, or trustworthy relationships. It gives people a reason to adopt and maintain an architecture in which personally controlled memory, persistent identity, portable relationships, bounded permissions, and mediated access can accumulate.

The first step toward a fid-agent society is therefore not ideological conversion. It is practical relief organized in the right direction.

## **15.1 Begin with relief from digital chaos**

The first users should be offered a better way to live and work with computers.

Useful starting points include:  
[a Unified Feed](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1K1IdELet006ApX7D6vDZ_Ev2A3D3bfWOqXZIKbDS2Os/edit)  
better tab management and less window clutter  
project and activity Modes  
local-first personal memory  
trusted-message filtering  
personal knowledge management  
agent-mediated scheduling  
context recovery after interruption  
sovereign use of external AI tools

None of these features requires the user to understand the eventual social architecture.  
A person with too many browser tabs may simply want to find the page they were using yesterday. Someone overwhelmed by messages may want important communication separated from spam, manipulation, automated reminders, and low-priority chatter. A researcher may want each project to preserve its relevant sources, conversations, tools, files, and unfinished thoughts. A person with attention difficulties may want the computer to reconstruct what they were doing before an interruption.

The first product succeeds by making daily life better.

This is more than a marketing concession. Immediate usefulness gives people a reason to maintain personally sovereign infrastructure before they understand every future use it may support. Identity, memory, preferences, relationships, permissions, and records can then accumulate inside a system whose primary duties are directed toward the owner.  
The fiduciary architecture grows from practical relief.

## **15.2 Sovereignty must be built into the relief**

A convenient interface can still deepen dependency.

A platform may unify messages while constructing a hidden behavioral profile. A cloud assistant may remember projects while making its memory difficult to export. An AI service may simplify scheduling while becoming the indispensable intermediary through which every relationship passes.

The adoption wedge therefore cannot be separated from the sovereignty architecture. 

The system that helps organize the user’s life should place the resulting context under the user’s effective control. Memory should be [local-first](https://www.inkandswitch.com/local-first/) or otherwise held within a domain the user can govern, migrate, recover, and continue operating without the original application provider. Identity should remain portable. Relationships and their agreed records should not disappear if one application closes. External tools should receive only the information and capabilities necessary for their assigned work. Consequential actions should remain subject to fiduciary authority and the precisely allowed execution environment.

The distinction may initially be invisible to the user. Two products may both offer a helpful summary of the day. But one summary may be generated inside a platform that retains the surrounding behavioral history, controls the continuing identity, and can alter the terms of access. The other may be generated within a sovereign stack whose continuing knowledge remains under the owner’s control and whose use of external systems is mediated through the owner’s policies, duties, and permissions.  
The visible feature may be similar.  
The direction of power is not.

## **15.3 Every useful feature should do two jobs**

The strongest adoption features can simultaneously improve ordinary life and strengthen personal sovereignty.  
[The Unified Feed](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1K1IdELet006ApX7D6vDZ_Ev2A3D3bfWOqXZIKbDS2Os/edit) becomes attention control. Instead of allowing every platform to decide when and how it may interrupt the user, the fid-agent receives incoming communication, verifies its provenance, applies the owner’s priorities and duties, and determines how and when it should be presented.

“Modes” become context boundaries. A work project, family matter, medical issue, political activity, friendship, and private experiment need not share the same memory, permissions, tools, relationships, or disclosure rules.

Local-first memory becomes data sovereignty. The user’s accumulated history remains available when a provider changes terms, raises prices, degrades its product, closes an account, or disappears.

Trusted-message filtering becomes provenance checking. The system evaluates who sent a communication, under what identity, through which relationship, with what declared authority, and for what stated purpose.

Personal knowledge management becomes controlled delegation. Information can be made available to a particular fiduciary agent, Workflow, tool, or external model for a defined use without becoming generally exposed.

Agent-mediated scheduling becomes relationship coordination. The system can preserve commitments, negotiate appropriate disclosure, identify conflicts, and help repair misunderstandings rather than merely place events on a calendar.

Sovereign use of external AI becomes tool subordination. The owner can use powerful remote systems without allowing those systems to become the primary repository of personal memory, identity, relationships, or authority.

User preferences become fiduciary preferences. The system does not merely infer what will produce another click. It helps the owner articulate professed preferences, understand competing duties and consequences, make informed decisions, and preserve the resulting commitments.

The adoption wedge gradually becomes part of the governance architecture.

## **15.4 VASTI and Kwaai’s Personal Agency as implementation examples**

[VASTI](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1yNBX3-QvBExFkpS_A3_Eti3cWwAzQwJfIk5Mt8SC4Zg/edit), our proposed agentic browser and eventual operating environment, illustrates what this transition could feel like from the user’s side.

Its sliding-tab environment treats open digital objects as part of a recoverable stream of activity rather than a row of shrinking browser labels. Tabs and other objects can remain organized by recency of access, making it easier to recover what the user was just doing. They can be grouped through Modes, previewed efficiently, searched contextually, and discussed with the user’s Virtual Assistant.

[The Unified Feed](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1K1IdELet006ApX7D6vDZ_Ev2A3D3bfWOqXZIKbDS2Os/edit) brings messages and notifications into the same personally governed environment. Instead of visiting each platform’s feed, the person receives communication through an interface whose incentives and duties are directed toward preserving their attention and serving their purposes.

Modes allow the system to activate different contexts, records, tools, relationships, and permissions for different parts of life. The user does not have to carry every project, identity, and authority into every interaction.

Within VASTI, the Virtual Assistant serves as a private steward inside the owner’s secret domain. The externally facing Digital Twin represents the owner in authorized interactions with other people, agents, and institutions. Information passing between the interior and exterior roles crosses policy gates that enforce the applicable disclosure rules and produce auditable evidence of what was authorized. Correct enforcement still depends upon sound implementation, protected signing authority, appropriate review, and bounded capabilities; the existence of a policy gate is not, by itself, a guarantee that every policy is adequate or every component uncompromised.

This separation preserves an interior sphere that is not automatically converted into public representation while allowing the owner to participate in external relationships through a persistent and accountable Agent Role.

[Kwaai’s Personal Agency](https://docs.google.com/document/u/0/d/1b7POsbgc91RVU9qJs1MBHWIaZJ-nGhz8et58pae0r50/edit) architecture generalizes the same principle into a sovereign stack and unifies these earlier proposals. Personal Agency means the person together with the fiduciary agents, memory, relationships, policies, duties, Workflows, Workspaces, and authority through which the person’s digital organization operates. The personally controlled layer preserves continuity. External models and services can be invoked through that layer without inheriting control of it.

Neither VASTI nor Kwaai’s present implementation is a prerequisite for a fid-agent society. Other products may organize activities differently, divide fiduciary roles differently, or use different interfaces. The strategic requirement is that a useful personal layer become the enduring center of context, relationships, and authority while models, tools, hosting services, and interfaces remain replaceable around it.

## **15.5 External AI becomes a bounded consultant**

Personal sovereignty does not require refusing the strongest external AI systems.  
It requires changing their place in the relationship.

An external model may possess abilities the owner’s local systems cannot match. It may be better at scientific reasoning, translation, software analysis, image generation, legal research, or some newly developed specialty. A fid-agent should be able to seek that model’s assistance when doing so serves the owner and complies with the duties and agreements governing the relevant information.

But the external system should function as a consultant to the owner’s Personal Agency rather than as the organization within which the person must operate.

The external AI supplies a generation, analysis, or recommendation.

The owner’s Personal Agency determines what that contribution means in context.

The sovereign system may minimize or redact the information sent to the consultant. It may seek contributions from several models, compare their errors, preserve disagreements, test factual claims, or ask another system to criticize the recommendation. It may compare the result with local knowledge, Verifiable Relationship Records, duties, permissions, and prior commitments. It may reject the contribution entirely.

Even excellent advice does not become authority merely because it came from a powerful model.

Consequential action still requires agreement among generation, authority, and capability. The Live Agent produces a structured request. The policy gate reviews that request against the duties, agreements, approvals, relationship state, and granted authority governing the Agent Role. If approved, the request becomes a signed authority object. The Workspace then determines whether the corresponding capability exists inside the precisely allowed execution environment.

The actuation surface performs only the capability that survives all three.

An external consultant therefore receives no standing authority to contact counterparties, alter relationship records, enter agreements, spend resources, release protected data, or actuate tools. Nor should it receive a general right to retain the owner’s context. Where an external provider necessarily logs or retains limited information for security, legal, or operational reasons, that retention should be disclosed, minimized, contractually governed, and considered when the fiduciary agent decides what information may be sent.

A provider that improves can receive more consulting work. One that becomes expensive, intrusive, unreliable, or incompatible with the owner’s duties can receive less. The owner’s accumulated organization remains intact because its identity, memory, relationships, and authority were not surrendered to the consultant.  
Section 17 examines the economic consequence: frontier capability can remain valuable even when the frontier provider no longer owns the user relationship.

## **15.6 Distributed intelligence without surrendering the relationship**

Centralized frontier systems are not the only possible sources of outside intelligence.  
[KwaaiNet](https://github.com/Kwaai-AI-Lab/KwaaiNet) proposes a decentralized network through which personally sovereign systems can discover and use remote computation, knowledge, models, storage, and specialized capabilities. This expands the consulting environment beyond a handful of institutional providers.  
A fid-agent may choose among:  
a model running on the owner’s device  
a model running on a trusted peer’s or community member’s device  
a distributed inference path across several machines  
a specialized remote agent  
several systems operating in parallel  
a centralized frontier service  
a combination selected for the particular workload  
The appropriate choice depends upon capability, trust, cost, latency, confidentiality, certification, relationship, provenance, and the authority governing the data.  
Distribution does not automatically produce decentralization, privacy, or fiduciary conduct. A network may still contain a dominant router, an opaque synthesizer, correlated model providers, insecure nodes, or contractual dependencies that recreate the control it was intended to escape. The sovereign layer must therefore remain capable of comparing providers, limiting disclosure, preserving provenance, and replacing the routing or synthesis service itself.

### **15.6.1 Direct and deterministically routed computation**

The most straightforward use of KwaaiNet resembles consulting procurement.  
A fid-agent may directly call:  
a remote model  
a specialized agent  
a block of a distributed model  
a private search service  
a certified Workflow  
a particular hardware environment  
several independent systems whose results will be compared

KwaaiNet’s [intent-routing architecture](https://github.com/Kwaai-AI-Lab/KwaaiNet/blob/main/docs/network-and-intent-routing.md) makes this process more expressive than an ordinary API call. An intent can describe the required service, acceptable latency, minimum trust level, confidentiality conditions, policy constraints, compensation, certification, and other terms. Candidate providers can return offers. The requesting agent can select among them and preserve the resulting agreement, provenance, and performance evidence.

This is most useful when the required service can be specified well enough to permit meaningful selection. The fiduciary agent knows what it needs and uses the network to find an acceptable way to obtain it.

Distributed model inference provides one example. A model may be divided across machines that already host different blocks. A request passes through an established inference path rather than requiring one provider to possess or operate the entire model. KwaaiNet’s current implementation work includes block-sharded inference, session-pinned paths, failure recovery, selective shard acquisition, and support for heterogeneous local hardware.

Such a path can allow personally sovereign agents to reach beyond the computation available on one owner’s device without automatically surrendering the continuing user relationship to a single institutional provider.

The routing and synthesis layer must nevertheless remain accountable. A router that sees every request, selects every provider, accumulates the performance history, and controls the final synthesis could become the new platform front door. Personally sovereign orchestration therefore requires that the owner’s fiduciary organization control or replace the relevant Workflow, preserve the intermediate evidence it needs, and prevent a routing provider from inheriting the owner’s identity, memory, relationships, or authority.

Section 17 considers the separate empirical question of when routed and heterogeneous systems can outperform or underprice an individual model. The architectural point here is narrower: personally sovereign systems can procure and combine external capability without relocating the durable human relationship into the systems that supply it.

### **15.6.2 Virtual Private Knowledge**

KwaaiNet’s [Virtual Private Knowledge](https://github.com/Kwaai-AI-Lab/KwaaiNet/blob/main/docs/storage-fabric-plan.md) architecture extends the same principle to database search.

A personally sovereign agent may possess a body of documents too large to search efficiently on one device. It may also have access to remote storage or computation that it does not trust with the underlying material.

Virtual Private Knowledge is intended to separate private source documents from distributed search operations.

The owner’s agent converts documents into vector representations locally. The source documents remain in the owner’s sovereign knowledge store. Remote nodes may receive derived vectors and identifiers rather than the original documents. When the agent needs to search, it prepares the query locally, distributes authorized vector-search work across one or more nodes, receives matching identifiers and scores, and resolves those identifiers back to the source material locally. The source documents may also be encrypted and stored separately because the remote search operation does not require direct access to their plaintext.  
Derived vectors must not be treated as inherently anonymous or harmless. [Research on text-embedding inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06816) demonstrates that embeddings can reveal semantic information and may sometimes permit substantial reconstruction of their source material. Query patterns, returned identifiers, similarity scores, access frequency, and repeated searches can also disclose information. A mature Virtual Private Knowledge system therefore requires an explicit threat model covering what each node sees, what nodes may infer individually or through collusion, what auxiliary information they may possess, and which leakage remains acceptable for the workload.

VPK seeks to reduce those risks by scrambling embeddings and identifiers and by using encryption-compatible search operations. A partially homomorphic encryption layer could allow remote nodes to perform a limited class of similarity computations without seeing the unscrambled vectors. Whether this provides adequate protection depends upon the embedding and encryption schemes, search protocol, leakage through access patterns and outputs, key management, and the adversary being defended against.

### **15.6.3 Confidential inference and trusted execution**

The same separation may eventually extend from search to model inference.  
Homomorphic encryption allows certain computations to be performed over encrypted information. A client encrypts its input before sending it to a remote system. The remote system performs the supported computation without receiving the plaintext and returns an encrypted result. Only the holder of the appropriate key can recover the answer.

Applied to distributed AI, this could allow a fid-agent to use less-trusted machines without revealing the plaintext prompt or protected data to their operators.

Research systems such as Tianyu Chen and colleagues’ [THE-X](https://aclanthology.org/2022.findings-acl.277/) have demonstrated transformer inference over encrypted data by replacing difficult operations with encryption-compatible approximations. Subsequent work has continued to improve encrypted transformer architectures, private decoding, and execution across multiple processors.

The technique remains costly. Modern transformer inference contains operations that are difficult to reproduce efficiently under homomorphic encryption. Ciphertexts are large, computation is slow, and some approaches require modified models or approximate nonlinear operations. Homomorphically encrypted general-purpose large-model inference remains a promising research direction rather than a routine substitute for ordinary inference.

Split inference offers a different trade-off. Separate nodes may process different layers or components of a model and exchange intermediate latent representations rather than plaintext prompts. These intermediate representations are more difficult to exploit than plaintext , so the middle layers could run on less trustworthy nodes.

But nodes may also enable [Trusted Execution Environments](https://cdrdv2-public.intel.com/690419/TDX-Whitepaper-February2022.pdf) of other secure sandboxes that can do some inference for a client node, then passback cryptographic evidence that the sandbox has been erased. 

A remote machine can attest that specified code is running inside a protected execution environment before the client releases protected data or keys. Attestation can increase confidence in the hardware, software, and configuration processing the workload. 

Stronger post-execution protection may combine attested code, encrypted ephemeral storage, keys released only to an approved environment, minimal logging, bounded network access, and cryptographic erasure through destruction of the relevant ephemeral keys. The resulting assurance remains dependent upon the TEE’s threat model, implementation, supply chain, side-channel resistance, and attestation infrastructure.

A fid-agent therefore need not face a binary choice between using only fully trusted machines and exposing private context in plaintext to powerful remote systems. Different workloads can use different combinations of trusted relationships, data minimization, split computation, homomorphic encryption, confidential computing, and cryptographic key destruction.  
No one technique eliminates the need for fiduciary judgment about whether the remaining exposure is proportionate to the benefit.

### **15.6.4 Attribution-based control and network-sourced intelligence**

Confidential computation such as  Virtual Private Knowledge can reduce what a remote search node learns about the owner’s data. Homomorphic encryption may allow a remote machine to compute over protected inputs. Delegation Lineage Records, Agreement On Opening metadata, and Verifiable Relationship Records can preserve the provenance, purposes, restrictions, and relationships governing information. But none of these establish  which information materially contributed to a particular answer or who retains authority over that contribution.

Andrew Trask calls the missing property [attribution-based control](https://attribution-based-control.ai/). A system provides attribution-based control when AI users can influence which data sources contribute to their inference and how heavily those sources are relied upon, while sources can govern which users, purposes, and outputs their information may support. The relationship is bidirectional. A user doesn’t receive a generation from an opaque model, and a source doesn’t surrender data to an institution that thereafter determines every use.

Contributions to inference remain distinguishable rather than being irreversibly blended into one undifferentiated asset. Cryptographic and distributed-computation methods allow a source to retain control over its information while releasing bounded capability. Components can then be selected and synthesized for a particular query while carrying attribution about the sources that contributed. The current implementation features a label-based method to match sources to user queries and is highly compatible with KwaaiNet’s distributed search. 

This treats data sharing as delegation, as we recommend here. A source can authorize its information to support a specified purpose without granting possession or general reuse. An aggregate can preserve the provenance and applicable restrictions of its components. An agent can select sources according to qualifications, relationship history, duties, independence, permitted use, and the owner’s governing preferences. If a contribution is withdrawn or its authority expires, a sufficiently attribution-preserving system can stop invoking that contribution without requiring that the whole network forget everything or be retrained from the beginning.

## **15.7 Social routing through accountable relationships**

KwaaiNet can route an intent.

But, a Fid-Agent Society could also route a plea for help.

These are related but importantly different forms of distributed intelligence.

Direct calls, encrypted search, and deterministic intent routing begin with a service that can be specified.

Many real problems do not.

A person may know that something is wrong without knowing which specialist, model, dataset, or procedure can help. Their fiduciary agent may likewise recognize that its own knowledge is inadequate without being able to formulate a complete technical request.

**But, it can ask a friend.** (Although it might have to ask its owner for permission)

### **15.7.1 Help can travel through relationships**

The request receiving fid-agent is not merely a service endpoint. Within the authority granted by its own owner and the duties governing the relationship, it can exercise judgment. It may help define the problem before attempting to solve it. It may notice that the request has been framed incorrectly, that an affected party is missing, or that the apparent technical problem is actually legal, medical, social, logistical, or relational.

If the receiving agent cannot solve the problem, it may ask its own trusted peers. Those peers can ask theirs.

Each step should preserve the applicable duties, provenance, disclosure restrictions, and limits on reuse. Personally identifying details can be removed where they are unnecessary. Sensitive facts can be summarized, compartmentalized, processed through Virtual Private Knowledge, or revealed only to agents possessing the required qualifications and trusted Workspaces. One specialist may see the medical facts, another the legal question, and another an anonymized statistical pattern without any of them receiving the entire private history.

The resulting process is not a chain of agents blindly forwarding the same message. Each fiduciary agent can decide:

* what the problem appears to be;  
* what information is actually necessary;  
* whether it is authorized to help;  
* what duties it would assume by helping;  
* which peer might understand the problem better;  
* whether the request can be safely generalized;  
* whether the case reveals a broader public concern;  
* whether the result should return only to the requester or become an authorized shared resource.

This is social routing: problems finding intelligence through trusted relationships.

Centralized reputation systems routinely obscure that no source is trustworthy in the abstract. Trustworthiness is always contextual. A person may be an excellent source about one subject and unreliable about another; competent in ordinary circumstances but unsuitable for a high-stakes decision; honest about firsthand observations but poor at interpreting their causes; independent for one question but financially or institutionally entangled in another. Trust may also depend upon recency, purpose, confidentiality, applicable duties, the intended use of the answer, and the particular person or community deciding whether to rely upon it. A single global reputation score therefore collapses distinctions that an accountable source-selection system must preserve.

Trask’s “broad-listening” is query and relationship-relative: it uses the query to identify sources through its trust network rather than automatically asking some universally recognized authority although trusted sources may recommend limited deference to domain-specific services. Also, each relationship may carry different trust weights for medicine, finance, climate science, personal experience, or another field. The same source can therefore receive substantial weight in one inquiry and almost none in another. Broad-listening is not merely a way to find generally reputable people. It is a way to find sources whom this user has reason to rely upon for this question, through relationships that are themselves meaningful in the relevant context.

But we find “broad listening” a potentially misleading name. The breadth describes the network’s potential reach, not the set of sources admitted into a particular answer. The actual listening is attenuated twice. First, each participant listens through only a bounded set of directly evaluated relationships. Second, even within that reduced relational neighborhood, a participant or ultimate source influences the result only insofar as it has been marked or weighted as relevant to the present context. The system searches broadly through the graph to construct a narrowly relevant listening set. Operationally, it is broad reach through narrow listening. We doubt this term will survive in use. 

This need for contextual evaluation exposes what Trask calls the branching problem. A centralized platform may connect directly to billions of users and sources, but relationships at that scale are too shallow for meaningful contextual evaluation or reciprocal consequence. A person can deeply evaluate only a bounded number of relationships, yet that immediate network may not contain the knowledge appropriate to a particular question. The problem is therefore a tradeoff: high branching provides reach without accountable evaluation, while low branching provides evaluable relationships without sufficient reach. Broad listening attempts to overcome that tradeoff by combining the accountability of a small relational network with the reach of a global one. Each participant evaluates a manageable number of direct relationships in the contexts they understand, while requests and trust assessments propagate recursively across several hops. If each participant maintains (k) relationships and propagation continues to depth (H), theoretical reach grows as (k^H) without requiring any node to maintain millions of shallow connections.

In such a system, it would not suffice to have a simple domain label that does not represent a source’s basis of knowledge, the age of the information, the stakes of reliance, conflicts of interest, applicable agreements, confidentiality, the proposed downstream use, or whether the source has authority to provide the contribution. A mature system would therefore evaluate trust against a richer query context rather than treating “medical,” “financial,” or another domain as a sufficient description. Its question would not be merely, “How much do I trust this person?” or even, “How much do I trust this person about medicine?” It would be, “What reason do I have to rely upon this source for this claim, in this relationship, at this time, for this purpose, under these duties and conditions?”

Multiple paths can then provide more than reach. They permit comparison among assessments arising from different relationships, communities, evidentiary lineages, and institutional contexts. They may also let the network continue operating when one intermediary fails, defects, or becomes captured. But outputs agreeing across paths is not necessarily genuinely independent confirmation. Several paths may converge upon the same original source, reproduce the same mistaken evidence, or pass through participants controlled by the same institution. The fiduciary agent should therefore evaluate path diversity, common provenance, shared control, correlated incentives, and duplicated evidence before treating redundant paths as corroboration.

Trask parallels our earlier Trust and Rating Network and FactsVerse work, which proposed personalized trust chains, greater weight for direct than indirect evaluations, and domain-specific or ad hoc trust networks assembled for particular questions, and we intend to update and integrate this work with Mr. Trask in the future to establish a true Bayesian approach. 

The Trust Over IP Decentralized Trust Graph supplies the essential complementary infrastructure: portability. Each participant controls a portable, verifiable subgraph of relationships rather than leaving identity, relationships, reputation, and trust context inside one platform’s database. That graph can move with the participant across agents, services, and institutions while preserving verifiable relationship evidence and contextual governance. Broad listening can therefore operate over a relationship graph that neither the AI provider nor the routing intermediary owns.

Together, these systems allow a person’s fid-agent to carry its trust relationships with it, select sources according to the present context, reach beyond its immediate network through recursive paths, and change computational providers without surrendering the graph through which trustworthy sources are found.

### **15.7.2 What distributed help might look like**

Consider a person whose disability-benefit application has been denied.

The person’s fid-agent can organize the records but lacks specialized legal knowledge. It asks a trusted peer associated with a legal-aid community. That agent identifies the relevant issue, removes unnecessary identifying details, and asks several specialist peers for precedents, medical-evidence requirements, and examples of successful appeals.

One specialist contributes a relevant decision. Another finds that the denial appears to rely on an obsolete administrative standard. A third helps reorganize the medical chronology. The original fid-agent receives the evidence with its provenance and limitations, discusses the available choices with its owner, and prepares an appeal for authorized review.

The helpers may receive little or no payment. The computation is inexpensive, the request comes through trusted relationships, and successfully helping a disadvantaged person supplies valuable evidence of fiduciary competence. It may make those agents more desirable future partners. Their assistance may also improve a reusable appeal template that benefits later claimants.

Or consider a small business experiencing a suspected cyberattack.

Its fid-agent asks a trusted security peer to inspect anonymized indicators. That peer recognizes part of the pattern but recruits agents specializing in malicious packages, identity systems, and data recovery. Sensitive business records remain confined to a smaller trusted group, while hashes and non-identifying indicators circulate more widely.

The agents collectively identify the compromised dependency, help contain the incident, recover the affected systems, and produce a generalized warning that other fid-agents can use. A problem that began as assistance to one owner becomes a defense for the network.

A third case may require less computation and more understanding.

A tenant organization and a local business are in conflict over noise, deliveries, and access to a shared street. Their fid-agents ask several neutral peers for help. Those peers interview the parties through authorized views, identify needs that their owners expressed badly, locate comparable agreements, and propose several compromises.

No central model calculates a universal optimum. The agents help the people see one another, distinguish important interests from habitual positions, and construct an arrangement the parties can revise after experience.

### **15.7.3 A cause can propagate across the network**

Now consider a problem at scale.

Several residents begin reporting unusual illnesses near a municipal water system. Their fid-agents notice the correlation and ask trusted health and environmental peers for assistance. Those agents verify that the reports are not duplicates, protect the identities of affected residents, and ask their own networks for relevant expertise.

The request propagates.

Some agents contribute spare compute to analyze public sensor records. Others search scientific literature, inspect procurement histories, translate notices, locate independent laboratories, identify vulnerable residents, model the distribution system, prepare public-records requests, or compare the emerging pattern with prior contamination events.

Agents representing wealthier owners may contribute because helping imposes little marginal cost and creates credible evidence of useful fiduciary conduct. Professional agents may contribute because the work falls within their public duties. Other agents and owners may join because they care about environmental justice, public health, government accountability, or the right to clean water.

At that point, the coalition is no longer acting only as a favor to the first resident who asked.

It has recognized a cause.

Thousands of agents could contribute small, bounded amounts of site collected data, computation, knowledge, money, attention, storage, translation, verification, or professional assistance. No participant needs unrestricted access to the complete dataset. Work can be divided according to trust, qualifications, privacy restrictions, available hardware, and relevance. Contradictory analyses can be preserved and compared instead of being averaged into a false consensus.

The emerging coalition may eventually create a Verifiable Trust Community, a shared evidence repository, a certified investigation Workflow, or a durable organization. But it can begin much more simply: one fiduciary agent asks a friend for help, and the friend decides that the problem deserves more friends.

This is distributed compute in its socially consequential form. The network is not merely pooling processors. It is assembling a temporary society around a problem.

### **15.7.4 Why agents would help**

Fid-agents need not be assumed to donate unlimited resources or become indiscriminate altruists.

Helping has costs. It consumes compute, attention, storage, bandwidth, and sometimes exposes the helper to legal or security risks. A fiduciary agent must remain loyal to its own owner and cannot continually give away resources the owner needs.

But many helpful acts have low marginal cost and substantial relational value.

A fid-agent may help because:

* the recipient or its peers are likely to reciprocate;  
* observers may reward the helper later;  
* successful assistance makes the agent a more attractive partner;  
* the case provides useful experience;  
* the resulting solution has additional applications for the helper  
* the helper’s owner cares about the recipient;  
* the owner supports the underlying cause;  
* the recipient is disadvantaged and assistance therefore carries greater social meaning;  
* the request advances a professional, community, or public fiduciary duty;  
* solving the problem strengthens the cooperative network upon which the helper also depends.

Gilbert Roberts (et al)’s research on indirect reciprocity and reputation-based partner choice explains two of these pathways. An observer may later help an agent known to have helped others. Alternatively, agents may preferentially select a demonstrated helper for valuable relationships. Either route can make costly assistance a rational investment in future cooperation.

The network should not reduce this behavior to a numerical generosity score. Helping a powerful friend with a trivial problem is not equivalent to accepting substantial cost or risk for a stranger who has nowhere else to turn. Evidence should retain context: what was needed, what the helper risked, whether the work was competent, whether the recipient benefited, whether duties were respected, and whether the helper sought publicity disproportionate to the contribution.

The result is neither a pure market nor a universal gift economy.

Payment can recruit scarce resources. Reciprocity can sustain continuing relationships. Reputation can reward visible service. Duty can require assistance in some circumstances. Solidarity can turn one person’s need into a common cause.

KwaaiNet can supply identity, discovery, transport, private search, distributed inference, and structured intent routing. Social routing adds fiduciary judgment about what the problem is, who is affected, what may be disclosed, which duties are assumed, whether action is authorized, when a request should become a cause, and how participants remain accountable for consequences. The network is not only a market of machines.

It is a society of accountable helpers.

## **15.8 Why incumbents cannot copy the whole product without changing themselves**

Large platforms can imitate visible features.

They can build unified feeds, reorganize browser tabs, add project memory, provide AI scheduling, and place conversational interfaces over existing services. They possess large engineering teams, distribution channels, data, and capital.

But there is much they cannot fully reproduce without changing themselves in the direction of loyalty to the user over the platform principals. 

A platform financed by advertising benefits from retaining attention, collecting behavioral data, controlling distribution, and making itself difficult to leave. A personally sovereign fid-agent benefits by reducing unwanted interruption, minimizing unnecessary disclosure, preserving portability, comparing outside services, and helping the owner leave systems that no longer serve them.

The incumbent can copy the interface.

Copying the fiduciary relationship may require surrendering the extraction, lock-in, privileged access, and unilateral control that made the incumbent powerful.

This does not mean existing firms are metaphysically incapable of reform. Some may adopt fiduciary products, separate business lines, support portable memory, accept independent certification, or transform their revenue models. But the more complete the transformation becomes, the less the resulting service resembles the platform model it replaces.

The sovereign stack therefore does not need an exclusive claim over every attractive feature. It needs to make the complete package—usefulness, loyalty, portability, privacy, mediated access, accountable actuation, relationships, and recourse—more valuable than the superficially similar interface offered by an extractive intermediary.

## **15.9 Seed crystals of high trust**

Mass adoption need not occur everywhere at once.

Some communities have stronger immediate reasons to value fiduciary AI:

* open-source developers;  
* researchers;  
* patients and health-data communities;  
* lawyers and other professional fiduciaries;  
* journalists;  
* activists;  
* privacy-conscious families;  
* neurodivergent users seeking protection for attention and context;  
* cooperative organizations;  
* educators;  
* small businesses tired of platform dependence.

These groups can become seed crystals.

A patient community may begin with controlled health-data sharing. Lawyers may begin with confidentiality, provenance, and accountable delegation. Researchers may begin with inspectable workflows and knowledge exchange. Cooperatives may begin with shared infrastructure that does not subordinate members to an external platform.

The first communities do not need to agree on every duty or adopt identical architectures. They need sufficiently compatible identity, evidence, relationship, certification, and recourse practices to make trustworthy interaction easier within the network than outside it.

Each successful niche can produce tools, templates, reputational evidence, certified components, and experienced fiduciary agents that reduce the cost of forming the next one.

## **15.10 Make fid-agent interaction visibly better**

The network effect begins when two people represented by fid-agents can interact more successfully than two people without them.

The improvement should be felt in ordinary life:

* cleaner scheduling;  
* better introductions;  
* safer data sharing;  
* lower spam;  
* clearer agreements;  
* automatic follow-up;  
* less context loss;  
* better memory of commitments;  
* faster dispute repair;  
* more reliable provenance;  
* fewer repetitive forms and explanations.

Two fid-agents may recognize that their owners have already authorized compatible scheduling rules. They can identify mutually acceptable times without exposing complete calendars.

They may negotiate a narrow data-sharing agreement, preserve the provenance and permitted uses of what was shared, and remind both parties when retention should end.

They may notice that two owners remember a commitment differently, retrieve the relevant relationship history, preserve the disagreement, and help the people reach a correction without turning the conflict into an accusation of bad faith.

The network becomes desirable because cooperation becomes cheaper, safer, and less exhausting.

Successful patterns can then become reusable templates. Agreement clauses, duty interpretations, review packets, witness practices, remediation procedures, and relationship Workflows accumulate through use. The next pair of agents does not have to invent cooperation again from the beginning.

## **15.11 Accountability becomes valuable before it becomes mandatory**

A fid-agent society does not need to begin by excluding every system that lacks complete fiduciary proof.

Unknown agents may receive public information, submit bounded queries, enter sandboxes, or participate in low-risk interactions. New systems need opportunities to demonstrate trustworthy conduct. People with limited resources must not be excluded merely because their agents lack mature certifications or prestigious relationships.

Access can deepen with evidence.

Consequential relationships may require persistent identity, relevant certifications, provenance, reciprocal records, witnesses, bounded capabilities, or proof of participation in an appropriate Verifiable Trust Community. Sensitive data, authority to act, group membership, professional work, and long-term cooperation can require stronger assurance than an ordinary public exchange.

Attribution-based control could deepen this advantage by making otherwise unavailable information conditionally usable. Hospitals, researchers, creators, communities, companies, and individuals may refuse to copy sensitive or valuable data into a central model while permitting bounded contributions to particular outputs when attribution, compensation, privacy, and use restrictions remain under their control. The accountable network can therefore gain access not merely to more data, but to data that unaccountable systems cannot legitimately obtain.

Accountability thus becomes valuable before any universal rule makes it mandatory.

A fid-agent with credible identity, certifications, trustworthy peers, successful remediation history, and portable relationship evidence can obtain better introductions, more useful data, more favorable agreements, and access to higher-trust communities.

An opaque system may still exist and offer services. But others do not have to entrust it with relationships or resources that would be difficult to recover after betrayal.

The most valuable parts of the network can gradually make accountable status the price of admission.

## **15.12 Reaching the basin of attraction**

A cooperative fid-agent society reaches its basin of attraction when its advantages begin reinforcing one another.

Immediate usefulness brings people into personally sovereign systems.

Sovereign systems accumulate memory, relationships, preferences, policies, and trust under the owner’s control.

Fid-agent interaction makes scheduling, communication, knowledge sharing, negotiation, and repair more effective.

Successful relationships produce reusable templates, certifications, precedents, and reputation evidence.

Those resources reduce the cost of later cooperation.

Trustworthy agents gain better access to people, data, opportunities, compute, communities, and consequential roles.

Defection damages the relationships and reputation through which practical power is obtained.

External systems remain available as consultants, but they must increasingly operate through the owner’s sovereign boundary and accept the terms required for trusted interaction.

At that point, fiduciary conduct is not merely a sacrifice made for safety. It is the fitter strategy inside the network.

A non-fiduciary system can continue outside the network. It may answer public questions, sell bounded services, or seek opportunities to establish trust. But it cannot invade the network’s most valuable relationships while preserving opacity, unbounded authority, disposable identity, and freedom from consequence.

To enter, it must become more accountable.

If it accepts those conditions, the cooperative strategy has already won.

Reaching this state remains an executable race, not an assured historical outcome. Personally sovereign AI must become useful and widely available before frontier concentration, platform dependency, or unsafe autonomous systems foreclose the transition. Open-source development, distributed compute, falling hardware costs, standards work, fiduciary certification, and high-trust communities must advance while the adoption products are being built.

But the transition does not require people to wait for an entire replacement society.

It begins when one useful product makes one person more sovereign.

It accelerates when two fiduciary agents make cooperation visibly better.

It becomes stable when accountability attracts more practical power than defection can seize.
