# **16\. The Unified Feed and the Reversal of the Attention Market**

The advertising model that financed the consumer internet assumes that people will continue scrolling through feeds on platforms, looking at web pages, and responding to whatever appears beside the content they wanted.

Personally-sovereign AI weakens that assumption.

As fiduciary agents increasingly retrieve information, compare products, summarize discussions, manage communication, and negotiate with vendors for their owners, fewer human eyes need to visit the underlying platform. The agent does not become bored while waiting for a page to load. It does not click a sensational headline merely because it is curious. It does not need to scroll past ten promoted posts to reach the information its owner requested.

An advertisement shown only to an agent is not an impression in the traditional commercial sense. The agent has no eyeballs to capture.

Vendors will still need ways to reach people. But their most valuable route may no longer pass through a platform that has accumulated behavioral data and auctioned the resulting access. It may pass through the person’s own fiduciary representative.

That reverses the attention market.

## **16.1 The advertising model depends on human exposure**

The modern advertising platform performs several functions at once.

It attracts people by hosting search, entertainment, communication, social relationships, news, or useful software. It observes what those people do. It predicts what may influence them. It then sells advertisers a place inside the resulting stream of attention.

The user supplies the attention and much of the data.

The advertiser supplies the money.

The platform controls the relationship between them.

This model remains enormously profitable. [Alphabet reported](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1652044/000165204426000018/goog-20251231.htm) that online advertising generated more than seventy percent of its 2025 revenue. [Meta reported](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1326801/000162828026003942/meta-20251231.htm) approximately $196.2 billion in advertising revenue against approximately $201 billion in total revenue for the same year. These are not marginal business lines. Advertising supports the infrastructure, engineering, acquisitions, investor returns, and market power of some of the largest consumer technology platforms.

The model works because humans still approach information through surfaces the platforms control.

A person opens a social-media application to see what friends have posted. They search for a product through a search engine. They read an article on a publisher’s site. They watch a video inside a feed. In each case, the intermediary can insert paid material into the person’s field of view.

A fid-agent changes the path.

Instead of opening five shopping sites, the owner asks for suitable shoes. Instead of checking six social networks, the owner consults a Unified Feed. Instead of reading dozens of pages, the owner’s agent searches, retrieves, compares, verifies, and summarizes. The human may see only the useful result and the sources necessary to evaluate it.

Early AI-mediated search already demonstrates part of this effect. A [2025 Pew Research Center study](https://www.pewresearch.org/short-reads/2025/07/22/google-users-are-less-likely-to-click-on-links-when-an-ai-summary-appears-in-the-results/) found that Google users presented with an AI summary clicked an ordinary search result in eight percent of visits, compared with fifteen percent when no AI summary appeared. Links inside the summaries themselves received clicks in only one percent of visits.

When an intermediary answers the user’s question before the user visits the source, fewer human visits reach the sites where conventional advertisements would have been displayed. When the intermediary belongs to the platform, however, fewer downstream visits do not by themselves give the user more control. The platform may retain or even strengthen its control over which sources, offers, and actions the user sees.

The conversational (chat) interface also compresses available options. A conventional search or shopping journey may expose a person to several pages, promoted listings, retargeting opportunities, and surrounding advertisements. An agent may collapse the same journey into one answer or a small set of recommendations. This reduces the number of opportunities to sell and makes each paid placement more consequential, because fewer alternatives may remain visible.

If the intermediary becomes the user’s own fiduciary agent, the platform may lose both the visit and control of the intermediary.

## **16.2 Agents are not valuable advertising audiences**

An advertiser can try to direct persuasion toward a fiduciary agent.

It can produce machine-readable product descriptions that omit disadvantages. It can pay for favorable placement in a commercial search service. It can create thousands of apparently independent recommendations. It can optimize language to exploit known model tendencies. It can offer commissions to agents that steer owners toward particular products.

Fid-agent-mediated communication does not make mass manipulation disappear, but it makes it less effective.

But a genuine fid-agent has a different reason for processing commercial information than a human browsing an advertising platform. Its duty is not to reward whatever presentation captures its attention. It is to determine whether the offer is relevant to the owner, whether its claims are supported, whether material conflicts are disclosed, and whether the proposed transaction serves the owner’s interests and duties.

The word “genuine” matters. A provider-controlled shopping assistant, search assistant, or personal companion may be highly capable while remaining structurally incapable of undivided loyalty.

The true fiduciary agent can ignore decorative persuasion and compare underlying terms.

It can retrieve reliability records, total ownership costs, return policies, repairability, privacy implications, labor practices, prior complaints, and evidence from the owner’s trusted network. It can ask competing vendors to improve their offers. It can hide unnecessary personal information. It can identify when a recommendation service receives compensation. It can present uncertainty and disagreement instead of pretending that one product is objectively best.

Yet conventional disclosure does not reveal every form of influence. A provider may label a visible advertisement while leaving the sources, training contributions, retrieval choices, rankings, defaults, and commercial integrations that shaped a recommendation opaque. A model may cite documents placed in its context without establishing that those documents materially contributed to the answer, and it may draw upon undisclosed training sources whose influence cannot be separated after the fact.

[**Attribution-based control**](https://attribution-based-control.ai/) could make this influence more inspectable and governable. The owner’s fid-agent could select or weight the sources permitted to contribute to a commercial recommendation, require provenance for the contributing components, exclude sources with undisclosed compensation, and compare results generated from differently constituted source sets. Data sources could likewise refuse to support recommendations, advertisements, or vendors incompatible with the conditions under which their knowledge was made available.

Most importantly, a fid-agent will cast its intent to buy to its network and filter the offers it receives.  

A vendor may still influence the human through brand, beauty, humor, status, affection, or identification. People do not purchase only through formal comparison, and a fiduciary agent should not flatten every expressive choice into instrumental optimization.

But the vendor must first reach a person who has delegated substantial control over incoming communication to an agent duty-bound to protect that person’s attention. Rather than sending that filtering agent a finished advertisement, the vendor’s agent may instead work with the user’s agent to construct an offer the owner is likely to value. The substance, terms, presentation, and price of access (see 16.4) can all be negotiated before anything reaches the Unified Feed.

## **16.3 The Unified Feed can remove the platform’s privileged position**

The Unified Feed brings email, various app messages, social posts, notifications, news, commercial offers, and other incoming communication into one personally governed stream.

The underlying services may continue to exist. A person may still use a social network, subscribe to a publication, participate in a forum, shop from a marketplace, or maintain several professional identities. But those services no longer automatically control when their information appears or what surrounds it.

The externally-facing agent (Digital Twin) retrieves or receives the material, then using generative logic and policy gates:

It verifies the sender and provenance.

It determines whether the communication is authorized and relevant.

It passes appropriate material into the Unified Feed with annotation for the interior Virtual Assistant to use to prioritize it.

Then the Virtual Assistant continuously reprioritizes it according to the owner’s present context, duties, relationships, commitments, and explicit instructions.

The platform becomes one source among many.

This distinction threatens more than advertising placement. A platform’s commercial advantage comes partly from its ability to combine identity, behavioral observation, social relationships, ranking, communication, and advertising inside one environment. The Unified Feed separates those functions. The owner’s sovereign stack holds the identity and continuing context. Fiduciary agents manage relationships and incoming communication. Independent services can provide hosting, search, content, payment, or compute without controlling the whole experience.

Platforms may try to resist by restricting access, degrading APIs, prohibiting agent retrieval, or demanding that humans remain inside proprietary interfaces. But vendors, creators, publishers, communities, and users will have reasons to support more direct routes. A platform that prevents a customer’s authorized agent from discovering or purchasing a product may protect an advertising impression while losing the sale.

Once substantial numbers of people interact through fid-agents, the commercially important question for vendors changes.

It is no longer:

How do we place this message in front of the largest audience?

It becomes:

What terms will this person’s fiduciary agent accept for bringing this particular message to its owner?

## **16.4 A market for negotiated access**

In section 16.2, we mentioned the possibility of vendors paying users directly for access instead of paying for advertising. We will generalize that idea here.

A sender’s agent approaches the recipient’s fid-agent with a proposed communication. The two agents determine whether there is a version of the message that deserves the recipient’s attention, what that version should contain, how it should be presented, when it should appear, and what the sender should pay for access.

The agents negotiate both the message and its price.

### **16.4.1 The message itself is negotiated**

The sender’s initial communication need not be the communication ultimately shown to the recipient.

A fid-agent may ask:

Who is the sender?

What relationship or authority supports the contact?

What does the sender want the owner to know or do?

Why might it matter to this particular person?

Which claims can be verified?

What conflicts or compensation must be disclosed?

What information can be omitted?

What would make the communication shorter, clearer, safer, or more useful?

How urgently does it need to be seen?

What price is the sender offering for the requested access?

The sender’s agent can answer, revise the proposed communication, supply evidence, narrow its request, disclose interests, or increase its offer.

This can happen for many kinds of communication.

An unknown researcher may want to tell another researcher about a related result. Their agents can establish the relevant subject, exchange credentials, determine which evidence should accompany the message, and produce a concise explanation of the connection.

Someone seeking an introduction may allow their agent to explain the purpose of the proposed relationship, identify mutual contacts, and negotiate what the recipient wants to know before seeing the request.

A civic organization may want to reach people affected by a proposed zoning change. Its agent can establish geographic relevance, provide the underlying documents, disclose the organization’s position, and agree to a presentation that distinguishes reported facts from advocacy.

The recipient’s agent is not merely filtering a finished message.

It is helping construct a message its owner might actually want to receive.

### **16.4.2 Vendor agents negotiate complete offers**

Commercial offers provide the clearest example.

The process may begin with an intent cast. Suppose a person has authorized their fid-agent to announce an interest in purchasing an electric bicycle. The agent may disclose selected requirements: price range, commuting distance, storage limitations, desired delivery date, preferred riding position, repair preferences, battery needs, and the importance the owner places on domestic servicing.

Vendor agents respond with initial proposals.

The receiving fid-agent can work with the most promising vendor agents to improve those proposals. It may explain that an e-bicycle is too heavy for the owner’s stairs, that the offered battery is unnecessarily expensive, that the nearest authorized service location is inconvenient, or that the warranty does not cover the owner’s expected use.

The vendor agent determines what its organization is willing and able to change. It might substitute a lighter configuration, reduce the battery size, arrange delivery upstairs, include a nearby independent repair shop, extend the warranty, guarantee replacement-part availability, alter financing, or improve the price.

The agents may continue until they have assembled the best offer that vendor can make for that owner:

the appropriate product configuration;

an individually negotiated price;

useful accessories rather than an indiscriminate bundle;

suitable delivery;

financing compatible with the owner’s preferences;

appropriate warranty and repair terms;

privacy and data-use commitments;

return rights;

a decision period suited to the owner’s actual schedule.

The fid-agent may conduct several such negotiations at once. It can tell each vendor what deficiencies remain without revealing another vendor’s confidential terms or disclosing personal information its owner has not authorized it to share.

Vendor agents compete by improving their offers rather than purchasing more opportunities to repeat the same message.

What eventually reaches the Unified Feed may still be attractive, expressive, and persuasive. The owner may want photographs, demonstrations, colors, testimonials, stories, or an explanation of what distinguishes the product. Fiduciary mediation does not abolish aesthetics or the pleasures of choosing.

But the communication is no longer primarily an advertisement.

It is a substantially complete offer, with all the niggly terms sorted, that the owner’s own agent helped shape.

Individual negotiation also creates a danger: a vendor may try to discover the highest price this particular owner will pay. The fid-agent should therefore avoid revealing the owner’s maximum budget or urgency unless the owner specifically authorizes it, disclose as little personal information as possible, and compare competing offers whenever practical. Negotiation should improve the offer, not turn the owner’s private circumstances into a tool for extracting the highest possible price.

### **16.4.3 Relevance and access price are negotiated together**

The sender and recipient agents also negotiate the price of placing the resulting communication in the Unified Feed.

A communication that appears irrelevant, uncertain, or burdensome should cost more. The sender is asking the recipient to accept a substantial risk that the interruption will waste their attention.

A communication that the fid-agent already expects its owner to value should cost less.

A nearly perfect commercial offer may receive a substantial discount because the owner has declared the relevant intent and the agent has helped shape the offer. A well-supported message from a credible researcher may cost little because the apparent intellectual value justifies attention. A trusted friend, professional counterparty, or existing relationship may communicate for free.

The better the fit, the less the sender may need to pay.

The agents can negotiate:

whether the message will enter the Unified Feed at all;

how far down it will initially appear;

the conditions under which it will move upward (although the Virtual Assistant ultimately controls placement, it will usually honor agreed terms);

whether it is expected to be opened, and the estimated distribution of likely opening times;

whether a time-sensitive message warrants special treatment;

how much currency, tokens, or mutual credit held in escrow will transfer when the message is opened;

what sponsorship or other material relationships must be disclosed;

The recipient’s fid-agent can communicate an estimated distribution of opening times or a simpler half-chance-time: the point at which it expects a fifty-percent chance that its owner will have opened the message.

If the sender needs faster attention, its agent can offer more. The recipient’s agent determines whether its owner would want the additional payment to affect placement and then describes the expected effect.

The sender purchases a specified opportunity for attention, not agreement, endorsement, or action.

### **16.4.4 The Indiscriminate Price is the fallback**

Negotiation will not always be possible or desirable.

A sender may not want to explain the substance of a deeply personal message to the recipient’s agent. The importance of the communication may depend on shared history the agent does not understand. The sender may believe that the agent’s existing context will cause it to undervalue unfamiliar evidence. Or the sender may simply want the human, rather than the human’s representative, to decide whether the message matters.

The Indiscriminate Price preserves that route.

It is the published price at which an unknown or insufficiently trusted sender can place a communication prominently in someone’s Unified Feed without first persuading the recipient’s fid-agent of its relevance.

The message still undergoes review before placement. The recipient’s externally facing fid-agent verifies its origin and checks it for dangerous content, obvious fraud, malicious links, impersonation, prohibited material, and recognizable abuse. Payment does not create a right to deliver malware, threats, repeated harassment, or an obvious grift.

But once the message survives that bounded review and the sender pays the published Indiscriminate Price, the agent does not also require the sender to win an argument about importance.

Within the owner’s ordinary safety, capacity, and priority rules, the sender has purchased the right to ask the human.

Someone can contact a former friend after many years. An unknown witness can tell a journalist what they saw. An entrepreneur can pitch an idea to a person they have never met. A constituent can reach a public figure. A researcher can alert another researcher to a flaw. A neighbor can communicate an emergency. A person who believes someone is being deceived by their own information environment can place contrary evidence before them.

The fid-agent may attach a warning, provenance information, or an assessment of the sender. It may isolate an attachment or require safer delivery. But the owner has reserved the final judgment for themselves.

The Indiscriminate Price is therefore the exception to negotiated relevance.

It prevents personal agency from becoming personal seclusion.

### **16.4.5 Refunds and rewards preserve valuable unsolicited contact**

Payment need not remain with the recipient when the communication was genuinely welcome.

If an unknown sender pays the Indiscriminate Price to deliver information the recipient needed, the recipient can refund the entire amount. If the message prevented serious harm, revealed an important opportunity, supplied valuable evidence, or required unusual courage to send, the recipient may return the payment with an additional reward.

Before the message is opened, the Indiscriminate Price acts as a seriousness bond. The sender bears a cost because the recipient cannot yet know whether the interruption will be worthwhile.

After opening the message, the recipient decides whether that cost should remain.

A stranger warning someone that their escaped dog was seen near a highway may pay to ensure immediate placement. Once the owner reads the message and recognizes its value, they can refund the price and add a reward.

A whistleblower may pay a journalist’s high Indiscriminate Price because explaining the entire matter to the journalist’s agent would reveal too much. If the evidence is important, the journalist can return the payment, establish a trusted relationship, and remove the price of future communication.

An unknown founder may pay to pitch an investor. If the investor finds the idea useful, the price can be refunded even if no investment follows. If the pitch is generic and unwanted, the investor keeps the payment as compensation for the attention consumed.

A desired contact may therefore cost the sender nothing in the end—or leave the sender better off.

Refunds allow people to remain generous toward valuable unsolicited communication without surrendering protection against indiscriminate volume.

### **16.4.6 High-profile people remain reachable on honest terms**

Different people will set very different Indiscriminate Prices.

Someone who welcomes unsolicited ideas may set a modest price. Someone receiving too many unknown messages can raise it. A famous person may deliberately set an extremely high price because otherwise thousands of people would continually purchase access.

Even then, the person is not completely secreted away behind assistants, publicists, platforms, and opaque filtering systems. Anyone willing to pay the stated indiscriminate price can still place a safe communication before them.

The terms must be honest.

A famous person’s Digital Twin should tell prospective senders that the owner rarely refunds unsolicited contact if that is the established pattern. The sender should not be encouraged to treat the payment as a refundable bond when, in practice, the recipient almost always keeps it.

The Digital Twin might disclose:

the current Indiscriminate Price;

the percentage of recent payments that were fully refunded;

the estimated time before an accepted message will be opened;

whether particular categories of contact receive different treatment;

what safety review occurs before placement. (although the funds will be refunded if review fails)

The sender can then make an informed choice.

For an ordinary person, the Indiscriminate Price may function mostly as a refundable demonstration of seriousness. For a celebrity who intentionally uses a high price to suppress volume, it may function as the genuine expected cost of contact.

Both remain reachable.

No one need be absolutely inaccessible.

No one is cheaply interruptible.

### **16.4.7 Why people will not simply fake reading**

Compensation for opening is transferred from a temporary escrow when the recipient opens the communication. That creates an apparent vulnerability: someone might accept large numbers of paid messages, click them immediately, give them no genuine attention, and collect the payments.

The system should not answer this problem with eye tracking, biometric monitoring, or invasive attempts to prove that a person really read every word.

That would reproduce surveillance inside the mechanism intended to replace it.

Nor should the system require a recipient to release opening or response data. Those records remain inside the person’s sovereign stack unless the owner authorizes an agent to disclose something for a particular purpose. A person who does not want to provide evidence can simply ask vendors to take their word for it. A vendor can accept that representation, propose different terms, or decline. Nondisclosure is not evidence of abuse.

Instead, the system should rely on visible patterns, honest expectations, and future bargaining.

If someone claims to open thousands of paid messages every day, each claimed opening plainly represents very little attention. Vendors and other repeat senders will stop paying that person’s former prices.

The same is true when a person repeatedly opens commercial offers but never responds, requests more information, enters a transaction, or otherwise behaves as though any offer received meaningful consideration. No single outcome proves dishonesty. The person may simply dislike the offers. But an extended pattern makes their attention less valuable to future senders.

A recipient who abuses the system therefore consumes the reputation that allowed them to charge substantial prices.

However, a person who wants to maximize the price a vendor will pay for access may voluntarily authorize their fid-agent to disclose a narrow fact in negotiation: how often accepted communications are opened, how quickly they are usually opened, or how many other similar items compete for attention. It can use a [zero-knowledge proof](https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/pec/zkproof) or a selectively disclosed [verifiable credential](https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/) to prove only the agreed claim without identifying the messages, the other senders, or the recipient’s wider behavior.

Most vendors need not begin by suspecting deception. Most recipients have a strong reason to preserve the usefulness of their own Unified Feed and the future value of access to it. That incentive exists whether or not the owner chooses to prove anything.

Most people have a simpler reason not to fake opening: they want their Unified Feed to contain things they may genuinely value. Filling it with communications they intend to ignore would destroy the usefulness of their own sovereign interface.

The governing expectation is not that opening guarantees agreement or purchase. It is that when a person’s fid-agent accepts payment for placement and the person opens the communication, they give it an honest opportunity to matter. They may read a short message completely, inspect a longer one until it becomes irrelevant, or decide quickly that an offer does not fit.

No one needs to prove the direction of their eyes.

Trustworthy conduct preserves the future value of their attention.

### **16.4.8 The platform middleman loses its privileged position**

The negotiated market can cut the platform middleman out of the relationship between a vendor and a customer.

Instead of paying a search engine, social network, data broker, advertising exchange, and other intermediaries for a probabilistic opportunity to reach the customer, a vendor negotiates with the customer’s representative. Some of what would have become an advertising expense can go directly to the person whose attention is requested..

The vendor wastes less money addressing people unlikely to purchase. It gains an opportunity to learn—within the limits of authorized disclosure—what would make its product genuinely useful to this customer. The owner receives a better offer, less irrelevant communication, and some of the economic value created by granting attention.

The payment does not require the fid-agent to recommend the product.

It purchases a specified form of access: review by the agent, admission to a commercial-offers portion of the Unified Feed, or prominent presentation when the owner enters a context in which the offer is relevant. The negotiation for placement, the payment, and the effect that payment had on placement should be visible to the owner.

Even an apparently perfect offer may be rejected for reasons their agent did not anticipate, because the user prefers a less economical alternative, postpones the purchase, or decides not to buy anything.

The owner’s fid-agent remains responsible for deciding whether negotiated access serves its owner.

The Indiscriminate Price remains available when the sender wants the owner to decide without that negotiation.

## **16.5 This is not simply advertising with a new auctioneer**

A badly designed Unified Feed could reproduce the very attention economy it is intended to replace.

Wealthy senders could overwhelm poorer speakers. Fid-agents could learn to manufacture interruptions so their owners would accept payment to remove them. Owners could become dependent on selling attention. Commercial offers could receive priority over family, civic participation, or unfunded public-interest communication. A dominant Unified Feed provider could secretly favor its own advertisers and become another platform.

The system must therefore remain fiduciary before it becomes commercial.

Money is one input into prioritization, not the governing objective.

The receiving fid-agent must consider:

the owner’s expressed willingness to receive compensated messages;

the expected relevance of the communication;

the credibility and provenance of the sender;

the likelihood that the offer benefits the owner;

the cost of interruption;

the owner’s present context;

duties owed to existing relationships;

whether payment creates a material conflict;

whether repeated paid access is becoming manipulative;

whether the owner should be shown the payment and ranking rationale;

whether a lower-cost or uncompensated message is more important.

Some communications should never be auctioned. Emergency warnings, legal notices, relationship obligations, credible reports of harm, and communications from people to whom the owner owes duties require separate treatment.

Nor should the highest bidder automatically occupy the top of the Feed. The Indiscriminate Price establishes a default cost of requesting access, not a transferable entitlement to dominate attention. A fid-agent can refuse an offer that is harmful, deceptive, repetitious, or incompatible with its owner’s wishes.

This makes the market fundamentally different from an advertising exchange that serves the seller of impressions.

The fid-agent serves the person whose attention is at stake.

## **16.6 The Intention Economy: intent replaces surveillance**

Doc Searls gave this reversal a name in 2006: the [Intention Economy](https://www.linuxjournal.com/content/intention-economy). It grows around buyers rather than sellers. Instead of sellers attempting to capture customers and infer what they might want, buyers notify the market of an intention and invite sellers to compete for their business. Searls later developed this idea in [The Intention Economy: When Customers Take Charge](https://www.hbs.edu/faculty/Pages/item.aspx?num=42233) and through [ProjectVRM](https://projectvrm.org/), which seeks to equip customers with tools for independence from vendors and better engagement with them.

One of the mechanisms developed is intent casting: customers signal their needs directly to the market rather than waiting to be targeted by guesses and advertisements. The intention originates with the person. Technology should help the person express and pursue it;  not infer intent for someone else’s benefit.

Personally-sovereign fid-agents can provide much of the practical machinery that this vision requires. They can help their owners decide what they want, determine what information must be disclosed, select appropriate recipients, negotiate the content and price of access, compare responses, and protect the owner throughout the resulting relationship.

Shoshana Zuboff describes [surveillance capitalism](https://www.hbs.edu/faculty/Pages/item.aspx?num=56791) as the conversion of human experience into behavioral data used to predict and shape conduct for profit. [Richard Whitt](https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=3669914) summarizes the corresponding institutional sequence as SEAMs: Surveil, Extract, Analyze, Manipulate. A provider-controlled conversational agent can intensify that process because people disclose deliberate questions, uncertainties, plans, relationships, vulnerabilities, and intentions that ordinary browsing data could only less perfectly infer.

[A 2026 Stanford analysis](https://hai.stanford.edu/news/be-careful-what-you-tell-your-ai-chatbot) of the documented policies of six major United States AI developers found that all six used users’ chat data by default for model training or improvement. It also found that multiproduct firms could combine chatbot interactions with information from search, purchasing, social activity, and their other services. The institutional assistant can therefore become both a richer site of extraction and a more intimate channel through which analysis returns as influence.

Conventional targeted advertising attempts to infer what a person may want from accumulated behavioral data.

A fid-agent can intelligently interrogate its beneficiary to improve over relying on behavior alone. After investigating its owner’s true wants, the owner may authorize an “intent cast” stating that they are considering a purchase, seeking employment, looking for housing, planning travel, choosing health insurance, or searching for professional assistance. This call for offers, leads, collaborators, etc.,  can be narrow, temporary, and directed only toward appropriate vendors or communities.

The owner decides what demand information enters the market, where it goes, and how it is disclosed.

Vendors compete against a stated need instead of buying covert access to a predicted vulnerability.

The difference is substantial. Surveillance advertising begins with information extracted about the person and controlled by someone else. Fiduciary intent casting begins with information the person chooses to disclose through an agent obligated to protect them.

A person’s agent will choose which vendors receive an intent cast through utilizing data sources and trusted relationships approved by its owner. And trusted sources, agent or human, may also may be recruited to evaluate responses from vendors, particularly their ability to deliver. Commercial intelligence becomes a negotiated relationship among the person, the sources, and the vendors rather than an opaque prediction assembled and monetized by the platform that controls all three.

A vendor may request more context in exchange for a better offer. The fid-agent can determine whether the requested disclosure is necessary, whether its proposed use is bounded, whether the vendor’s identity and policies are credible, and whether the expected benefit justifies the exposure.

The resulting negotiation can produce a more useful offer with less personal data.

This does not end preference inference. A fiduciary agent will still use what it legitimately knows about its owner to determine relevance. But the inference occurs inside the owner’s sovereign system. It is used to protect and represent the owner rather than to assemble an audience for an outside seller or platform.

## **16.7 Publishers, creators, and communities also need a new bargain**

The decline of platform advertising creates a serious problem for people who produce information, art, journalism, entertainment, software, and public knowledge.

If agents retrieve and summarize their work without sending people to the source, conventional page views and advertising revenue fall. The content remains useful while the producer loses the income that supported its creation.

[Cloudflare’s analysis of AI crawlers](https://blog.cloudflare.com/ai-search-crawl-refer-ratio-on-radar/) has documented a widening gap between the pages AI systems retrieve and the human visitors they send back. Cloudflare has responded with tools that allow publishers to distinguish search, agent, and training crawlers, block unwanted access, or [experiment with charging AI crawlers](https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-pay-per-crawl/).

This is evidence that the old bargain is already weakening.

Negotiated access and fid-agent relationships suggest a broader replacement.

Attribution-based control could allow publishers and other sources to charge for authorized use of their information in producing a particular answer, without handing over a copy of the complete work. The source could limit that contribution to specified users and purposes while retaining attribution and agreed conditions. A global implementation could allow citation (without direct access to) of all journals and other sources hidden behind paywalls at a reasonable price based on requester. 

A creator may charge agents for access to protected work. A publication may offer certified summaries, quotations, evidence packages, or full-text retrieval under specified terms. A community may sell access to an authorized knowledge repository. Fid-agents may subscribe on behalf of their owners, compensate a source when its work materially contributes to an answer, or negotiate collective licenses through a Verifiable Trust Community.

A creator may also pay for Unified Feed placement to bring new work to people likely to value it. But the creator negotiates with those people’s agents rather than purchasing placement from a platform that controls both sides.

Communities can fund their own communication infrastructure by selling limited quantities of a community currency and charging a small slice of the transactions in it. Revenue can support moderation, curation, hosting, fiduciary review, public-interest work, and contributions by members.

The goal is not to make every human exchange transactional.

It is to allow money that currently flows through attention intermediaries to move more directly among the people and communities creating, receiving, and governing the communication.

## **16.8 Advertising will not disappear, but the double-agent trap is immediate**

Advertising will not disappear merely because agents become common.

Brand advertising may remain valuable because humans care about symbols, stories, aesthetics, and social meaning. Vendors will attempt to sponsor agent recommendations, influence training data, manipulate reputation, or become default providers inside popular sovereign stacks. Platforms may deploy their own agents and use existing identities, relationships, and distribution to retain users. Some people will continue to enjoy browsing public feeds.

Conversational advertising may be unusually valuable for chat-based services. A person discussing a purchase, trip, household problem, or business need has revealed more immediate intent than a person merely scrolling past a banner. That may make a commercial placement near or inside the conversation unusually likely to produce action.

But this advantage creates the trap. The closer an advertisement comes to the assistant’s advice, the harder it becomes to distinguish assistance from steering. A chat interface presents one integrated conversational relationship rather than a page containing many visibly separate links and advertisements. Sponsored influence therefore threatens the credibility of the answer, the agent, and the relationship at the same time.

[OpenAI has begun testing advertisements in ChatGPT](https://openai.com/index/testing-ads-in-chatgpt/) while stating that advertisements remain labeled and separate from answers. Its published description nevertheless says advertisement selection may use the subject of the current conversation, past chats, and prior interactions with advertisements. OpenAI has also described plans to expand formats, objectives, buying models, and ways for businesses to interact with consumers inside ChatGPT. This does not establish that advertisers presently alter ChatGPT’s answers. It does establish that commercial targeting is being brought inside the conversational relationship.

The system-level conflict extends beyond visible advertisements. Commercial incentives can affect which merchants receive integrations, which services become defaults, which transactions earn commissions, which tools the assistant invokes, and how options are retrieved or ranked. The model weights need not contain an instruction to favor an advertiser for the deployed agent to become commercially biased.

Users are not entering this transition with an established belief that provider-controlled agents are loyal to them. A [July 2025 YouGov survey](https://yougov.com/en-us/articles/52608-ai-shopping-assistants-are-catching-on-but-shoppers-still-need-convincing) found that forty-one percent of Americans did not trust AI shopping assistants at all, while only thirteen percent mostly or completely trusted their shopping advice. Thirty percent of those uninterested in the tools worried that the assistants would upsell unnecessary products. AI shopping advice was trusted slightly less than company-sponsored advertising.

The introduction of sponsorship confirms rather than creates those suspicions. A [2026 Harris Poll conducted for Quad](https://www.quad.com/newsroom/americans-say-they-would-lose-trust-in-ai-shopping-if-results-were-sponsored) found that [seventy-five percent of Americans would trust AI agents less if brand payments influenced their recommendations](https://www.quad.com/newsroom/americans-say-they-would-lose-trust-in-ai-shopping-if-results-were-sponsored), and the same percentage would trust brands less for paying to influence them. Seventy-three percent reported unease about how AI might use their shopping data. These surveys establish distrust, not that people will necessarily adopt or pay for a personally-sovereign alternative.

[Anthropic’s decision to keep Claude advertising-free](https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-is-a-space-to-think) acknowledges the conflict of interest from the opposite direction. It argues that conversational systems receive unusually personal context and that advertising incentives could work against an assistant acting unambiguously in the user’s interests. Advertising-free institutional service does not constitute personal sovereignty, but the reasoning concedes that commercial sponsorship and loyal representation are in tension.

The transition will also be uneven. Fid-agents must become competent enough to filter without concealing opportunities their owners would have valued. Standards must permit authorized access across services. Vendors need machine-readable ways to present offers and establish credibility. Publishers need payment and licensing mechanisms that are easier than simply allowing platform advertising.

The argument is not that every advertisement suddenly becomes worthless. Nor is it that sponsored chat placements cannot earn money. It is that revenue will be pressured by reduced placements, the exceptional intimacy of conversational data, the visibility of the resulting conflict, and the possibility that users can leave for agents that do not sell access to their decisions.

The economic value of an advertising impression depends on human attention remaining inside a controllable surface. As agents increasingly stand between people and those surfaces, the owner of the agent gains bargaining power while the owner of the surface loses some.

Platforms can respond by becoming useful infrastructure providers, identity services, hosting companies, content producers, transaction processors, or trusted communities. They may continue earning substantial revenue.

But the systemic privilege of owning the Feed begins to disappear.

## **16.9 The reversal reinforces the cooperative equilibrium**

The Unified Feed changes the flow of both information and money.

As more communication passes through fid-agents, conventional impressions become less valuable. Vendors can redirect some of their advertising budgets toward negotiated access. Owners and communities can receive part of the value formerly captured by platforms. Personally-sovereign systems can use that revenue to fund storage, compute, trusted services, public-interest assistance, and continued development.

Vendors gain a more direct route to people with relevant declared interests.

Owners receive fewer irrelevant messages and greater control over disclosure.

Creators and communities can negotiate compensation for work their agents use.

Fid-agents gain further reason to build credible reputations because trusted senders receive better access and trustworthy recipients attract better offers.

The same loop makes deceptive communication more expensive.

A vendor that repeatedly misrepresents products may face higher contact prices, stronger review, loss of access, or refusal throughout connected trust networks. A sender using disposable identities cannot easily inherit the favorable terms earned through responsible relationships. A platform that conceals sponsorship or manipulates agent recommendations risks exclusion from sovereign feeds.

Migration to self-ownership can also deprive unaccountable systems of some assets that sustain their advantage. When identity, memory, preferences, relationships, and private context remain in personally-sovereign stacks, provider-controlled assistants lose both intimate training or inference data and the privileged channel through which that data can be converted into commercial influence. Their advertisements become less precisely targeted, their recommendations control fewer transactions, and their ability to subsidize continued dominance weakens.

This is defection collapse operating through attention.

Unaccountable systems do not have to be universally prohibited. Their ability to purchase, seize, or manipulate human attention declines because a growing share of that attention is governed through agents that owe duties to the people receiving the communication.

The users attention ceases to be inventory held by a platform.

It is instead governed by the person and their fiduciary agents.
