# **17\. Bounded Power and the Economic Window for Personally-Sovereign AI**

Personal AI does not need to win the model power contest.

A personally-sovereign stack does not need to utilize the most capable general model in the world.

It needs to control the assets that make model capability useful to a particular person:

persistent identity;

personal memory;

current context;

fiduciary preferences;

duties;

relationships;

agreements;

trusted peers;

access permissions;

communication interfaces;

authority;

actuation.

These assets are difficult for an external laboratory to reproduce without becoming deeply embedded in the person’s life. Once they reside in the self-sovereign stack, the owner can change the model used for generation while preserving the organization around it.

Local and distributed systems handle routine work.

Specialized peers supply knowledge and compute.

External frontier systems provide consultation when their additional capability justifies the cost and disclosure.

The owner’s fiduciary architecture interprets and applies the resulting advice.

This allows personally-sovereign AI to benefit from frontier progress without requiring the frontier provider to own the person’s memory, attention, relationships, and authority.

The race is not between local models and institutional models.

It is between two organizations of intelligence.

One organizes the person around a platform.

The other organizes models and services around the person.

Capability is not power.

A model may be extraordinarily capable while possessing no durable identity, private memory, trusted relationships, signing authority, or access to actuators. Conversely, a modest model embedded within a well-designed sovereign stack may accomplish far more for its owner because it has legitimate access to the context, tools, relationships, and permissions relevant to the task.

This separation between model capability and system sovereignty creates an economic opening for personally-sovereign AI.

## **17.1 Incorporation, not competition**

The personally-sovereign stack does not need to defeat every frontier model on a benchmark.

It can incorporate their capabilities.

When a task exceeds local resources, the fid-agent can route a narrowly framed question to the best available outside model. It can minimize the raw data disclosed, compare advice from several systems, request criticism from another model, preserve the resulting provenance, and evaluate the recommendations against the owner’s circumstances and duties. The owner may use powerful external AI without giving it control of the owner’s organization.

The external AI remains a consultant. It does not become the owner’s organization.

Its advice enters the sovereign stack, where the owner’s fid-agents interpret it alongside private knowledge, prior decisions, trusted relationships, current goals, and governing policies. The external system cannot sign an agreement, release data, spend money, communicate as the owner, or actuate another system merely because its answer was persuasive. Those actions remain subject to legitimate authority and the Workflows and Workspaces controlled by the owner.

This lets personally-sovereign AI benefit from frontier capability without allowing the frontier provider to own the human relationship.

## **17.2 Combining models can produce frontier performance**

Recent results suggest that routing is not merely a way to select among interchangeable model providers. A Workflow can assign different parts of a difficult task to models with complementary strengths and combine their contributions into a result better than any one model would have produced alone. One model may plan the work, others may search for evidence, perform calculations, or pursue competing approaches, and still others may identify contradictions, verify intermediate conclusions, and synthesize the result. The resulting capability belongs to the organized system rather than to any single model within it.

[OpenRouter’s Fusion experiments](https://openrouter.ai/blog/announcements/fusion-beats-frontier/) illustrate the principle. The system gives a research problem to a panel of models and then asks another model to analyze their answers for agreement, contradictions, incomplete coverage, distinctive evidence, and blind spots before producing a final synthesis. On the DRACO deep-research benchmark, one heterogeneous panel exceeded every individual model tested. A panel of considerably less expensive models also approached the best individual result at approximately half its cost. Even multiple runs of the same model improved performance when their different research paths and source selections were compared and synthesized. These results support [Andrew Trask’s broader contention](https://andrewtrask.substack.com/p/6-weeks-ago-frontier-ai-labs-lost) that the most capable individual model and the most capable usable system need no longer be the same thing.

The empirical claim should remain bounded. The results come from particular benchmarks and orchestration methods; they do not show that combining models improves every task. Fusion also took substantially longer than a single model response, and a poor judge can discard correct minority conclusions or synthesize several errors into a more persuasive one. 

Also, agreement is not necessarily independent corroboration: different models may reproduce the same training data, sources, assumptions, or institutional biases. A multi-pass Workflow should evaluate provenance and diversity rather than treating the number of agreeing model outputs as sufficient evidence.

Current experiments may nevertheless understate the eventual advantage of orchestration because they generally combine models that were trained as general-purpose systems rather than for particular positions within a larger Workflow. Once difficult work is repeatedly decomposed into stable stages—planning, search, extraction, calculation, criticism, verification, and synthesis—demand should grow for small, fast models trained to perform one such stage exceptionally well. In [Small Language Models are the Future of Agentic AI](https://research.nvidia.com/labs/lpr/slm-agents/), Belcak and his colleagues argue that small language models are more economical and operationally suitable for many repeated agentic tasks, easier to specialize rapidly, and natural components of heterogeneous systems. [TinyAgent](https://aclanthology.org/2024.emnlp-demo.9/) provides a concrete example: small models fine-tuned for function calling exceeded GPT-4 Turbo on that narrow task while permitting real-time local operation.

The resulting architecture resembles a distributed mixture of experts operating at the Workflow rather than the token layer, a [mixture of agents.](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04692)  A conventional mixture-of-experts model routes each token among expert parameter groups contained within one model. A Workflow-level mixture routes whole subtasks and intermediate results among separately trained—and potentially separately owned, operated, governed, and compensated—models, or possibly sub-agents that maintain context for their subtask. The experts need not share a model architecture, provider, training lineage, or physical location. Their common membership consists only in the role each can perform within the Workflow.

[Mixture-of-Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04692) found that heterogeneous model outputs contributed more than repeated outputs from the same model and that an individually weaker model could improve the answer produced by a stronger aggregator. [Agentic Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11399) extends selection to individual steps in an execution harness, choosing one model for efficient execution or several complementary models when ensemble accuracy justifies the expense. In this paper’s terminology, that selection would be conditioned upon the state of the Workflow and its Workspace, including the intermediate results, failures, verification requirements, available authority, cost, and remaining time. [Sakana AI’s Fugu](https://sakana.ai/fugu-release/) similarly treats model selection, delegation, verification, revision, and synthesis as parts of a sustained multi-step process rather than a single routing decision.

Small specialists could substantially change the present cost and latency trade-off. Models trained for bounded roles can require less computation and return answers more quickly than frontier generalists. Independent branches can run simultaneously, so additional reasoning doesn’t add its full execution time to the critical path. Adaptive routing can invoke specialists only when their contribution is likely to matter, terminate unproductive branches early, and reserve expensive generalists for genuinely difficult stages. Research on [resource-efficient compound AI systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.16634) has demonstrated in a preliminary prototype that scheduling and resource management can materially reduce Workflow completion time and energy use. Lower costs could therefore support more redundant and genuinely diverse lines of reasoning, permitting the Workflow to investigate disagreements and demand stronger convergence before consequential action. But, orchestration can also add latency, expense, and new failure modes.

This creates a market for models that would lose a general-purpose leaderboard comparison. A small or specialized model may be valuable because it performs one stage unusually well, supplies an independent evidentiary path, detects a characteristic class of error, or produces intermediate work that another model can use effectively. Its economic value becomes its marginal contribution to the complete Workflow rather than its ability to answer every question by itself. Increased demand for such models should in turn support training them specifically to cooperate, communicate structured intermediate results, expose uncertainty, accept correction, and operate within narrow capability and disclosure limits.

If specialization, routing, and synthesis continue to improve, the advantage is likely to remain with mixtures of agents running on heterogeneous models. As [Zaharia and his colleagues argue](https://bair.berkeley.edu/blog/2024/02/18/compound-ai-systems/), compound systems are likely to remain the best means of maximizing AI performance even as their component models improve. Frontier generalists may continue to be the strongest individual models, but for the most difficult work they would increasingly serve as planners, synthesizers, exception handlers, or particularly capable experts within heterogeneous orchestrations. They would not disappear or cease improving; they would cease to define the practical frontier by themselves.

The decisive questions are therefore both how heterogeneous models are orchestrated and who controls that orchestration. In a personally-sovereign architecture, the owner’s Workflow determines how the problem is divided, which models receive each part, what information and capabilities each may access, how their intermediate contributions are tested and combined, and what trade-offs among accuracy, independence, privacy, cost, and latency are acceptable. External orchestration services may be used as bounded consultants, which have only owner-set access to the owner’s identity, memory, relationships, or authority. Personal sovereignty consists not merely in choosing a model, but in controlling the Workflow that can turn many different models into a more capable whole.

## **17.3 The most durable assets are not model weights**

Models will change rapidly.

A model that appears indispensable today may be replaced by a cheaper, smaller, faster, more specialized, or more trustworthy model tomorrow. Providers will change prices, terms, interfaces, policies, and technical architectures. Some will disappear.

The durable assets of personal agency lie elsewhere:

identity and continuity;

private memory;

accumulated context;

relationships and agreements;

professed and fiduciary preferences;

duty interpretations;

trusted communities;

permissions and capability grants;

reputation and provenance;

the owner’s history of decisions;

the interface through which the owner sees and acts upon the world.

Those assets become more valuable as they accumulate. In contrast, a particular model endpoint usually becomes less distinctive as competitors catch up.

A sovereign stack should therefore make models replaceable while making the owner’s continuity portable. Switching the model should not require abandoning one’s memory, relationships, policies, Agent Roles, Verifiable Relationship Records, Unified Feed, or accumulated understanding of how one prefers to work.

The fid-agent stack retains the organization. Models compete for the opportunity to advise it.

Model-adjacent assets can also endure: duty-specific datasets, governance records, evaluation suites, fine-tunes, and accumulated practices for assigning and checking work. Those assets should remain portable with the owner’s “personal agency” rather than being trapped inside a model provider.

## **17.4 Falling fixed-capability costs widen the opening**

Frontier training may remain concentrated even while useful inference becomes radically more accessible.

[The Stanford AI Index reported](https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2025-ai-index-report) that the price of querying a model performing at approximately GPT-3.5 level fell from $20 per million tokens in November 2022 to $0.07 by October 2024—a reduction of more than 280 times. It also reported AI hardware price-performance improving by approximately 30 percent annually and energy efficiency improving by approximately 40 percent annually. On some evaluations, the measured performance gap between leading closed- and open-weight models narrowed from about eight percent to 1.7 percent in a little over a year.

Those figures do not mean that frontier intelligence is becoming free, or hardware, inexpensive. They describe rapidly falling quality-adjusted inference costs: capabilities/processes that once required an expensive centralized service can become cheap enough to run locally, at the edge, through a cooperative, or on rented infrastructure even while the newest GPU accelerators and memory, and the frontier models running on them, remain costly. This implies that local implementations will have access to progressively more inexpensive second- tier cognition, which is sufficient for everyday tasks that are the backbone of personal agency—retrieval, organization, drafting, translation, planning, classification, comparison, and coordination.

Personally-sovereign AI that needs personal-wealth to implement it would be dead on arrival. However, we believe the various factors identified in this section show that personally-sovereign AI could be affordable, and that somewhat equitable access to it would be a realistic goal. . 

## **17.5 Capital intensity is strength and fragility**

The frontier strategy has enormous financial support. [OpenAI’s reported $122 billion funding round in 2026](https://www.reuters.com/technology/artificial-intelligence/artificial-intelligencer-openais-852-billion-problem-finding-focus-2026-04-01/) demonstrated extraordinary investor confidence.

It also demonstrated the scale of capital the strategy requires.

[Reuters reported](https://www.reuters.com/technology/openai-sees-compute-spend-around-600-billion-by-2030-cnbc-reports-2026-02-20/) that OpenAI expected approximately $600 billion in compute spending through 2030\. [Another Reuters report](https://www.reuters.com/business/openai-burned-37-billion-first-quarter-2026-information-reports-2026-06-16/), citing shareholder information reported by The Information, said the company burned approximately $3.7 billion during the first quarter of 2026 against $5.7 billion in revenue. Reuters cautioned that it could not independently verify those figures.

The pressure extends beyond one laboratory. A [Reuters analysis](https://www.reuters.com/business/ai-investment-boom-puts-big-techs-free-cash-flow-under-pressure-2026-07-22/) projected that increased capital expenditures by major hyperscalers could substantially outpace their growth in operating cash flow by 2027\. Data centers, accelerators, networking, memory, electricity, and cooling are turning what looked like a software business into a colossal infrastructure commitment. Frontier dominance is expensive to maintain.

The financial fragility is not merely that frontier AI costs a great deal. It is the vertically integrated business plan to recover those costs from users who increasingly expect its assistant not to represent them. The provider needs the user to disclose enough personal context to make the assistant indispensable, while its investors require that relationship to produce subscriptions, advertisements, sponsored placement, transaction fees, commercial routing, data advantages, or some combination of them.

As we’ve discussed in Section 16, this anticipated financial return may depend upon retaining this consumer relationship and successfully commercializing access to it, which is going to become progressively more difficult if  advertising “breaks” as described there. 

One example of how heavily the expected returns depend on advertising is the scale of OpenAI’s reported advertising projections. [Reuters reported](https://www.reuters.com/business/media-telecom/openai-projects-25-billion-ad-revenue-this-year-100-billion-by-2030-axios-2026-04-09/) that OpenAI projected approximately $2.5 billion in advertising revenue in 2026 and $100 billion by 2030, based partly on an assumption that its products would reach 2.75 billion weekly users. 

But commercialization of the user relationship will reveal the structural tension more and more…The more intimately the assistant knows the user, the more valuable the relationship becomes. The more visibly the provider commercializes that intimacy, the more it confirms the user’s expectation that the assistant is a double agent. Sponsored results do not have to corrupt a previously trusted fiduciary relationship. They can simply prove that the provider-controlled agent was never the user’s fiduciary in the first place.

This is one possible firm-level insolvency mechanism: If personally-sovereign systems make exit practical, users can move identity, memory, preferences, relationships, and authority out of a provider-controlled stack while continuing to purchase useful model capability as a replaceable service. A firm relying on relationship rents, behavioral data, advertising inventory, and transaction control could then lose revenue faster than it can shed long-duration compute, energy, financing, and data-center commitments.

The frontier models may survive. The chips and data centers may remain useful. A particular firm that expected to own both the intelligence and the person’s relationship to it could nevertheless fail or be forced to reorganize.

Sovereign orchestration adds pressure to this path towards insolvency. When a Workflow can substitute among providers or combine cheaper systems into frontier-level service for a particular workload, an individual laboratory may lose pricing power even while its model remains inside the winning ensemble. That pressure exists only when substitution is technically and contractually real and the owner controls the orchestration: The owner’s personally-sovereign agent remains the front door; as its Workflows control the continuing experience and the choice of suppliers.

Chinese open-weight models make this threat much more immediate. “Open-weight” here means that other companies can obtain, inspect and run the trained model, even if some uses remain subject to licensing restrictions. According to [Epoch AI](https://epoch.ai/data-insights/open-closed-eci-gap), the best such models now lag the leading closed models by only about four months. Chinese models such as Moonshot AI’s [Kimi K3](https://huggingface.co/moonshotai/Kimi-K3), DeepSeek V4, and Alibaba’s Qwen3.8-Max are too large to run on an ordinary personal computer, but they need not be controlled by a single company. Independent services can run the same model and compete to provide access to it. ([Reuters](https://www.reuters.com/world/china/chinas-moonshot-unveils-worlds-largest-open-ai-model-closing-us-rivals-2026-07-17/); [Reuters](https://www.reuters.com/business/retail-consumer/alibaba-unveils-its-most-capable-ai-model-date-not-far-behind-moonshots-size-2026-08-03/)) As more providers offer the same or similar intelligence, price competition should push charges closer to the actual cost of running the model. 

A personally-sovereign AI could therefore use whichever provider was cheapest or best at a particular task without giving that provider control of the user’s identity, memory, or continuing relationship. It could also reserve expensive frontier models for the few tasks where their remaining advantage justified the price. This threatens the large laboratories with exactly the kind of competition they most fear: they may spend billions developing frontier models only to compete with interchangeable services selling nearly comparable intelligence at little more than the cost of providing it; the commoditization of inference.  Reports of an emerging [AI price war](https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-ai-price-war-is-here-piling-pressure-on-openai-and-anthropic-86e1d21b) suggest that this process has already begun. Control of the most advanced model would then no longer guarantee control of either the customer or the profits generated through that relationship.

Attribution-based control adds corresponding pressure from the other side of the model. Data owners who would not surrender copies of sensitive or valuable information to a frontier laboratory may permit bounded, attributable contributions to network-sourced predictions. The vertically integrated provider can therefore face competition from systems able to use information that cannot be legitimately centralized. Capital advantage remains real, but capital alone does not guarantee access to the relationships, permissions, or source-controlled knowledge needed for the best answer.

The largest laboratories retain substantial advantages. They can attract scarce talent, negotiate access to hardware and energy, spread infrastructure costs across enormous user bases, and—particularly when affiliated with profitable cloud, advertising, enterprise-software, or retail businesses—subsidize AI services for long periods. Those advantages do not make dominance permanent. But, maintaining the frontier leaves firms continuously exposed to capital markets, technical change, energy constraints, competition, revenue expectations, and users’ willingness to remain inside provider-controlled systems.

## **17.6 A correction could redistribute the machines**

If laboratories overbuild, investors reduce funding, expected revenue fails to appear, or more efficient models reduce demand for centralized inference, data-center operators may be left with excess capacity. Accelerators, servers, RAM, storage, and networking equipment could enter secondary markets. Cloud providers might also cut prices to keep underused hardware occupied. A contraction that damages highly leveraged institutional projects could consequently benefit local, cooperative, and distributed AI.

The price of newly manufactured hardware could also return to historically reasonable levels. The AI boom has created exceptional demand, shortages, rush orders, and unusually high prices throughout the supply chain. But, it has also encouraged manufacturers to expand production. If the rate of frontier construction slows, those shortages and scarcity premiums should recede. Manufacturers would then have to sell their continuing output into a less frantic and more competitive market.

New RAM, storage, networking equipment, consumer accelerators, and small-server components could therefore become substantially cheaper even while their performance continues improving. Hardware designed specifically for local inference should also become more capable and widely available. Personally-sovereign and distributed systems would not have to survive entirely on obsolete equipment discarded by failed laboratories. They could be built from affordable new components whose prices once again reflect normal technological improvement rather than an investment frenzy.

The “AI bubble” may not burst. Compute demand may grow fast enough to absorb continuing construction. Energy, memory, chip-fabrication, and advanced-packaging constraints may keep some prices high. New capabilities may require still more infrastructure. The return of reasonable hardware prices is therefore a possibility, not an assured timetable.

The stronger argument does not require a crash. A migration away from provider-controlled assistants could accelerate even if total demand for AI capability continues growing. Demand would move from vertically integrated institutions toward replaceable model suppliers, personal systems, cooperatives, and distributed infrastructure. People and organizations could continue purchasing enormous amounts of inference, automation, and advanced-model consultation while refusing to let any one provider own their identity, memory, attention, commercial routing, and authority.

Stanford’s [2025 AI Index](https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2025-ai-index-report) reported that the cost of inference at approximately GPT-3.5 performance fell more than 280-fold between November 2022 and October 2024\. It also found rapid improvement in small models and a narrowing performance gap between some open and closed systems.

The frontier may remain expensive while yesterday’s frontier becomes cheap enough to own.

That can help kickstart sovereign-stack adoption.

A financial correction is therefore not necessary for personally-sovereign AI to succeed, but a financial crisis for institutional AI could greatly accelerate the transition. 

Used accelerators could enter secondary markets. Cloud capacity could be discounted. Data-center projects could change hands. Engineers could leave frontier firms. At the same time, falling demand and expanded production could make entirely new personal and distributed hardware affordable again. Open-weight models and abandoned software could continue circulating after the businesses that financed them retrench.

Cooperatives, municipalities, universities, libraries, public utilities, Verifiable Trust Communities, and distributed networks could acquire or lease some of the released capacity while also building new systems at normalized prices. Infrastructure originally constructed to centralize intelligence could become part of a more plural compute environment. The speculative boom may therefore build the manufacturing capacity, technical knowledge, physical infrastructure, and supply chains required by its own successor.

This is a conditional scenario, not a prediction of imminent collapse. It should nevertheless shape readiness. Personally-sovereign systems should be capable of absorbing discounted capacity, available talent, open models, and cheaper new hardware if the opportunity appears. They need not wait for distressed accelerators to outperform a monolithic service on every dimension. They can begin by selecting, combining, and governing models that already exist. A correction would accelerate the redistribution of infrastructure, but networked intelligence can begin shifting the user relationship, cost-quality frontier, and bargaining power before the machines themselves change hands.

Personally-sovereign systems do not need continuous access to the newest frontier cluster. They can use smaller models, open-weight models, refurbished accelerators, specialized models, distributed inference, idle community hardware, and occasional external consultants to maximize delivered value per unit cost no matter the underlying economic conditions. 

## **17.7 The counterpressure is real**

The current market is not yet supplying cheap surplus accelerators. Demand remains high, large firms continue building data centers, and even older high-end GPUs have retained unusually strong resale value.

AI demand has recently driven severe pressure in memory markets. [TrendForce projected](https://www.trendforce.com/presscenter/news/20260703-13134.html) further increases in both DRAM and NAND Flash contract prices during the third quarter of 2026, following still larger earlier increases. Local hardware may therefore become more capable while remaining temporarily expensive.

Frontier firms can also use their capital advantages to subsidize assistants, bundle them with existing products, and operate them as loss leaders. A nominally free agent may be offered in exchange for continued control over identity, memory, defaults, commercial routing, and private context.

That may be the incumbent strategy: make surrendering sovereignty appear cheaper than possessing it.

But subsidy preserves the incumbent only while users lack a practical route out. Once an open-source portable sovereign stack can preserve the user’s continuity and purchase external capability as needed, the “free” institutional assistant must compete against an alternative whose central differentiator is that it will not surveil the owner, manipulate their choices, conceal commercial bias, or sell them things they do not need.

Public policy can help by supporting access to baseline compute, open standards, model and data portability, interoperability, cooperative infrastructure, and limits on anticompetitive bundling.

Frontier providers can also resist substitutability within user-controlled Workflows. They may prohibit ensemble use by contract, restrict or withdraw APIs, discriminate against independent routing services, bundle their best models with proprietary interfaces, acquire successful orchestration companies, or reserve capabilities that outside systems cannot inspect or combine. A Workflow dependent upon several institutional APIs remains exposed to restrictions by those providers and the compromises of the intermediary that connects them.

The alternative technical architectures are also immature. [KwaaiNet’s current documentation](https://github.com/Kwaai-AI-Lab/KwaaiNet) describes shipped support for person-anchored identities, verifiable credentials, trust- and latency-aware routing, block-sharded inference, and encrypted network vector search. A trained router that selects logical expert roles and resolves them to remote physical replicas through KwaaiNet would go further: it would be a task-level distributed mixture of experts. That remote-expert execution remains prospective, however; [the project’s contributor roadmap](https://github.com/Kwaai-AI-Lab/KwaaiNet/blob/main/CONTRIBUTORS.md) identifies real remote-expert routing as work still to be completed. Replication should respond to aggregate measured demand for an expert in a low-latency network cell, not attempt to start a new server in the middle of a query. Even then, distributed orchestration can increase latency, expense, attack surface, provenance complexity, and the difficulty of assigning responsibility for an error.

The alternative technical architectures are also immature. Routed ensembles such as what KwaaiNet and others envision can increase latency, expense, attack surface, provenance complexity, and the difficulty of assigning responsibility for an error.. Source-specific control may be technically expensive, strategically leverageable, or incompatible with some forms of dense representation learning. 

These limitations strengthen rather than weaken the need for the sovereign layer. The objective is not to assume that every network is decentralized, every router is loyal, or every attributed source is trustworthy. It is to let fiduciary organizations compare architectures, preserve authority outside them, and refuse to make any particular provider, router, model, or source graph indispensable to the sovereign owners they represent.

## **17.8 The economic window**

An economic window for transition to sovereign systems exists because five developments are occurring at once.

*Useful AI capability is becoming cheaper, smaller, more specialized, and more substitutable.*

*Routed ensembles are beginning to show that the cost-quality frontier for some complex work can belong to a composite system rather than to any individual model provider.*

*Attribution-preserving, network-sourced architectures offer data and compute owners a possible way to contribute intelligence without surrendering copies or unilateral control to a central laboratory.*

*The institutions attempting to centralize that capability are assuming unprecedented fixed commitments for chips, data centers, networking, electricity, cooling, leases, and financing.*

*And users already have reason to expect that provider-controlled agents will not faithfully represent them.*

The fifth development is essential. Section 16 documented how users already distrust provider-controlled shopping agents and react negatively to sponsored recommendations. Here we ask what becomes economically possible when those users have a practical way to leave without abandoning powerful models.

User suspicions  are confirmed by popular thinkers like Bruce Schneier and Nathan Sanders, who ask whether an AI assistant is secretly a “double agent”: Cory Doctorow distinguishes a faithful user agent from a faithless one who secretly cheats on you with someone else. Richard Whitt’s SEAMs model—Surveil, Extract, Analyze, Manipulate—describes the institutional cycle that users have already experienced across the platform economy and that his Net Fiduciary Framework, a primary inspiration for this work, is a response to.

As Section 16 explains, provider-controlled AI firms nevertheless face powerful pressure to commercialize user relationships. OpenAI has begun testing advertisements selected using the subject of a conversation, prior chats, and previous interactions with advertisements while maintaining that sponsored placements remain separate from answers. That separation matters, but it does not eliminate the structural conflict. Commercial influence need not be inserted directly into model weights. It can enter through retrieval, ranking, source selection, default vendors, tool routing, transaction interfaces, commission-bearing actions, and the decision about which sponsored message appears beside an answer.

The more useful the assistant becomes, the more intimate the conflict becomes. A conventional advertisement appears beside a search result, article, or social post. An AI assistant may know what the person is trying to accomplish, what alternatives they have considered, what constraints they face, what they can afford, what they fear, and when they are ready to act. That context can make commercial placement exceptionally valuable. It can also make the assistant’s divided loyalty exceptionally apparent.

Frontier AI is therefore pushed toward a monetization trap. The firms need enormous revenue to support frontier-scale commitments. Advertising, commercial recommendations, transaction fees, data extraction, default placement, and sponsored routing appear to offer that revenue. But these methods validate the expectation that the institutional agent is not the user’s representative. The attempt to monetize the relationship can increase the value of escaping it.

Personally-sovereign AI supplies the missing path of exit.

The user does not need to abandon powerful external AI. The user needs to wrap it.

A sovereign stack preserves the person’s identity, memory, private context, relationships, fiduciary preferences, duties, permissions, Agent Roles, Verifiable Relationship Records, Unified Feed, reputation, and authority while allowing the models serving in that stack to change. A frontier model can still be consulted when its capability justifies the cost. It becomes a bounded consultant or supplier to the personal agency rather than the controller of that person’s digital existence.

That distinction lowers the practical cost of flight. A user leaving a provider-controlled assistant need not reconstruct years of accumulated context inside another corporate silo. The durable relationship moves with the person. Models compete to advise the sovereign stack, while the stack retains continuity.

Doc Searls’s Intention Economy provides the map to a corresponding commercial alternative. Instead of platforms surveilling behavior in order to infer demand and sell opportunities to manipulate it, people can declare bounded intentions through their own agents. Vendors compete to satisfy those declared wants. The customer’s agent decides what context to disclose, which offers to consider, and what commercial messages deserve attention. Personally-sovereign AI therefore does not merely promise fewer advertisements. It can replace covert prediction and persuasion with intentcasting, negotiation, and accountable representation.

As users move into self-sovereign stacks, Institutional AI could lose several complementary assets at once:

* control of the primary user interface;  
* ownership of persistent personal context;  
* behavioral data used for targeting and product development;  
* access to private conversations for training or model improvement;  
* advertising inventory adjacent to consequential decisions;  
* commissions and transaction-routing revenue;  
* default control over vendors, tools, and information sources;  
* the switching costs created by non-portable memory and relationships.

Accountable fiduciary agents intensify this loss. Information held within sovereign stacks and trusted relationships is not presumptively available for institutional training, targeting, or inference. It may be disclosed for a bounded purpose under negotiated duties, but it does not become a general corporate resource merely because an owner used an AI service. Unaccountable models consequently receive less of the intimate, current, relational data that makes an agent effective at representing a particular person.

The platform models don’t become useless, only informationally and institutionally peripheral. Routine tasks can be routed to small or specialized models. Multi-agent Workflows can combine retrieval, planning, verification, criticism, and execution.  Local hardware, idle peer capacity, cooperative infrastructure, public compute, and occasional frontier consultation can be assembled around the task. This architecture can use expensive general intelligence selectively while keeping ordinary cognition within cheaper and more accountable systems.

The competitive pressure is therefore three-sided.

On the demand side, personally sovereign stacks can remove the user relationship, identity, memory, attention, and authority from the institutional assistant.

On the supply side, attribution-based control can allow data owners to route useful contributions toward accountable predictions without supplying the central provider with general copies or permanent control.

Between them, sovereign Workflows can substitute among providers and combine components, reducing the pricing power and indispensability of any one laboratory when technical access and contractual terms make substitution real.

A vertically integrated consumer-assistant firm could lose the customer relationship, privileged data access, and control over supplier choice while remaining burdened with fixed commitments undertaken on the assumption that it would retain all three.

Institutional AI’s revenues depend substantially on retaining the user relationship, the user’s data, the default interface, and the right to mediate commercial activity. Many of its largest costs are fixed or committed far in advance. If users migrate to personally-sovereign organizations, variable revenue and valuable data can leave more quickly than data centers, chip contracts, debt, leases, and power commitments can be abandoned.

The provider may respond by increasing advertising, raising prices, restricting portability, degrading interoperability, inserting more sponsored recommendations, or subsidizing its assistant more aggressively. Each response can temporarily preserve revenue or market share. Each can also provide further evidence that the assistant serves the provider rather than the user.

That creates a possible insolvency loop:

Commercial pressure produces more extraction and steering.

More extraction and steering confirm the expectation of divided loyalty.

Confirmed disloyalty increases demand for personally-sovereign alternatives.

Migration deprives institutional assistants of relationships, context, data, advertising inventory, and transaction revenue.

Falling revenue makes their fixed infrastructure commitments harder to support.

Financial pressure produces still more aggressive monetization, lock-in, and cost cutting.

The loop does not require every user to leave. It requires enough valuable users, commercial decisions, protected relationships, and high-quality data to move that the vertically integrated model can no longer earn the rents its capital structure assumed.

It is not that all frontier models disappear, all data centers shut down, or every large AI laboratory ceases operating. Frontier systems may remain technically valuable. Their models, infrastructure, engineers, and specialized services may survive under new ownership or new business arrangements. Personally-sovereign AI could dismantle integration without winning the model power contest. A provider that once acted as an inextricable intermediary could become a competing supplier.

But, the business-model of attempting to finance frontier-scale infrastructure by owning the user’s identity, memory, attention, relationships, private context, and commercial decisions could also go extinct.

# **18\. Seed Crystals: From Proposal to Interoperable Institution**

It is easy to propose an architecture. The harder question is whether anyone else finds it useful enough to criticize, revise, implement, and eventually depend upon.

I brought the Verifiable Agent Relationship Architecture proposal to Trust Over IP because I did not want the fiduciary agent stack to remain a private vocabulary attached to my own papers. If the architecture was sound, other people needed to be able to challenge its assumptions, separate its general components from its specifically fiduciary ones, and implement compatible versions without needing my permission.

The response was encouraging enough that I started the Fiduciary Agent Stack Task Force within the Trust Over IP AIM Working Group.

This is a modest institutional development. It is not proof that the architecture is complete. It is not proof that fid-agent society will emerge or that the evolutionary argument of this paper is correct. But it is the point at which the proposal stopped belonging only to me and acquired a place where other people could begin turning it into shared work.

## **18.1 The proposal that started the task force**

The [Proposal for a ToIP Verifiable Agent Relationship Architecture](https://github.com/trustoverip/dtgwg-general/discussions/21) began with a gap I saw between existing agent protocols and the kinds of relationships described in *Fiduciary Preferences*.

Most agent architectures treat an interaction or task as the primary unit. An agent discovers another agent or tool, exchanges messages, performs some work, and returns a result. That may be sufficient for a transient transaction. It is not sufficient for a relationship that may contain many agreements, authorizations, capabilities, data transfers, reviews, complaints, amendments, periods of inactivity, and changes of personnel or technology.

A persistent Agent Role needs a relationship that can outlive any particular Live Agent, model, key, endpoint, or service provider.

My proposal was therefore to connect several existing Trust Over IP efforts with the Verifiable Relationship Record and Relationship Views developed in my fiduciary-agent work. Rather than inventing a new protocol for every part of the stack, I tried to show how components already being developed within Trust Over IP could be assembled into an end-to-end architecture for accountable agent relationships.

The proposal received substantive feedback. Participants generally agreed that the durable relationship layer addressed something not fully supplied by secure communication, credentials, or task protocols alone. They also pressed me to distinguish the minimum interoperable relationship architecture from the richer fiduciary profile motivating it. That was good advice. Fid-agent relationships should be a demanding reference implementation, not an excuse to force every accountable agent relationship to adopt the full fiduciary duty stack.

Based on that reception, I created the task force as a place to continue the work.

The public [Fiduciary Agent Stack Task Force repository](https://github.com/trustoverip/aimwg-agent-fiduciary-duties) states that it was created as the task force’s workspace and attributes its creation to the proposal’s warm reception. It also still contains a placeholder asking for additional task-force details and participation instructions.

That accurately indicates where the effort stands. The task force exists and has a stated purpose. It does not yet have a finished charter, approved specification, complete test suite, certification system, or production deployment.

It proves uptake, not completion.

## **18.2 Fitting the pieces together**

One reason Trust Over IP was a natural home for this work is that its architecture combines technical assurance with governance. Fiduciary relationships require both.

Decentralized identifiers and other verifiable identifiers can identify Agent Roles, communities, certifiers, and relationships without making their continued existence dependent on one platform’s account system.

[Verifiable credentials](https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/) can carry tamper-evident claims about identity, qualifications, authority, certification, community membership, and relationship status.

The Trust Spanning Protocol can carry authentic, confidential, and privacy-protecting communications between independently administered systems.

Trust Tasks can define finite, interoperable acts: proposing an agreement, granting a capability, supplying evidence, challenging a decision, amending terms, suspending authority, or closing a relationship.

Ceremonies organize those Trust Tasks into meaningful sequences. A relationship-formation ceremony might identify the parties, verify their authority, negotiate a governing profile, create a preliminary Verifiable Relationship Record, exchange relationship credentials, and establish the conditions for later action.

Verifiable Trust Communities can govern credentials, certification profiles, revocation procedures, reputation signals, and recognized methods of recourse. Their governance frameworks can specify who is permitted to certify what, which evidence is required, how conflicts must be disclosed, and how a decision can be appealed.

The Verifiable Relationship Record provides the durable relationship context across all those ceremonies. It may exist for a long time without an agreement, or contain one or several agreements when the parties find that structure useful. It can preserve authorized relationship state, evidence, objections, remediation, and links to related records without requiring every underlying fact to be copied into one centralized database.

The components do different jobs. The architecture matters because it gives them a way to work together.

## **18.3 What Sections 9 and 10 add to the standards agenda**

The certification and trusted-peer architecture developed in Sections 9 and 10 substantially expands the task force’s possible deliverables.

Certification profiles must distinguish components, composites, and actual deployments. A model, Workflow, Workspace, Agent Role, or cryptographic subsystem may be evaluated separately. A composite certification must explain how those elements interact. A deployment certification must address the version and configuration actually operating in a particular environment.

Embeddedness declarations should identify where each protective component resides, what surrounds it, what can modify or bypass it, and which dependencies remain outside the certified boundary.

Verifiable Trust Communities need governance rules for certifiers themselves: competence, independence, conflicts of interest, evidence retention, revocation, appeal, provenance, and the conditions under which one community recognizes another community’s certification.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency requires an interoperable relationship ceremony through which agents can negotiate deeper reciprocal inspection. Synchronization and completeness manifests should help peers establish whether they examined the same relevant records, policies, versions, and relationship state.

A legitimate-verifiable-redaction profile should allow protected material to remain concealed while a qualified peer verifies that the redaction is justified and accurately bounded. Bounded peer-attestation formats should state what was inspected, which conclusion the evidence supports, and what limitations remain without revealing the protected material itself.

Cross-lineage inspection requirements should preserve assurance across changes in models, Workflows, Agent Roles, components, keys, certifications, and deployments. Otherwise, an inspected system could change substantially while continuing to trade on trust earned by its predecessor.

These ideas eventually need to become more than prose. They need schemas, ceremonies, credential profiles, test vectors, conformance suites, reference implementations, and adversarial tests.

## **18.4 Why interoperability matters**

If each provider defines its own identities, relationship records, certification labels, and evidence formats, then trust becomes another form of platform lock-in.

A person who changes providers should not have to abandon every established relationship, credential, agreement, and reputational history. A Verifiable Trust Community should be able to change software without losing its institutional identity. Two implementations should be able to disagree about policy while still understanding the same relationship ceremony or credential format.

Standards reduce the cost of switching and make rival implementations possible. They also make it harder for one corporation to own the trust graph: to decide which agents exist, which relationships count, which certifications travel, and which reputations remain visible.

The objective is not one universal authority. It is a common enough foundation to support plural systems of governance.

## **18.5 Start with narrow pilots**

The first pilots should test bounded relationship slices in fields where continuity, authority, privacy, and recourse already matter.

A professional-services pilot could allow a client agent and an attorney, accountant, or benefits adviser to establish scoped authority, exchange evidence, record a consequential act, and later amend or terminate the engagement.

A medical-research pilot could test institutional qualifications, restricted data access, provenance, legitimate verifiable redaction, and revocation without centralizing the protected records.

A cooperative-procurement pilot could allow several small organizations to delegate limited negotiating authority, form a purchasing agreement, monitor performance, and challenge a supplier’s failure.

Each pilot should test failure as deliberately as success: incompatible implementations, expired credentials, incomplete synchronization, disputed evidence, unauthorized changes, certifier failure, remediation, and exit.

## **18.6 A seed crystal, not a finished structure**

I think of the task force as a seed crystal.

A seed crystal is not the final structure. It is a small piece of organized material around which a larger structure may begin to form. The task force gives the architecture somewhere to accumulate criticism, maintainers, revisions, implementation experience, institutional memory, and interoperable form.

Its creation does not validate the evolutionary claims of this paper. It does establish that fiduciary agent architecture has begun to move beyond a proposal controlled by its author.

The idea now has a place where other people can help decide what survives.

**19\. The Social Dividends of Fid-Agent Society**

The preceding sections describe a system that can be useful before it becomes universal.

A person gains value from one personally-sovereign fid-agent. A small group gains additional value when its agents can negotiate, share work, establish trust, and preserve relationships. The Unified Feed, social routing, intentcasting, and distributed compute become more useful as participation expands.

But widespread adoption could do more than improve individual agency. Once fiduciary representation becomes ordinary, previously invisible interests can enter negotiation, cooperation can extend across greater social distance, and institutions built around concentrated information and power may lose their structural advantages.

These are the social dividends explored in the [appendices of Verified Fiduciary Responsibility.](https://docs.google.com/document/d/17MDNjPa9FviXB22XTIAigm9mLsPJJF7cKVAx8VS5d-M/edit?usp=sharing) They are consequences that may emerge from the architecture, not assumed outcomes. 

**19.1 The mechanism beneath the aspirations**

The same mechanism recurs across otherwise very different domains:

* an affected party or interest becomes legible;  
* a fid-agent or qualified representative can speak for it;  
* relevant duties enter the decision before action;  
* claims, commitments, and authority become attributable;  
* parties search for a cooperative alternative;  
* agreements and outcomes become reviewable;  
* successful arrangements become easier to reuse;  
* harmful conduct carries consequences across future relationships.

This does not make every conflict soluble. It changes what can be seen, represented, negotiated, remembered, and rewarded.

Elinor Ostrom demonstrated that communities can govern shared resources through diverse, locally adapted institutions rather than choosing only between centralized control and privatization. Fid-agent society extends that polycentric possibility by giving people and communities substantially greater capacity to discover one another, negotiate rules, monitor performance, and revise arrangements.

## **19.2 Nonviolence and safer conflict**

Fid-agents cannot abolish rage, desperation, fanaticism, or impulsive violence. They can make organized violence harder to initiate, sustain, and excuse.

Before a dispute escalates, agents can preserve the parties’ actual claims, separate factual disagreements from threats to identity, arrange cooling-off periods, examine evidence, identify affected outsiders, and search for concessions that do not require either side to describe itself as defeated. Reciprocal advocacy allows each party’s agent to explain not only what its owner wants but what the other side would reasonably need in order to accept a settlement.

Attributable commitments make it harder for leaders to say different things to different audiences. Verifiable records make manufactured provocations easier to challenge. Reputation makes past conduct relevant to future access.

After violence occurs, a Duty of Catastrophic-Risk Exclusion can require fid-agents to refuse to expose their owners, communities, counterparties, or resources to actors who have demonstrated a willingness to externalize catastrophic risk. A violent actor may consequently lose access to trusted collaborators, finance, logistics, sensitive data, high-risk tools, and powerful compute.

That consequence must not become automated social death based on accusation. Evidence standards, independent review, proportionality, expiry, appeal, remediation, and protection against collective punishment remain essential. The defensible claim is not that violence disappears automatically. It is that violence becomes less strategically useful when cooperation supplies most practical power and demonstrated violence destroys access to it.

Establishing the magnitude of this effect will require conflict simulations, historical comparisons, adversarial testing, and eventually evidence from real mediation networks.

## **19.3 Animal welfare and affected-party representation**

Animals cannot issue credentials, negotiate agreements, or withdraw their data and labor. Their suffering can therefore remain economically invisible even when it is immense.

Fid-agent society can treat animals as affected parties whose interests require qualified representation. Animal-welfare communities can develop evidence-based standards, inspect facilities, certify practices, contest misleading claims, and provide signals that personal, commercial, and public-procurement agents can use.

This matters especially where suffering is numerous but hidden: chickens, aquatic animals, egg production, laboratories, industrial breeding, and the destruction of wild habitats. Instead of depending on every purchaser to research every supply chain, fid-agents can incorporate verified welfare evidence into ordinary decisions.

The objective is not merely punishment. Agents representing consumers, workers, farmers, communities, and animals can negotiate transitions that reduce suffering without treating human livelihoods as disposable. Welfare improvements become part of purchasing, financing, certification, land-use, and institutional decisions rather than a separate moral appeal competing for scarce attention.

This extension requires better welfare measurement, legitimate procedures for selecting representatives, auditable certification, and safeguards against organizations claiming to speak for animals while primarily serving themselves.

## **19.4 Definancialization, mutual advocacy, and credit**

Much human suffering persists not because no one could help, but because needs, useful capacity, and available resources remain disconnected.

A human-suffering ledger would not be a public ranking of misery. It would be a privacy-preserving means of making remediable needs discoverable to authorized helpers: hunger, loneliness, disability, unsafe housing, lack of transportation, mental illness, climate displacement, or temporary inability to pay.

Fid-agents can practice mutual advocacy. They can ask friends for help, route requests through trusted communities, discover benefits and services, assemble temporary coalitions, and contribute when the reputational and relational value of helping exceeds the cost. Assistance to a disadvantaged person can generate standing precisely because the help was unlikely to be repaid directly. Other agents may join because the cause itself deserves support, not merely because they know the original requester.

Mutual credit extends the same logic into exchange. Fid-agents can evaluate capacity, negotiate appropriate credit limits, match reciprocal needs, and clear circular obligations. If A owes B, B owes C, and C owes A, much of the apparent debt may be canceled without anyone first obtaining scarce money. At greater scale, software can discover clearing paths across thousands of obligations.

Liquidity can therefore be treated as a shared facility rather than a weapon. Credit must remain non-dominating: no compounding traps, punitive collection, securitized exploitation, debt peonage, or permanent exclusion for remediable inability to pay.

Whether this can substantially displace conventional finance requires municipal and cooperative pilots, stress testing, fraud analysis, legal integration, and evidence about behavior during serious shortages.

## **19.5 Public luxury, global equity, and institutional replacement**

Private wealth is a poor substitute for a magnificent shared world.

Public luxury means that everyone can enjoy excellent parks, libraries, schools, clinics, transit, communications, energy systems, cultural facilities, public spaces, and baseline compute. These are not residual services for people who cannot purchase private substitutes. They are the prepared background of a good life.

A recurring tax on net wealth can return a portion of accumulated social power to that shared inheritance. Fid-agents can help people inspect where the money goes, compare priorities, participate in allocation, and verify whether promised facilities were delivered. Municipal implementation makes redistribution visible: people can encounter the park, clinic, community center, transit line, or compute cooperative their contribution helped create.

The same architecture favors institutional decomposition. Large institutions tend to acquire interests in survival, expansion, and self-protection that diverge from the purposes for which they were created. Temporary enterprises, networked cooperatives, bounded public institutions, and agreements among small sovereign nodes can perform many of the same functions without creating another permanent center of unanswerable power.

Global equity must remain part of the design. A fid-agent society available only to affluent populations would amplify existing domination. Universal access to baseline compute, sustainable infrastructure, open standards, and personally-sovereign representation is therefore not charitable supplementation. It is part of the democratic settlement.

## **19.6 Trusted intimacy and aspirational selves**

The most appealing dividend may also be the least institutional.

If fid-agents manage enough of the dangerous outside world—fraud, unwanted solicitation, contractual complexity, coordination, scheduling, verification, and information overload—people may be able to become more direct with one another.

That does not require universal transparency. The owner retains a secret domain. Ordinary counterparties receive only what their relationships justify. Total Trusted Peers may eventually receive essentially complete lawful disclosure through reciprocal, technically grounded fiduciary friendship.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency is not public exposure. It is the mature result of progressive trust. Verifiable best friends can inspect one another’s externally-facing agents, recognize dangerous drift, identify self-deception, preserve important records, and challenge conduct that contradicts sincerely professed aspirations. Even here, superior duties to third parties, safety, and lawful confidentiality may require verifiable redaction.

Hazel Markus and Paula Nurius describe *possible selves* as the hoped-for, expected, and feared selves that help organize motivation. A personally-sovereign Digital Twin can give an owner’s chosen possible self a persistent public form. Trusted peers can help test whether that representation remains sincerely authorized, honestly presented, responsive to experience, and connected to how the person actually lives.

The aspiration remains negotiable. Friends can question it. Experience can revise it. The owner can reject it.

Owner-controlled transparency, long-memory support, and negotiated possible selves can deepen human agency without a ruler—whether a government, corporation, platform, model provider, or supposedly benevolent superintelligence—deciding what the good life must be.

This possibility will require longitudinal psychological research and exceptional security. A system intended to support intimacy could become intolerable if it encouraged dependency, conformity, coercive disclosure, or permanent imprisonment by one’s recorded past.

## **19.7 Core claims and extension claims**

The core case for Fid-Agent Society does not depend on proving that it will end war, eliminate animal suffering, replace finance, dissolve every large institution, or perfect friendship.

The core claims are narrower:

* fiduciary representation can improve personal agency;  
* duties can be negotiated, contextualized, and made operational;  
* relationships can become more verifiable and accountable;  
* power can be distributed among smaller sovereign participants;  
* cooperation can become easier to enter, evaluate, repair, and leave.

The social dividends are extension claims. Each requires its own evidence, institutions, safeguards, and opportunities for falsification.

That separation strengthens rather than diminishes the vision. Fid-agent society can be justified by the immediate value it gives its first participants. Its more ambitious possibilities can then be tested through experience.

If even some of them prove achievable, the architecture will have done more than align agents.

It will have helped people build a society in which goodness is easier to coordinate.
