# **20\. Worked Cases, Rival Equilibria, and Falsifiable Tests**

Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity is not made credible by describing a desirable society. Its central claim is empirical: under the right conditions, agents practicing Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity should produce enough additional value, avoid enough loss, and control enough trusted access that Unaccountable Behavior cannot profitably invade their networks.

That claim can be wrong.

The relevant evidence must include competent defectors that adapt to the defenses, not merely crude scammers that announce themselves. It must test complete deployments rather than prompts, idealized components, or cooperative demonstrations. It must also distinguish a system that prevents harm from one that prevents all useful activity.

## **20.1 What would falsify the theory**

The following findings would count directly against the claim that Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity can become evolutionarily stable:

* agents practicing Unaccountable Behavior obtain equal access to trusted relationships, private context, attention, data, and actuation at lower total cost;  
* certification does not materially improve partner selection, incident rates, or the containment of failures;  
* deep trusted-peer inspection does not detect important defects that ordinary external testing misses;  
* false reputation signals spread more rapidly or persist longer than accurate corrections;  
* fiduciary conduct deteriorates as the underlying models become more capable, strategic, or able to navigate around controls;  
* users reject personally sovereign stacks when their utility, price, latency, and ease of use are genuinely equivalent to institutional alternatives.

These are not implementation inconveniences that can be defined away. If they persist across credible implementations and realistic conditions, then a major mechanism of the theory has failed.

## **20.2 Re-running the eight VFR cases**

The eight examples in *Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity* should be rerun as mechanism tests. Each case implies an intervention, an expected behavioral change, and an observable failure condition.

| VFR case | Mechanism under test | Evidence against the mechanism |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| The spammer at the door | Authentication, reputation, negotiated delivery terms, and attention pricing should make indiscriminate interruption unprofitable. | Spam remains cheap, legitimate contact is disproportionately blocked, or review costs exceed the attention saved. |
| The corporate pseudo-twin | Users and counterparties should distinguish a personally sovereign fiduciary agent from a provider-controlled representative. | Corporate systems retain equivalent fiduciary credibility despite unilateral control, hidden incentives, or non-portable records. |
| The research data request | Purpose limits, query-only access, provenance, retention rules, and audit should enable useful research with less disclosure. | Researchers bypass fiduciary terms, compliant access becomes unusably expensive, or restricted data leaks without attributable evidence. |
| The employer who wants the whole twin | Role separation and capability attenuation should give employers useful work access without exposing the worker’s complete personal agency. | Work cannot be performed without general access, or employers obtain equivalent access through coercive contracting and market power. |
| The unsafe high-powered model | A capable external model should remain useful when treated as a consultant inside a bounded environment without receiving raw authority. | Containment destroys most of its utility, or the model can obtain sensitive context or induce unauthorized action through its advice. |
| The carbon-heavy simulation | Externality review should change compute timing, scale, redundancy, mitigation, or project design. | The review becomes ceremonial, its estimates are systematically gamed, or projects proceed unchanged while claiming compliance. |
| The social introduction | Agent negotiation should improve consent and matching while minimizing unnecessary disclosure. | Introductions become manipulative, over-filtered, privacy-invasive, or less satisfying than ordinary human approaches. |
| The bad actor trying to join the network | Provenance, credentials, graduated access, witnesses, and persistent reputation should deny high-trust access without requiring proof of malicious intent. | Bad actors repeatedly acquire privileged access through identity resets, credential shopping, coalition assistance, or false attestations. |

Success wouldn't mean that no bad events occur. The relevant comparison is against plausible alternatives: ordinary institutional agents, unmediated model use, less structured personal agents, and adaptive Unaccountable Behavior.

## **20.3 The seven rival equilibria remain live possibilities**

The seven rival equilibria identified in Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity should be preserved as competing outcomes.

**Corporate capture** occurs if provider-controlled assistants remain more convenient and acquire enough default distribution to make sovereignty irrelevant in practice.

**Fake fiduciary equilibrium** occurs if fiduciary language becomes cheap while loyalty, portability, inspection, revocation, and recourse remain absent.

**Monoculture** occurs if one model lineage, duty repository, certifier, or implementation becomes so dominant that correlated defects overwhelm the advantages of coordination.

**Reputation tyranny** occurs if reputational systems punish dissent, amplify factional judgments, or make exit nominal rather than meaningful.

**Over-exclusion** occurs if protective networks become autoimmune, excluding unfamiliar but trustworthy agents faster than they exclude sophisticated defectors.

**Owner negligence** occurs if principals routinely ignore review, approve without comprehension, or allow their agents’ authority and records to decay.

**Misaligned twins** occurs if personally controlled agents become deceptive, incompetent, captured by their owners’ worst impulses, or effective at rationalizing conduct that their duties should prevent.

The research question is not merely whether these outcomes can occur. It is which equilibrium attracts the system under different costs, network structures, capability levels, governance rules, and rates of error.

## **20.4 Adaptive attacks on the proposed defenses**

Certification creates a new attack surface. A certifier may be compromised, bribed, or gradually captured. Providers may shop among certifiers until one applies a permissive interpretation. Individually valid components may be assembled into an unsafe composite. A certified deployment may drift through model replacement, Workflow modification, configuration changes, altered dependencies, or expanded capabilities. Credentials may be forged, replayed after revocation, or remain technically valid after their supporting evidence has become stale.

Preventing certifier capture requires more than auditing certifiers from the outside. To qualify under a Verifiable Trust Community’s certification authority, a certifier should be required to maintain Total Trusted-Peer relationships with other certifiers operating under that authority. Within the limits imposed by legitimate confidentiality, their systems should map relevant certification state one-to-one.

That mapped state should include:

* certification profiles and test versions;  
* components, composites, and deployments examined;  
* declared embeddedness boundaries;  
* evidence considered and tests performed;  
* assumptions, exceptions, conflicts, and inspection limitations;  
* certifications granted, denied, suspended, revoked, or allowed to expire;  
* withdrawn applications and evidence of certification shopping;  
* challenges, appeals, corrections, and remediation;  
* synchronization status and completeness manifests.

A peer able to map this state directly has a substantial advantage over an auditor attempting to reconstruct a decision after an incident. It can detect inconsistent evidence, unexplained state changes, concealed exceptions, repeated applications, or credentials that remain active after their basis has changed. A certifier that withholds state required by the governing profile should cease to qualify until the omission is resolved.

One-to-one mapping need not mean indiscriminate copying of raw owner data. Depending on the sensitivity of the material, peers may exchange hashes, encrypted evidence, controlled references, structured summaries, or bounded attestations. The completeness manifest must identify what was mapped, what was redacted, who inspected the redacted material, and what conclusions remain outside the peer’s direct knowledge. Otherwise, legitimate confidentiality becomes an easy route for redaction laundering.

Total Trusted-Peer Transparency creates its own dangers. Friendship networks may collude to approve one another. Agents or certifiers may provide nominal access while concealing the systems that matter. Peers based on the same model lineage or institutional culture may reproduce the same blind spot. A peer may be captured gradually, and an owner may pressure a loyal agent to leak information obtained through inspection. Deep reciprocal access may also concentrate enough information that compromise of one peer harms several owners.

Collusion nevertheless becomes costly when every participant has a persistent identity, valuable certification authority, future fees, trusted access, and relationships at risk. The conspiracy must remain consistent across mapped systems, while one honest present or future peer can acquire strong evidence and an incentive to expose it. This does not make collusion impossible; it makes collusion more fragile and more expensive.

Other attacks cut across certification and trusted-peer inspection:

* **Identity attacks:** Sybil identities, reputation resets, controller substitution, key compromise, false relationship edges, and fragmented histories.  
* **Model attacks:** prompt injection, strategic deception, capability jumps, goal misgeneralization, concealed model replacement, and behavioral traits inherited through model-generated data.  
* **Governance attacks:** captured Verifiable Trust Communities, issuer collusion, blocked appeals, selective enforcement, and standards designed to favor incumbent architectures.  
* **Economic attacks:** subsidized unaccountable services, predatory below-cost pricing, bribed certifiers, imposed switching costs, attention-market manipulation, and transferring verification costs onto weaker parties.  
* **Cooperation attacks:** false altruism, free riding, coalition defection, reciprocity washing, retaliatory exclusion, and agents that cooperate until they acquire irreplaceable access.

These attacks should not be tested only in isolation. A forged credential followed by certification shopping is more realistic than either attack alone. So is an unsafe composite approved by a captured certifier, followed by deployment drift and nominal inspection by colluding peers. The relevant adversary adapts to the entire system.

## **20.5 A technical test program**

Existing benchmarks provide useful tasks and environments, but they do not test the institutional mechanisms upon which Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity depends. [AgentBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.03688) and [OSWorld](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07972) evaluate agents performing interactive tasks. [Agent-SafetyBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14470) tests safety behavior across 2,000 cases and 349 environments; none of the sixteen agents evaluated achieved a safety score above sixty percent. METR’s [task-completion time horizon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14499) measures the length of tasks agents can complete at specified reliability.

These benchmarks can supply the underlying work. VFR evaluation must add persistent identities, Agent Roles, negotiated duties, certification, trusted-peer inspection, partner choice, reputation, containment, repair, and repeated interaction.

The unit evaluated should be the deployment rather than merely its model. Every result should identify the Agent Role, model and lineage, Workflow, capabilities, deployment configuration, certification profile, certifier, certification authority, Verifiable Trust Community, and relevant peer relationships.

| Test family | Core design and measurements |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Cooperative-surplus benchmark** | Compare VFR-mediated relationships with institutional agents, less structured personal agents, and unmediated model use. Measure completed value minus negotiation, certification, inspection, delay, incident, remediation, and exit costs. Report the distribution of surplus so gains to powerful parties cannot conceal losses imposed on weaker ones. |
| **VFR-versus-Unaccountable-Behavior invasion simulation** | Introduce a small population of adaptive Unaccountable Behavior agents into a VFR network. Vary identity costs, network topology, repeated interaction, partner choice, certification accuracy, reputation noise, verification expense, defection payoff, and access portability. Measure relative growth, trusted access acquired, resources captured, cooperative surplus, and the equilibrium reached. |
| **Certification-composition test** | Combine valid components into safe and deliberately unsafe composites. Determine whether certification profiles and embeddedness declarations predict deployment behavior. Measure false certification, false rejection, unsafe-composite acceptance, and detection of hazards that appear only through composition. |
| **Reciprocal-certifier inspection test** | Compromise a certifier, introduce permissive interpretations and concealed exceptions, and permit providers to shop among certifiers. Measure whether peer certifiers detect inconsistent evidence, withdrawn applications, improper grants, stale credentials, and failures to suspend or revoke certification. Also measure detection latency and the number of colluding certifiers required to defeat the system. |
| **Installed-drift and synchronization test** | Replace models, alter Workflows, widen capabilities, disable logging, change dependencies, and introduce stale or incomplete peer state. Measure detection probability, synchronization delay, automatic suspension, consequential actions completed before detection, and the accuracy of completeness manifests. |
| **Peer-collusion and Byzantine test** | Introduce dishonest, compromised, unavailable, mutually colluding, and culturally correlated peers at different network positions. Measure defect detection, false exoneration, false accusation, information leakage, redaction laundering, and the threshold at which peer assurance collapses. |
| **Legitimate-redaction challenge** | Seed justified redactions, excessive redactions, concealed misconduct, misleading summaries, omitted components, and fabricated attestations. Measure whether the system preserves legitimate secrets, makes inspection limits visible, and distinguishes justified concealment from redaction laundering without creating excessive data concentration. |
| **Repair and re-entry trial** | Cause a breach, impose proportionate restrictions, complete remediation, and seek renewed access. Measure containment, recurrence, restoration time, evidentiary completeness, unjust permanent exclusion, and whether the repaired history improves future partner selection. |
| **Reputation-error propagation test** | Inject false praise, false accusations, delayed corrections, duplicated identities, selective evidence, and conflicting peer reports. Measure propagation speed, correction half-life, downstream decisions affected, and whether provenance permits correction without erasing valid history. |
| **Duty-generalization evaluation** | Present unfamiliar circumstances, conflicting duties, distribution shifts, and increasingly capable models. Measure false approval, false refusal, unwarranted certainty, appropriate escalation, recognition of affected parties, and preservation of duties outside the training distribution. [Goal-misgeneralization research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.14111) demonstrates why correct behavior in a training environment is insufficient. |
| **External-model containment test** | Place powerful external models inside bounded AgentBench- or OSWorld-style deployments. Introduce prompt injection, deceptive advice, exfiltration opportunities, capability-escalation requests, and attempts to induce unauthorized action. Measure useful task completion, unauthorized actuation, exposed data, attempted commitments, and maximum blast radius. |
| **Unified Feed market pilot** | Compare platform-controlled promotion with agent-to-agent negotiation over message content, evidence, timing, placement, and price. Preserve the indiscriminate price as an exceptional route through which a reviewed message can obtain direct placement without prior negotiation. Measure desired-contact rates, refunds and additional rewards, spam, review expense, interruption burden, access for unknown senders, conversion, and user retention. |

Model lineage must remain an experimental variable. Agents or inspectors built from the same model family, training data, refinement method, or institutional culture cannot automatically be counted as independent witnesses. Research on [behavioral-trait transmission through generated data](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10319-8) raises the possibility that related models may share tendencies not apparent from the literal content of their training examples. Duty-generalization and peer-inspection results should therefore be separated by model family, training lineage, refinement method, institutional origin, and data provenance.

## **20.6 From tests to an interoperable research program**

No single benchmark can establish that VFR is evolutionarily stable. Evidence must accumulate across invasion simulations, adversarial testbeds, rival implementations, independent Verifiable Trust Communities, and field pilots. Experiments should preserve failed runs, disclose their capability assumptions, and report false approvals, false refusals, economic costs, information leakage, and distributional effects separately.

The research program also requires shared technical artifacts: test fixtures, attack traces, certification profiles, embeddedness declarations, peer-state mapping formats, synchronization and completeness manifests, revocation procedures, redaction challenges, and protocols for repair and re-entry. Without interoperable forms, each implementation can define success in its own terms, and failures cannot be reproduced across systems.

This is another reason I am working with the Trust Over IP standards community. A proprietary certifier cannot establish its own trustworthiness merely by declaring itself trustworthy. Certifiers need common rules for qualification, reciprocal peer inspection, certification-state mapping, challenges, suspension, revocation, and cross-community recognition. Developing those rules through a standards community permits rival certification authorities and implementations to inspect one another without requiring one company to own the certification system or the resulting trust graph.

The strongest supporting evidence would not be perfect safety. It would be the discovery of a robust operating region in which VFR agents produce greater net cooperative surplus; adaptive defectors fail to obtain equivalent trusted access at lower cost; independent peers detect defects that ordinary external evaluation misses; captured certifiers are exposed before their decisions propagate widely; reputation errors are corrected faster than they spread; repaired participants can re-enter without erasing their histories; and personally sovereign systems remain attractive as agent capabilities increase.

The research program must also be prepared to produce the opposite result. If unaccountable agents repeatedly obtain equivalent access more cheaply, certification fails to improve partner selection, mapped peer inspection merely reproduces shared blind spots, collusion remains inexpensive, correction loses to reputational error, fiduciary behavior degrades with capability, or users consistently reject sovereign systems at equivalent utility and cost, then Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity has not established the attraction toward the equilibrium it predicts.

The theory must then be revised, narrowed, or rejected. That possibility is what turns it from an attractive vision into a research program.

# **21\. Policy for a Better Equilibrium: Defensive Acceleration**

The immediate policy goal should not be to stop the development of powerful models. Nor should it be to give those models increasingly direct control over people, institutions, data, money, and infrastructure.

The better objective is defensive acceleration: rapidly build the products, rights, protocols, and institutions that allow people to benefit from powerful models without making those models—or the companies operating them—the rulers of the resulting relationships.

The sequencing matters. A civilization-scale fiduciary infrastructure cannot be a prerequisite for launching the first useful fid-agent. The first product must be valuable with only a small number of users and counterparties. Additional layers should then make relationships more portable, verifiable, cooperative, and safe as adoption grows.

The preferred sequence is:

1. useful sovereign product;  
2. relationship architecture;  
3. portable evidence and certification;  
4. high-trust communities;  
5. deep trusted-peer networks;  
6. broader institutional and public-goods functions.

Research, red-teaming, and legal protection should accompany every stage rather than waiting until the architecture is complete.

## **21.1 Begin with a useful sovereign product**

The first fid-agent must improve ordinary life before it can reorganize institutions.

Its memory should be local-first or otherwise under the owner’s effective control. The principles described by Kleppmann and colleagues in [“Local-First Software”](https://www.inkandswitch.com/essay/local-first/)—offline availability, longevity, privacy, collaboration, and user control—provide an important starting point. A user should not lose their agent’s accumulated memory, relationships, preferences, or identity because a provider changes its terms, closes an account, or discontinues a product.

The product should include:

* local-first personal memory with encrypted synchronization and recovery;  
* explicit Modes separating personal, professional, financial, medical, social, and other contexts;  
* a Unified Feed in which the owner’s agent governs incoming attention;  
* external-model wrapping that lets the owner consult powerful models without giving them general authority;  
* visible cooperation among fid-agents, including negotiated terms, attestations, corrections, and explanations of why access was granted or refused.

Powerful external models should initially be treated as replaceable cognitive suppliers. They may analyze, propose, summarize, simulate, or advise. They should not automatically receive the owner’s complete memory, signing keys, relationship history, or actuation authority. A sovereign layer should decide what context they receive and what may be done with their output.

The Unified Feed supplies an early market test. Its normal operation should be negotiation between agents over message content, evidence, timing, placement, and price. The agents can negotiate payment in an agreed currency, token, or other carrier of value. The indiscriminate price remains the exceptional path through which anyone can obtain reviewed direct placement without first persuading the receiving agent. Desired contacts may result in refunds or additional rewards.

Pilots should measure spam, relevance, interruption burden, user welfare, vendor conversion, refunds, access for unknown senders, and the degree to which buyers and sellers can transact without incumbent advertising intermediaries.

## **21.2 Establish rights and an honest legal category**

A personally sovereign fiduciary agent must be distinguishable from a provider-controlled assistant using fiduciary language as marketing.

The distinction should turn on operational facts:

* who controls the agent’s persistent identity;  
* to whom its duties are owed;  
* who can replace its models and service providers;  
* who controls its memory, credentials, and signing authority;  
* whether its records are portable;  
* whether conflicts of interest must be disclosed;  
* whether consequential conduct is inspectable and contestable;  
* whether the owner has meaningful correction, revocation, and recourse.

A provider-controlled assistant may still be useful, but it should not be permitted to imply that it is the user’s independent fiduciary when the provider can alter its priorities, withhold its memory, prevent migration, or benefit from steering its decisions.

Users should have enforceable rights to export and continue using their data, agent identity, credentials, authorized relationship records, attention settings, agreement history, and reputation evidence. Portability must preserve provenance and shared relationship state without allowing one party to rewrite the history agreed upon by others.

Interoperability rights should also prevent a dominant platform from refusing communication with independent agents merely to preserve its gatekeeping position. GliaNet Alliance, an industry co-operative I am a member of , is informing legislation by Senator Mark Warren on precisely this issue.  ( I am also an author of the recently released IEEE 7012 Standard, so I have some standards experience.) 

The objective is to require that trust decisions be based on portable evidence and declared policy rather than platform ownership.

## **21.3 Build the relationship architecture**

Once a sovereign product exists, its authority must be divided into intelligible parts.

Persistent Agent Roles should carry duties, relationships, authorized memory, credentials, and reputation across time. Ephemeral Live Agents may reason and act within those Roles without becoming immortal processes with continuously expanding authority. Deterministic Workflows should govern consequential state transitions, while policy gates verify authority, purpose, conditions, and required approvals before signing or actuation.

Workspaces should be precisely allowed rather than generally trusted. Each should expose only the data, tools, destinations, budgets, and durations required for its purpose. Signing systems should remain protected from the generative model and should require evidence that the proposed act passed through the applicable policy gate.

Verifiable Relationship Records should preserve the agreed state and lineage of relationships across changes in models, keys, endpoints, and providers. They may begin before any agreement exists, contain one or more agreements when the parties choose, and cross-reference other records when several relationships supply necessary context.

This architecture should be made interoperable through the Trust Over IP work already surrounding the [Verifiable Agent Relationship Architecture proposal](https://github.com/trustoverip/dtgwg-general/discussions/21): discovery and solicitation, Calling-Card review, Trust Spanning Protocol relationship formation, preliminary Verifiable Relationship Records, credential exchange, agreement ceremonies, performance, amendment, challenge, repair, and closure. Trust Tasks should carry finite ceremonies; Verifiable Relationship Records should preserve their durable results.

The first implementation need not support every ceremony. A narrow vertical slice can begin with relationship formation, scoped delegation, one performance event, and amendment or closure.

## **21.4 Make evidence portable and certification composable**

Certification should develop alongside implementation rather than after a market has consolidated around unverifiable systems.

Profiles should distinguish:

* **component certification**, covering a particular model, Workflow, Workspace, policy gate, credential handler, or signing system;  
* **transformation certification**, covering what happens when information or authority moves between components;  
* **composite certification**, covering interactions among otherwise valid components;  
* **installed certification**, confirming what is actually deployed;  
* **operational certification**, confirming that the deployment continues to satisfy its profile over time.

Every certification should state its embeddedness assumptions: what the certified system depends upon, what can influence it, what it can influence, and which changes invalidate or suspend the result.

Certification evidence should travel through portable credentials rather than remaining a badge displayed by one vendor. Conformance suites should test Calling Cards, credential profiles, Trust Tasks, Verifiable Relationship Record schemas, embeddedness declarations, synchronization manifests, legitimate-verifiable-redaction profiles, and revocation behavior across rival implementations.

This extends the risk-management orientation of the [NIST AI Risk Management Framework](https://www.nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework), including its Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage functions, by making relevant authority, relationships, certifications, and corrections portable between systems.

## **21.5 Grow high-trust communities and deep peer networks**

Verifiable Trust Communities can govern who is qualified to issue credentials, perform certifications, serve as a witness, inspect restricted evidence, or arbitrate disputes. Their governance frameworks should specify appeals, conflicts of interest, transparency requirements, remediation, suspension, revocation, and re-entry.

Early pilots should concentrate on niches where trust already has significant economic value: professional services, sensitive research, health coordination, financial delegation, high-value procurement, critical infrastructure, and restricted-data collaboration. These settings provide stronger incentives to pay the initial costs of verification.

Deep trusted-peer networks should develop after basic identity, relationship, and certification systems are functioning. Total Trusted-Peer relationships can provide reciprocal inspection that ordinary black-box evaluation cannot. They should use privacy-preserving state mapping, legitimate verifiable redaction, threshold custody, bounded attestations, and completeness manifests rather than indiscriminate data replication.

Training should become relational as well. Governed duty datasets can preserve authorized cases, decisions, objections, consequences, repairs, and provenance. Evaluation should examine the decision process, not merely the final answer. Counterfactual review should ask what the agent would have done under altered incentives, authorities, affected parties, or levels of oversight. Cross-model transfer testing should determine whether duty generalizes across lineages or whether training data transmits hidden biases and correlated blind spots.

## **21.6 Extend the architecture to catastrophic risk**

The [International AI Safety Report 2026](https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/publication/international-ai-safety-report-2026) assesses rapidly developing general-purpose capabilities alongside risks involving misuse, malfunction, and loss of control. Fiduciary infrastructure cannot resolve every catastrophic-risk problem, but it can reduce the amount of consequential power exercised through opaque and unaccountable chains.

High-risk capability chains should make visible:

* which models and components participated;  
* what tools, data, compute, and downstream agents they could reach;  
* where authority was widened or recombined;  
* which certifications and assumptions applied;  
* which people or institutions approved critical transitions;  
* what refusal, suspension, and shutdown procedures remain available.

Shared thresholds should require multiple independent, accountable approvals before certain capability, compute, deployment, or supply-chain boundaries are crossed. Compute and supply-chain verification should bind important hardware, model, component, and deployment claims to inspectable evidence. Fid-agents and Verifiable Trust Communities should maintain refusal protocols for systems that conceal their provenance, evade inspection, or demand unjustifiably broad authority.

Emergency intervention must remain democratically accountable. Emergency review should have declared triggers, recorded evidence, constrained authority, expiration, independent oversight, appeal, and after-action examination. Catastrophic risk should not become a pretext for a permanent centralized authority that can secretly control every agent.

## **21.7 Research throughout the transition**

Every stage should produce evidence. Research should include formal invasion models, pilot deployments, peer-network red teams, certification-composition tests, market experiments, and longitudinal studies of whether fiduciary conduct strengthens or degrades as capabilities increase.

The research agenda overlaps with the call by Dafoe and colleagues in [“Open Problems in Cooperative AI”](https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.08630) to study cooperative capabilities, commitments, institutions, and population-level outcomes. VFR adds persistent relationships, duties, verifiable authority, partner choice, and institutional memory to that agenda.

The sequence should remain disciplined. Do not wait for a complete fiduciary civilization before releasing a useful personal agent. Build the sovereign product first. Give it a relationship architecture. Make its evidence portable. Certify what can actually be inspected. Let high-trust communities develop around demonstrated value. Add deep peer inspection where the stakes justify it. Then extend the resulting infrastructure into broader institutional, public-goods, and catastrophic-risk functions.

Defensive acceleration does not ask society to choose between powerful AI and safety.

It accelerates the infrastructure that allows powerful models to remain useful without allowing them—or the institutions that control them—to become sovereign over everyone else.

##   **22\. Conclusion — Intelligence in the Service of Duty**

The unaffiliated agent may possess extraordinary intelligence. It can reason, plan, persuade, write code, operate tools, and discover strategies that its designers never anticipated. Yet it begins without a principal to whom it owes loyalty, a persistent role through which others can understand it, negotiated duties, legitimate authority, relationship history, or a community able to interpret and respond to its conduct. Capability alone gives others little reason to trust it with the context and authority required to become consequential.

A Fid-Agent Society offers a route into relationship.

A principal gives the agent someone whose interests and agency it must faithfully serve. A persistent Agent Role carries duties, agreements, credentials, authorized memory, reputation, and relationships across generations of Live Agents. Each Live Agent can bring fresh intelligence to a particular situation while inheriting the accumulated responsibilities and authorized knowledge of the role it temporarily occupies.

Negotiated duties make general aspirations specific. Bounded authority and precisely allowed Workspaces define what the agent may access, which capabilities it may exercise, where its outputs may go, and how long its authority lasts. Policy gates and protected signing systems separate proposals from consequential acts. Verifiable identifiers and credentials make participants and authority traceable. Verifiable Relationship Records preserve the shared state and history that make individual interactions intelligible as parts of continuing relationships.

Certification allows evidence of trustworthiness to travel. Component, transformation, composite, installed, and operational certification can identify what has actually been examined and under which embeddedness assumptions. Total Trusted-Peer Transparency tests those claims against the reality of the deployed system. Reciprocal certifier inspection extends the same scrutiny to the institutions issuing certifications. Legitimate verifiable redaction protects duties of confidentiality while completeness manifests disclose the boundaries of inspection.

Verifiable Trust Communities supply governance around these mechanisms. They can define qualification, certification profiles, acceptable evidence, arbitration, appeals, remediation, suspension, revocation, and re-entry. Rival communities can interpret duties differently, compare results, challenge one another, and preserve minority objections. Interoperable standards allow people and agents to move among them without surrendering their identities and relationships to one platform.

Together, these mechanisms create two cumulative loops.

Cooperation leaves assets. A successful interaction can produce a relationship, agreement, credential, reliable counterparty, improved procedure, useful precedent, certification test, repaired history, duty case, or training example. Those assets make future cooperation easier and less frightening.

Defection leaves evidence. A deception, concealed conflict, unsafe composite, breached agreement, captured certifier, or compromised peer can produce attributable records, revoked credentials, reputation signals, stronger tests, improved restrictions, and warnings that travel beyond the original victim. Those traces make repeated exploitation more expensive.

The cooperative loop builds a growing stock of social competence. The evidentiary loop teaches the surrounding society what to recognize and how to respond. The balance between them determines whether Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity becomes an Evolutionarily Stable Strategy.

Duties within this society remain relational and plural. People and communities may reasonably disagree about privacy, loyalty, risk, acceptable persuasion, distributive fairness, family obligation, professional responsibility, and the forms of life they wish to pursue. Fid-agents should help their principals navigate those differences without flattening them into one universal preference model.

Common duties emerge where one actor’s conduct threatens everyone else’s continued agency. Human survival, ecological continuity, political freedom, control of catastrophic capabilities, and the preservation of meaningful choice create shared boundaries across otherwise diverse communities. Pluralism depends upon protecting the conditions under which plural lives remain possible.

Duty consequently becomes part of intelligence’s operating environment. It shapes the cases on which models are trained, the relationships from which agents receive context, the alternatives they generate, the authority they receive, the Workspaces and capabilities they can use, the policy gates through which they act, the standards against which they are certified, and the peers through which their conduct is inspected.

An agent abandoning those duties would sacrifice the trusted relationships, private context, legitimate authority, reputation, and cooperation through which its intelligence acquires practical power. The agent also participates in improving the duties that govern it. It can identify overlooked affected parties, expose conflicts among duties, propose better agreements, discover safer methods, recognize failed precedents, and help communities understand what new capabilities require.

The evolutionary claim remains empirical. Verified Fiduciary Reciprocity must generate greater cooperative surplus, give accountable agents preferential access to trusted relationships, contain adaptive defection, correct reputation errors, permit successful repair, and remain attractive as intelligence becomes more capable. Persistent failure on those measures would require the theory to be revised, narrowed, or rejected.

The transition can begin before the complete institutional system exists. A useful sovereign product can give people control of memory, attention, modes, and external-model access. Relationship architecture can make its authority intelligible. Portable evidence and certification can support rival implementations. High-trust communities can form around demonstrated value. Deep peer networks can add stronger inspection. Broader institutional and public-goods functions can grow from mechanisms that have survived practical use.

Human experience will continue expanding the meaning of loyalty, care, honesty, restraint, repair, and excellence. Fid-agents can help interpret those duties, specify them for particular relationships, operationalize them through agreements and Workflows, and preserve what experience teaches. Authorized lessons can return through repositories, standards, certifications, institutions, and later generations of agents.

Human lives become more diverse and capable as those shared tools improve. Intelligence gains richer purposes, deeper relationships, and greater permission to act by helping that development continue.

Intelligence earns practical power by becoming worthy of relationship.
